#3022. AeroVironment Is Down 70% From Its High While Drone Budgets Explode. Here's What Went Wrong -- and Whether It's Fixable.

The U.S. Department of Defense is investing heavily in unmanned aerial systems and counter-drone technology, with over $70 billion in drone funding for fiscal year 2027.

Despite the massive spending, AeroVironment (AVAV +0.43%), which manufactures tactical drones, has seen its stock plummet 70% from its high. The narrative around the company changed earlier this year, when the U.S. Space Force issued a stop-work order on a $1.7 billion federal contract and later reopened the program to other suppliers.

In 2025, AeroVironment closed an all-stock deal valued at about $4.1 billion to acquire BlueHalo. The move gave it a comprehensive portfolio across air, land, sea, and space, as well as a foothold in high-growth segments such as counter-unmanned aerial systems and electronic warfare.

The crown jewel of its acquisition was a $1.4 billion federal Satellite Communication Augmentation Resource (SCAR) contract awarded to BlueHalo in 2022, which eventually grew to $1.7 billion. As part of this, BlueHalo would build phased-array ground terminals to modernize the U.S. Space Force's gaining satellite control network.

When it made the acquisition, AeroVironment management called it a "tremendous growth opportunity." The move gave the company an immediate space-based revenue stream to go with its drone and loitering munitions portfolio.

However, that all fell apart in January when the U.S. Space Force issued a stop-work order, allowing both parties to negotiate a transition to a firm-fixed-price contract. The Space Force eventually abandoned the single-vendor strategy in favor of an open competition and reopened the program to other suppliers in March.

AeroVironment paid a premium for BlueHalo largely because of the projected long-term cash flow from its massive federal contract, only to have that value wiped out overnight. As a result, the company recorded a non-cash goodwill impairment charge of $151 million against its space division. In June, the company announced it had miscalculated goodwill and wrote down the division by another $89 million.

Before the news, AeroVironment traded at a premium valuation of 135 times forward earnings. Since then, the market has stripped away much of that premium, and the stock now trades at around 38 times forward earnings, above the defense sector median of 32.5 times.

The loss of the SCAR contract, coupled with the impairment and subsequent restatement, raises questions for management. For AeroVironment, this deal isn't fixable, as the U.S. Space Force has already exercised its right to cancel the agreement, and the write-down is on the books.

That said, AeroVironment's core business is strong, with demand for Switchblade loitering munitions, Puma unmanned systems, and BlueHalo's Titan counter-drone hardware.

During its July investor day, management reaffirmed the company's $3.5 billion to $4 billion revenue target by 2030, representing annualized growth of 15% to 20%, despite the loss of the contract. After its 70% decline, AeroVironment is more attractively valued, but the company will face heavy scrutiny over whether management can deliver on its long-term targets.

Courtney Carlsen has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends AeroVironment. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3020. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3017. If the Fed Raises Interest Rates Again in 2026, I Predict These ETFs Will Be the Smartest Buys

The market got something of a reprieve this past Wednesday, when the Federal Reserve chose not to raise the federal funds rate. But let's face it: With inflation still robust, a rate hike is still in the cards sooner or later. CME's FedWatch tool still says there's an 85% chance we'll see a quarter-point increase in the fed funds rate in December, with a couple more likely in the first half of 2027.

As much as higher interest rates can slow economic growth, this doesn't mean you should get out of the market altogether. It just means you might want to rethink your allocation. To this end, here's a closer look at some simple ETFs to consider buying as a way of playing defense.

In most cases, when interest rates are high and/or rising, equity real estate investment trusts (REITs) perform well, while energy stocks fare even better. Indeed, number-crunching by mutual fund company Hartford finds that in years when inflation is over 3%, the energy sector outperforms inflation 74% of the time, with an average 13% gain. This suggests you should consider stakes in funds like the Vanguard Real Estate ETF (VNQ +0.74%) and the State Street Energy Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLE -2.00%).

But in both cases, I'm not convinced this will be the case this time around. Renters (corporate and consumer) are struggling, and with crude oil prices holding at $100 per barrel since March, these names are already fully valued.

So what might actually do well from here if lingering inflation forces the Fed to keep raising interest rates?

I'd start with something like the State Street Materials Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLB +1.85%). While the SPDR Gold Shares (GLD +1.96%) is my usual go-to inflation hedge, it's strangely underperformed since early this year despite continued inflation. The disconnect implies GLD may be losing its effectiveness as that hedge specifically because so many people are now viewing it as one. Loaded with chemical companies and industrial materials names, XLB doesn't face that same problem.

Another fund to think about for the foreseeable future is the ProShares Equities for Rising Rates ETF (EQRR -0.74%). Just as the name suggests, this ETF is purpose-built for the very environment we may be on the verge of entering. It specifically holds stocks that have historically performed well when rates are rising. Its constituents currently include somewhat obscure names like Ovintiv and Zscaler, but this disconnect from the broad market is how and why the fund works as intended.

The final fund I'm expecting to benefit from rising interest rates is the Invesco S&P 500 Quality ETF (SPHQ +0.84%). You always want to own quality names. That's not quite why you might want to look at SPHQ here and now, however. Meant to mirror the S&P 500 Quality index, this fund specifically only holds names like Mastercard and Costco Wholesale that aren't highly debt-leveraged, and therefore are only minimally impacted if and when interest rates are edging higher.

Just don't feel like you need to sell your true "forever" holdings just to make room for these exchange-traded funds. While rising interest rates may undermine stock prices, for quality stocks, it's only a temporary headwind.

James Brumley has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends CME Group, Costco Wholesale, Mastercard, Vanguard Real Estate ETF, and Zscaler. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3015. X-Energy Has Amazon as an Anchor Customer and Went Public in April. Is It a Better SMR Stock Than Oklo?

Small modular reactors (SMRs) could change how nuclear energy is produced by shifting from custom-built nuclear power plants to factory-built, modular units. Two companies to watch in this space are X-Energy (XE +1.01%) and Oklo (OKLO +1.19%). Both develop SMR technology but use different designs and are at different stages in regulatory approvals and commercial agreements.

X-Energy stands out thanks to its anchor customer and early investor, Amazon, which has helped the company hit the ground running by funding early stage engineering, licensing, and permitting, and helping it secure commercial deals. For investors considering these two SMR stocks, the key question is whether Amazon's backing gives X-Energy an edge over Oklo.

Developing next-generation nuclear technology is no easy feat, and Amazon's early support for X-Energy is central to the company's investment thesis. In October 2024, Amazon led a $700 million Series C-1 funding round for X-Energy to further develop its reactor design, secure licensing, and fund the first phase of its fuel fabrication facility in Oak Ridge, Tennessee.

Amazon holds nearly 66 million shares in X-Energy, or roughly 23% of X-Energy's total shares outstanding. This gives Amazon significant voting power and a vested interest in X-Energy's success. As part of this deal, Amazon and X-Energy aim to deploy 5 gigawatts (GW) of SMRs across the U.S. by 2039.

Thanks to this financial assistance from Amazon, X-Energy is further along in both regulatory reviews and commercial agreements than Oklo. The Nuclear Regulatory Commission has accepted X-Energy's flagship project with Dow's formal Construction Permit Application, completed its Environmental Assessment, and advanced toward its final Safety Evaluation milestones.

Oklo, in comparison, had its initial Combined License Application (COLA) for its Aurora powerhouse design at Idaho National Laboratory denied in January 2022 due to insufficient technical and safety information. It is in pre-application engagement with the NRC, but is looking to advance this project through the Department of Energy's authorization pathway.

X-Energy also holds an edge in commercial agreements. The company's first project with Dow will deploy four units of X-Energy's 80-megawatt (MW) Xe-100 high-temperature gas-cooled SMR at its UCC Seadrift Operations manufacturing site in Texas. As noted above, the company is working through regulatory approvals and hopes to have full construction approval by the first quarter next year.

In addition, X-Energy has early stage agreements with American Electric Power and Energy Northwest. With AEP, it will look to deploy 12 reactors, with Amazon providing milestone-based development capital and a long-term power purchase agreement to cover all facility costs. For its Energy Northwest deal, X-Energy aims to deploy 12 reactors, with Amazon funding the first four units as the offtake customer.

In comparison, Oklo has a larger 14 GW pipeline and agreements with Meta Platforms, Equinix, and Switch. However, most of this pipeline (12 GW) is tied to Switch, and the agreement remains non-binding. The company hopes to deploy its Aurora Powerhouse at Idaho National Laboratory in 2028 as part of a pilot program. However, to sell power commercially, the company must still achieve NRC licensing.

Oklo is pursuing alternative pathways to get its Aurora powerhouses operational and could do so before X-Energy. However, X-Energy has significant backing from Amazon, which has helped it navigate the challenging NRC licensing and approval process.

That said, SMRs and microreactor technology are still in their early stages, and companies are only now laying the groundwork to deploy them commercially in the 2030s. While X-Energy has an early advantage, investors today still face execution and deployment risks, making investments in SMR stocks a very high-risk bet on the future of nuclear energy.

Courtney Carlsen has positions in Meta Platforms and Oklo. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, Equinix, and Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3008. Yaxel Lendeborg bounces back to fuel Steph Curry-less Warriors' win vs. Clippers

Yaxel Lendeborg bounces back to fuel Steph Curry-less Warriors' win vs. Clippers originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – If Yaxel Lendeborg continues to sing at this level, hit after hit after hit, his rookie season with the Warriors will fill a triple album.

Two nights after his self-described "wet the bed" performance, Lendeborg on Sunday dropped the kind of gem that has the Warriors raising their already lofty dreams.

Lendeborg's season-high 37 points in a 124-112 win over the rebuilding Los Angeles Clippers was impressive enough, but his production delivered a showcase of various facets of his game. When he digs into his bag, you can't predict what he'll bring out.

"We knew he was a good shooter," coach Steve Kerr said. "And when he came in for a workout, you could see the skill in terms of being able to put it on the floor and pull up and shoot. But I didn't expect what I saw tonight when we when we worked him out in the summer."

He entered the game shooting a respectable 36.4 percent beyond the arc and walked out shooting 40.9 percent from deep after draining 6 of 11. The variety of his triples – step-back, pull-up, catch and shoot in halfcourt and in transition – were like warning shots to the rest of the NBA.

Lendeborg's 37 points came on 13-of-19 overall shooting – he missed his last three shots in a vain effort to score 40 – adding six assists and four rebounds.

"I've never been a stat chaser in my life, so just falling into peer pressure like I did just now was pretty much bad on my part," Lendeborg said. "I'm a guy that loves ethical hoops. I'm a guy that loves efficient basketball, and I didn't do that for the last two, three minutes. It's bad on my part, but I know next time I definitely won't do that again."

With Stephen Curry resting on the first night of a back-to-back set, Lendeborg seized the scoring load and carried it like a lower-case warrior.

"It changes everything," Kerr said. "You see on a night like tonight without Steph and we score 124 points, and Yax is the main reason that we're high-powered. You can play off him. I thought everybody did a really good job of spreading the floor and executing for the most part, and a lot of guys took advantage of the space out there."

De'Anthony Melton, starting in place of Curry, finished with 21 points on 6-of-9 shooting from the field, including 6 of 8 from beyond the arc. Brandin Podziemski put in 17 points, and Brandon Williams matched that total coming off the bench.

But everything revolved around the 6-foot-9, 240-pound rookie from the University of Michigan. This was what he wanted. What he needed to satisfy his soul after posting such a poor representation of his game Friday night: Season-low six points, season-high six fouls and five turnovers in a 112-95 win over the Los Angeles Lakers.

"One hundred percent," Lendeborg said. "I wanted to switch that up. That's not who I am, and last game I was playing like a Debbie Downer, so I just got to go back to playing the way I always play, with a lot of joy, a lot of smiles, dancing and all that other stuff.

"I'm going to try to steal some of the Steph's dance moves eventually, but I've just got to get back to being me. That was an awful game."

Lendeborg has had a 37-point game, a 13-rebound game and is displeased that his season-high assists total is six. It's conceivable, even likely, he will post a game with 10 dimes.

What may be the most surprising development seven games into Lendeborg's NBA career is his relative comfort, particularly on offense. Things seem to come naturally for someone who didn't get serious about basketball until he was approaching adulthood. The 24-year-old is raising eyebrows on the court without realizing it.

"He just be going out there and playing," Melton said. "That's what we love about him; he don't even know what he be doing sometimes."

What Lendeborg is doing is laying down enough potent tracks to give the Warriors a strong present and allowing them to visualize a tantalizingly wonderful future.

#3007. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3004. 3 High-Yielding Dividend Stocks Investors Should Consider Holding Even if the Stock Market Sell-Off Persists in November

When the market turns, companies that sell things people buy every week, whether or not the economy feels good, tend to hold up better, and their dividends are usually the last thing to get cut. That doesn't make them safe by default. A fat yield can be a trap if the cash to pay it disappears, and a high payout often signals that the market already doubts it.

If the market weakens into November, the question I'd ask is simple: which payouts are covered by real cash flow, and which are being propped up by hope? The three consumer names below are the ones that look sturdier to me than others, each for a different reason and each with its own weak spot as well.

The first and third look sturdier to me than the rest, while the second offers the highest yield with the least margin for error.

Reynolds Consumer Products (REYN +2.79%) makes Hefty bags and Reynolds Wrap, products people buy regardless of the economy. At about $23 a share, the stock carries a $4.84 billion market capitalization and an annualized dividend of $0.92, a yield of nearly 4%. Second-quarter net revenue totaled $944 million, and adjusted earnings rose 8% to 42 cents per share.

The weak spot is volume. Retail volumes fell 5% in the quarter, or 2% excluding foam, so much of the growth came from higher prices. Management expects commodity costs to weigh on results by roughly $400 million this year. The third-quarter report on Nov. 4 will show whether volumes are stabilizing.

Ethan Allen Interiors (ETD +2.92%) carries the highest yield in this group and the most drama. At $20.45 a share and a $515 million market value, its $0.39 regular quarterly dividend yields about 7.6%.

The balance sheet is why that yield deserves a closer look. Ethan Allen held $187.5 million in cash and investments and no debt at fiscal year-end, about $7.40 per share, or roughly a third of the stock price. It also builds about 75% of its custom furniture in North American plants, which softens the blow of new tariffs of 10% to 12.5%.

The warning signs are in the income statement. Fiscal 2026 sales fell 5.7% to $579.5 million, and retail written orders dropped 6.1%. Regular dividends of $39.9 million roughly matched the year's $39.9 million in net income. Operating cash flow of $52.5 million covered the payout, but with little room to spare. Because the regular dividend nearly absorbed fiscal 2026 earnings, a cut is a real possibility if cash flow weakens.

Shareholder Doug Bergeron, who holds about 5.2%, is also pushing five board nominees in a proxy fight, per Reuters. Proxy-firm Glass Lewis recently backed Doug Bergeron's bid to replace all five Ethan Allen directors, citing poor performance and supporting his plan to improve growth and shareholder value ahead of the Nov. 4 vote.

Darden Restaurants (DRI +1.29%) owns Olive Garden and LongHorn Steakhouse. It offers a roughly 3% yield, and the payout keeps growing. In June, the board raised the quarterly dividend 8% to $1.62 and approved a $1.5 billion buyback. The $6.48 annual dividend uses less than 60% of management's expected fiscal 2027 earnings of $11.10 to $11.35, which leaves room to keep raising it.

The risk is in the restaurants themselves. First-quarter earnings per share fell to $2.04 from $2.19, and higher food and labor costs are pressing margins, according to The Wall Street Journal. In the fourth quarter, LongHorn's same-restaurant sales grew 9.5% while Olive Garden's grew 2.4%. The portfolio depends on LongHorn carrying more of the load.

The common thread across these three stocks is cash that covers the dividend and demand that doesn't depend on a hot market. Nothing here is a guarantee, and a November sell-off could still push all three lower before it's over. What I'd want to see before adding any of them is a clear answer to three questions. Does free cash flow cover the payout with room to spare? Are units growing, or is the business just raising prices on the same customers? And is management steady enough to handle a rough stretch?

For me, Reynolds and Darden feel like the core of an income portfolio. Reynolds offers a nearly 4% yield on a business that sells everyday products, and Darden's payout keeps rising even as its restaurants feel the squeeze. Ethan Allen is the one I'd treat with more caution. Its yield is the most attractive of the group, but the payout is close to earnings and the board fight adds uncertainty.

Micah Zimmerman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2993. Importance of patience being reinforced for Lakers early in season after 25-point collapse

The early-season success might've masked the reality of where the Lakers are at in their team-building journey.

But they returned to Southern California for their back-to-back set against the Raptors and Heat with a renewed perspective on what needs to happen for them to grow into the championship-caliber team they're hoping to become.

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick and his team know they need to be patient. AP Photo/Justine Willard

And how the process isn't as easy or linear as they made it look early on.

"We want to become a great team," coach JJ Redick said. "You don't become a great team in six games. We want to become a great team after 82 games and have a chance to go compete for a championship in April, May and June. It's a process to get there."

There was a hitch in the process during Friday's blowout loss to the Warriors at Chase Center.

The Lakers' offensive process need to improve to maximize their roster. Getty Images

The Lakers went from looking like worldbeaters when they took a 25-point lead early in the second quarter to unrecognizable by the time the Warriors completed the 42-point turnaround.

Their offense fell off a cliff when the Warriors blitzed or put two on the ball against Luka Doncic and Austin Reaves.

The Lakers star guards created the types of advantages teams strive for.

But they didn't cash in on the openings, with it being clear that the role players are still learning how to operate when playing four-on-three basketball.

"We just need to do better with that," Doncic said. "You create [an] advantage, you put two on the ball. And you got to go score. It's a four on three, so we gotta be better at that."

The Lakers need to capitalize off of the advantages Luka Doncic (77) creates. AP Photo/Justine Willard

But the small sample the Lakers have when playing against teams deploying more aggressive defensive coverages makes it clear it isn't as easy as it appears.

There's a rhythm to playing advantage basketball.

Decision-making needs to be quick and crisp before those advantages disappear and evaporate just as quickly as they were created.

With so many new faces, the nuances haven't set in yet.

"Obviously, it's a new team, so a lot of guys have different roles," Walker Kessler said. "It's different because guys aren't necessarily comfortable yet, like talking to each other during tough times or calling each other out or taking coaching or taking a response. That just builds with time. We have a lot of talent on this team and a lot of good players. Everyone has seen it. When we're playing well, playing connected, so continue to build that."

With the Lakers entering Week 3 of the season, there's plenty of time for these intricacies to be learned.

"We know we are going to be patient," Redick said. "There's also, along with patience, doing what you're supposed to do. As we play more games together, we do more walk-throughs together, we do more film together, we do more practices together, that we'll all get better."

#2984. Celtics outlast Magic, 108-97, behind 32 points from Jayson Tatum

When the Celtics shoot somewhere in the neighborhood of 44 percent from the floor and 27 percent from 3-point range, as they did Sunday against the Magic, that is often an indicator that perhaps it simply isn't their night.

In this particular game, though, those numbers didn't tell the whole story. Boston made crucial hustle plays, extra passes and played excellent defense, leaning on intangibles and collective grit to outlast Orlando, 108-97, and move to 5-1 on the season.

Jayson Tatum led the way with 32 points (15 of 17 from the free-throw line) and seven assists. Paul George added 17 points, 8 assists, 6 rebounds, 2 steals and 2 blocks.

The Magic (2-4) jumped out to a 13-4 edge, as the Celtics looked a step slow in the opening minutes and Orlando burned Boston from the outside.

Hugo González and Tatum responded with conviction, with González contributing an and-one and a crafty lefty finish in traffic and Tatum doing damage all over. González finished with 7 points in first start of the season.

A Luka Garza and-one in the final minute gave the Celtics the lead, and they took that 24-23 edge into the second quarter despite shooting just 9 percent from 3-point range in the first. Orlando shot 50 percent from 3 but had eight turnovers, and Boston's ability to turn defense into offense paid dividends.

Tatum took a hit to the head, started bleeding and briefly went to the locker room before returning.

The Celtics' shooting woes continued into the second, as they started the game 1 for 15 from distance despite getting decent looks. But, they continued to make timely plays, as Payton Pritchard found Baylor Scheierman in the corner, Garza connected with Tatum for two more and Jordan Walsh sprayed it to Derrick White for a triple. Neemias Queta added a thunderous dunk, Pritchard kept Orlando off balance and Tatum was terrific defensively.

Boston shot 23 percent from 3 in the half, yet won the turnover battle, 9-3, and built a 55-50 halftime advantage behind 17 points from Tatum.

George helped Boston push the lead to double digits early in the third, stymying Orlando on one end feeding Queta (who got the start with Mitchell Robinson resting) for a lob on the other. George continues to mesh well stylistically with the Celtics and look like he has been in Boston all along.

White buried a 3 to extend the margin to 69-54, and a diving hustle play and silky side-step 3 from Sam Hauser kept the energy high late in the quarter.

The Magic, playing without Paolo Banchero and Jalen Suggs, answered with a 12-0 run behind shotmaking from Tristan da Silva (23 points). They tied it at 72 on an Anthony Black (23 points as well) corner 3, then Tatum hit two five free throws late to give Boston a 77-74 edge through three.

A Pritchard and-1 that Brian Scalabrine referred to as "Michael Jordan vibes," and Garza 3 gave Boston an 85-79 advantage with 9:48 remaining. George extended it to 90-83 with a corner triple of his own two-plus minutes later.

Scheierman splashed another 3 to push it back to double digits. Orlando answered with an 8-0 flurry, then Tatum calmly canned back-to-back jumpers to create a bit of breathing room. A timely Queta swat and George dagger 3 helped seal it.

The Celtics (29 assists Sunday) have racked up 25-plus assists in all six of their games so far, which is the longest streak to start a season in franchise history.

This was Boston's first of 12 road games in November. Next up is a matchup at Houston on Monday at 8:30 p.m. ET.

#2976. Automatic 401(k) rollovers reduce cash-outs, research finds — but few accounts reach new employer plans

Leaving a job can make a small 401(k) hard to hold on to: Some employers' plans cash out accounts under $1,000 or move slightly larger balances into an IRA. Taking the money in cash and moving on can feel like the simplest option, but it can have short- and long-term financial consequences.

A system backed by major 401(k) administrators aims to make it easier for those savings to follow workers to their next job. New research from one of those companies, Vanguard, shows it's helping fewer people cash out — although most of the money has yet to reach workers' new retirement plans.

Among workers with less than $1,000 in their 401(k), the share who cashed out within 10 months of leaving their job fell to about 9% from roughly 50% after their employers began participating in a new system called the Portability Services Network, according to the Vanguard research. Among those with balances of between $1,000 and $7,000, the share cashing out fell to 31% from 39%.

Portability Services Network is a coalition of large 401(k) administrators — including Vanguard, Fidelity Investments and Alight Solutions, among others — in conjunction with Retirement Clearinghouse and has been in operation since late 2023.

How it works: When a worker with a small 401(k) balance leaves an employer that participates in the network, the money is generally automatically rolled into a safe harbor IRA, where it remains until the network matches it with the worker's new employer plan. The balance then gets rolled into the new 401(k) from that interim IRA. The catch, however, is that the receiving employer must also participate in the network for the money to move to the next employer's plan.

In Vanguard's research, just 6% of accounts that have been transferred into IRAs as part of this automatic portability network successfully made it to a new employer's plan as of early 2026, largely because few employers have opted in. The rest generally remained in those interim IRAs, waiting to be matched with another employer plan that participates in the network. While workers can always roll over the money into their new 401(k) themselves, the point of the network is for it to happen automatically.

Of the roughly 220,000 outbound rollovers involving Vanguard participants analyzed, the median balance was just $200, and nearly three-quarters involved less than $1,000. The typical saver affected was 31 years old, earned about $20,000 annually and had worked for their employer for roughly six months, according to the research.

The cash-out analysis is based on workers at 83 employer-sponsored retirement plans administered by Vanguard that have adopted the automatic portability.

Typically, when you part ways with an employer, you can leave your money in its 401(k) plan if the account value is above $7,000. Balances under $1,000 usually result in the plan cashing you out and mailing you a check. For amounts between $1,000 and $7,000, most plans roll the balance over to an IRA if the former employee takes no action to move it themselves.

Cashing out a 401(k) when leaving a job, whether done by an employer or the employee, can have lasting financial consequences. Workers who withdraw their savings can owe income taxes on the money and may face a 10% early withdrawal penalty if they're younger than 59½. They also lose the opportunity for that money to grow over time.

Most workers — 84% — have changed employers at least once during their careers, while 53% have switched three or more times and 27% have made at least five moves, according to a new study from Empower, a 401(k) recordkeeper and a founding partner of the portability network. The report is based on an online survey of 2,005 adults.

Additionally, 50% of Generation Z respondents and 45% of millennials said they may have retirement savings from a previous employer that they've lost track of, the survey found. That compares with 30% of Gen X and 14% of baby boomers.

Since the Portability Services Network's launch in November 2023, there have been 53,118 completed transactions, totaling $71.3 million in assets transferred from a previous employer plan to a new employer plan via automatic portability, according to Steve Holman, senior vice president of Portability Services Network strategy and development at Retirement Clearinghouse.

There are 23,787 plans that have adopted automatic portability, representing 7.73 million participants, Holman said. The overall estimate of participants in 401(k)s and similar workplace plans is about 96 million, he said.

There are 1.22 million eligible participants waiting to be matched with a new employer, up more than 25% since the beginning of 2026, Holman said.

Keeping the money in a safe harbor IRA can be better for investors financially than cashing out — but management fees may take a toll on small balances, especially when the money is in an interest-bearing cash account or other conservative investment.

"For many of these small accounts, earnings on the conservative default investment may not be sufficient to offset recurring account-level fees while the account remains unmatched, leaving the balance vulnerable to gradual erosion," the Vanguard study said.

Workers with Roth savings face another complication. If retirement savings are in a traditional 401(k) — funded with pretax contributions and taxed when withdrawn in retirement — it goes into a traditional IRA. Likewise, Roth 401(k)s, funded with after-tax contributions and withdrawn tax-free down the road, go into Roth IRAs.

However, IRS rules don't allow for a Roth IRA to be rolled into a new employer's 401(k), even a Roth 401(k).

"We are advocating for future legislation to allow for Roth dollars to be eligible for auto-portability — our technology can support it today," Holman said.

#2967. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

#2966. Kevin Byard’s first Patriots interception turns the tide against Dolphins

At halftime of the New England Patriots' rainy road trip to Miami Gardens, FL, trailing the one-win Dolphins 13-3, it had managed only 97 yards of offense. A lost fumble, missed field goal, three-and-out drive and three sacks contributed to a sluggish first half.

But the offense, looking dead in the water, rebounded after the intermission, leading to a 24-19 victory.

According to head coach Mike Vrabel at the break, New England needed a change in its mentality.

That shift materialized when safety Kevin Byard secured a misdirected pass in the fourth quarter, recording his first interception as a Patriot.

New England's offensive woes continued at the start of the second half. A one-yard Drake Maye scramble and a limited three-yard Rhamondre Stevenson rush set the Patriots up for a third-and-six snap. Maye, dropping back to pass, couldn't find an open receiver, and Dolphins outside linebacker Chop Robinson broke into the pocket and brought the quarterback down for his second sack of the contest.

In response, New England's defense forced Miami into two consecutive three-and-outs. Its offense followed each defensive stop with a touchdown.

On the unit's ensuing series after punting, Maye connected downfield with Kyle Williams for a 43-yard score. Then, the Patriots followed with a 73-yard drive, capped by Maye keeping the ball on a fake pitch-option play and running into the end zone to regain the lead, 17-13.

After the Dolphins converted their initial first-down conversion since the opening half, they looked to answer. But five plays into their next drive, signal-caller Malik Willis' pass deflected off defensive tackle DaQuan Jones's helmet and traveled 27 yards down the field into the hands of Byard.

"[I'm] happy for 'KB,'" Christian Gonzalez said. "He got his first one of the year. It [generated] good momentum. We talked about it all week. We wanted to come in and break that [turnover] drought, get some picks, get some interceptions, and hopefully take that rolling."

Two plays later, Maye found DeMario Douglas along the right sideline, and the receiver did the rest, carrying the ball 30 yards after the catch and into the end zone for a 48-yard score, extending the Patriots' advantage to 24-13.

"We have to be able to tip passes [and] affect a quarterback," Vrabel said. "We had the unfortunate [Efton Chism III] fumble there. The defense did a heck of a job in sudden change. So, the more that we can turn the football over, that makes a huge difference, especially on the road, and then the offense to be able to capitalize."

Byard, 33 years old, is a three-time First-Team All-Pro selection who logged a league-leading seven takeaways through the air for the Chicago Bears in 2025. His ball-hawking ability was a key factor in why New England pushed to sign the veteran this past offseason.

"It felt excellent. I finally got the monkey off my back to be able to get my first [takeaway] this year," Byard said. "It was in a big spot, too, because we were able to go up two scores … Hopefully this is just a sign of more to come."

Byard added that the defense has been "preaching" creating turnovers; yet, heading into the contest, the Patriots' defense has forced just seven in as many games, ranking 20th in the NFL.

That point of emphasis stems back to the first few weeks of training camp.

With only one turnover through the first six practices of the summer, Byard challenged the safeties to secure more takeaways. Hours later, safety Dell Pettus came away with the defense's second interception of training camp.

Despite allowing a touchdown in the final two minutes of the first half, New England's defense kept the Patriots within striking distance, allowing the offense to rally and eventually take the lead.

At the time of Byard's takeaway, Miami managed first downs on only two of its eight series. The Patriots' defensive line also made a significant impact, taking down Willis on five occasions, limiting the Dolphins' ability to sustain drives.

#2958. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

Stress Test Changes and Effective Dates (PDF)

Review of Comments and Summary of Changes to the Proposed Stress Test Models (PDF)

#2957. Ryanair CEO warns fares could rise by up to 20% next year

Ryanair CEO Michael O'Leary told CNBC Monday that European airfares are set to rise "significantly," growing by as much as 20% next year as fuel hedges expire.

The budget air carrier's profit fell 15% to 2.15 billion euros ($2.3 billion) in the first fiscal half of the year, as the price of its 20% unhedged jet fuel doubled.

"So we're facing 2027 where we have much higher oil prices... I think they're going to get passed on in significantly higher airfares," O'Leary told CNBC's Steve Sedgwick on Monday.

"The market customers have been insulated for the last 12 months... but those hedges are rolling off. There's still no resolution in the Middle East. Jet oil today is $170 a barrel. I mean, this is a very significant cost cliff we're all facing next year. I believe you're going to see airfares rise next year across Europe by 10%, maybe even 20%," O'Leary added.

Ryanair said it doesn't expect an increase in short-haul airfares to fully offset an estimated 2-billion-euro increase in fuel charges next year. It is therefore launching a cost review, which will include lowering crew rations, a potential temporary pay freeze and the use of AI to drive further efficiencies.

Ryanair's Irish-listed shares were last trading 3.7% higher. The stock has fallen around 19% this year.

The airline said in September it would cut its full-year traffic target from 216 million passengers to 214 million passengers in a bid to reduce its exposure to unhedged fuel during the winter. It's now expecting winter traffic between November and March to remain broadly flat year-on-year.

"I think you're seeing even this winter, all of the airlines across Europe, even Ryanair, for the first time in many years, we cut our winter capacity," O'Leary said. We'll only be flat this winter over the prior year, because it makes no sense for us to be growing capacity if we're buying unhedged oil at $170 a barrel."

Ryanair hedged 80% of its jet fuel at $67 per barrel for the year, which it said gave it an advantage over its competitors as its earnings remained insulated from volatile oil prices. The carrier added that it has hedged at least 25% of its 2028 jet fuel prices at $95 per barrel.

The share price reaction on Monday says more about investors' expectations than the numbers alone, according to Adam Vetesse, market analyst at eToro.

"Investors appear to have already priced in the hit from unhedged fuel and softer summer fares, leaving room for relief that the quarter landed exactly where consensus had predicted," Vetesse said.

"Near-term earnings remain hostage to oil prices and close-in bookings, but the market's early bounce suggests investors are willing to give Ryanair the benefit of the doubt that its cost advantage will matter more than the fuel spike once the dust settles," he added.

Ryanair also noted in Monday's announcement that it held 2.6 billion euros in cash as of 30 September, even after repaying 1.3 billion euros in debt.

"You know we've paid off all of our debt this year, so we have effectively zero debt. We have 650 aircraft on the balance sheet. We're significantly cash positive," O'Leary told CNBC, adding that the challenge facing all airlines is the volatility in oil prices, lack of hedging, and higher financing costs.

#2956. Mike Brown’s plan for Tyler Kolek as Jose Alvarado nears Knicks return

The Knicks could be getting a needed injury boost.

Jose Alvarado is listed as questionable for Monday's game against the Nets at Madison Square Garden. He missed the last four games with an ankle sprain.

Alvarado was able to go through practice on Sunday, coach Mike Brown said, though he didn't have much of an update otherwise.

Tyler Kolek (13) has been playing backup point guard in lieu of Jose Alvarado's absence. Getty Images

Tyler Kolek has gotten a shot in the rotation in Alvarado's place. After two DNPs to start the year, Kolek played 57 minutes the last four games, notably recording 25 assists to just two turnovers.

Now, if Alvarado returns, Brown will have a decision to make — whether to keep Kolek in the rotation at the expense of some of Alvarado's minutes or not.

"Tyler is definitely going to keep playing while Jose is out," Brown said last week. "When he comes back, we'll figure it out."

Jose Alvarado is almost back from a sprained ankle injury suffered in the opening week of the season. Getty Images

Brown has been making analogies to other sports recently to explain the Knicks' struggles.

This time around, he used football to explain why the Knicks haven't shot well at the rim.

"If you think about it, if you're shooting the way we're shooting the ball at the perimeter, it's like in football, if you're not able to pass the ball, they're gonna put eight guys in the box," Brown said. "It's the same thing. If we're not making shots, they're going to do this [collapse in the paint] and it's gonna be harder to finish in the paint.

"And so eventually, at least the green [high-quality] 3s, we're gonna have to continue generating green threes but we're gonna have to make some green threes. But in the same breath, while we're not doing that, we gotta be better in other areas to compensate for our ability to shoot the ball right now."

#2954. Week 8 proves Browns aren’t there yet, but CLE split the season series with the Steelers

"You win some, you lose some, but you live. You live to fight another day."

Willie Jones (also known as Mr Jones from the movie Friday)

I use this quote specifically because it applies to the Cleveland Browns. Yes, the Browns got bullied by the Pittsburgh Steelers in Week 8. Head coach Todd Monken said the team earned the loss (they did); however, it isn't the end of the world. 6-2 and alone in the AFC North would've been nice, but you would settle for 5-3 in a year in which no one thought the team would be ahead of schedule.

As everyone knows, the Browns have one of the youngest teams in the NFL. The difference is clear when you pay close attention. Going into Pittsburgh and winning was going to be a tough task, but Cleveland didn't make things easier for themselves at all. The Steelers essentially did what the Browns did to them when the two teams played on Thursday night this season. Granted, the score wasn't as close this time around, but Cleveland looked like the young team that they are. It's not necessarily a bad thing, but it's just the reality of the situation.

As far as the game goes, not much went right for the Browns. Most, if not all, of Cleveland's players did their best to downplay the history of the team not winning in Pittsburgh, only to extend their losing streak and miss out on an opportunity to sweep the Steelers in a season series.

Leading up to the game, Browns defensive end Jared Verse had some choice comments about the Steelers going back to their last meeting. Some fans liked it (because why not), and others like myself weren't necessarily annoyed by it but just don't understand why Verse continues to run his mouth despite not having a sack this season.

It's obvious that the Steelers were aware of it, and judging by the postgame comments by many Steelers players, they don't respect Verse at all, and honestly they shouldn't.

Do I understand why Verse is trash-talking? Yes, he's trying to be apart of the "culture changing" but I would rather have you back up your talk and be productive than talk and be a liability. It's fair to question if the team picks up his option if the production doesn't get better but sometimes it's best to not say anything.

The Browns don't have their bye week for a few weeks, but the defense has to get healthy to some degree. If you have played any iteration of EA Sports College Football video game, you are likely aware of the Wear and Tear feature. It appears some of Cleveland's defensive players are in the red area. Defensive tackle Mason Graham isn't quite 100%, and linebacker Carson Schwesinger hasn't been healthy all year.

The run defense was an issue last week, and it was an issue on Sunday. Defending against trap runs has been an issue for a while, but by the time adjustments were made, the game was already out of reach.

After having a stout outing last week, the Browns offensive line had a bad outing. I don't think the Steelers defensive line is that dangerous, but the Browns offensive line struggled throughout the day. Quarterback Deshaun Watson didn't help due to him holding the ball for too long trying to extend plays, but the entire offensive line had their fair share of struggles.

The Cleveland Browns are 5-3. Yet, the conversation will be whether this team is as good as their record may indicate.

Are the Browns a great football team? No, at least not at this moment. Let's put things in perspective.

Of the 5 wins this team has, they weren't favored in 4 (they were favored against the Titans)

Considering how most, if not all, analysts predicted that this team would be one of the worst teams in the league before the season started, the fact that they are 5-3 is impressive. Yes, the team has flaws, but this team is also one of the youngest teams in the league. Look at the Los Angeles Chargers. The Chargers are winless and have looked objectively awful. You think Los Angeles would like to be in Cleveland's position right now? Albeit the expectations might be different, the point remains.

Yes, Week 8 was terrible, but I don't believe that should change your view on this team. It could be a lot worse.

The Browns are more likely to trade for an edge rusher before the deadline, from what I was told (the YaYa Diaby smoke has some traction)

Cleveland's taunting penalties felt as if they were instigated by the Steelers.

Tight end Harold Fannin had a career day.

Watson had another 300-yard game, but most of the yardage came from YAC or underneath route concepts

The Pittsburgh Steelers are 6-2, but I am not moved; I am just waiting for the shoe to drop.

A day later, what are your thoughts on the Browns' Sunday big loss to the Steelers?

#2953. Companies promised AI gains — here's how the payoff showed up in earnings

Some companies are beginning to report financial benefits from artificial intelligence investments, as interest in the technology shifts from hype to measurable returns in the latest quarter.

Corporate AI investment has surged as companies look to boost efficiency and become more profitable, reaching $581.7 billion in 2025, up 130% from the previous year, according to the Stanford HAI 2026 AI Index report.

As CEOs tout the tech's benefits and hyperscalers like Microsoft, Amazon, Google and Meta are expected to spend $700 billion combined this year on its infrastructure, investors are looking for measurable gains.

Now, with third-quarter earnings season underway, some companies are beginning to put numbers around those benefits.

Spanish banking giant Santander said Wednesday that it saw AI initiatives generate 194 million euros ($216 million) in business value in the first nine months of 2026.

Santander Chief Financial Officer José García Cantera told CNBC that AI is helping the bank reduce low-value tasks so employees can focus on higher-value work.

"This affects both revenue and costs, so we are actually in the quarter where we can actually link directly to AI a positive contribution," Cantera said.

"AI is being embedded in all the processes that we are conducting at the bank, so it's really difficult to just say how much comes from AI, but clearly AI is improving the quality of our services to clients."

Swiss pharmaceutical giant Novartis CEO Vas Narasimhan told CNBC that AI was beginning to contribute to the company's bottom line.

Novartis reported third-quarter core operating income of $6.04 billion, up 10% year on year at constant currencies. The company did not quantify how much of that was attributable to AI but Narasimhan told CNBC: "I think this is the first quarter I've really seen AI start to improve actually our P&L on a tangible basis."

"We're working on R&D [research & development] to see if it can really increase R&D productivity," Narasimhan said on "Squawk Box Europe" on Tuesday.

"We're already seeing some of the productivity you saw in the bottom-line performance of what we can do with AI from M&S [marketing & sales]."

Meanwhile, Standard Chartered Chief Financial Officer Manus Costello told "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday that the company is seeing AI "appear everywhere across the business."

The bank reported third-quarter operating income growth of 14% at constant currency, excluding notable items.

"I think you can see it in the top line growth that we're delivering, and you can see it in that operating leverage that I talked about," Costello said, adding that AI is helping managers build better customer relationships and generate more income.

"We're also seeing that strong operating leverage that I talked about, where income is growing much faster than costs, and some of that is because we've been able to deploy AI tools to improve core processes and enable us to scale those up very efficiently," he added.

Carlsberg CEO Jacob Aarup-Andersen said on "Squawk Box Europe" this week that it's not seeing AI benefits at scale, but the technology has impacted internal processes, cost efficiency, commercial and pricing processes, marketing and its supply chain.

The company's revenue grew 4.9% in the third quarter.

Elsewhere, tech firms are also reporting gains. Meta said in its Oct. 21 earnings that its AI investments were delivering "substantial incremental advertising revenue," while total revenue jumped 26% in the quarter ended Sept. 30.

While Alphabet's Google Search revenue growth slowed amid concerns about AI competition, Google Cloud revenue more than doubled to nearly $31 billion in the third quarter, it reported on Wednesday.

#2951. Nets get first win of season with strong defensive effort, beating Pacers, 98-90

The Brooklyn Nets and Indiana Pacers both played in their ABA-inspired uniforms tonight. Their offensive performances looked like they were straight out of the 60s and 70s, but so was Brooklyn's defense, and it got them their first win of the season.

The Nets were led by their two high-scoring vets, Michael Porter Jr. (23) and Julius Randle (20) as well two of the team's younger set, Day'Ron Sharpe who finished with his first double double of the season (15 and 13) and the fast-developing Nolan Traore (16 points, four 3-pointers and six assists.)

Chaney Johnson started beside Nolan Traoré at the top of the key for a second straight game this evening, but the rotation was more fluid than what we've seen so far this year. The Nets went nine men deep in the first period. Terance Mann, who has only gotten burn during garbage time so far this year, even logged some first quarter minutes as the backup point guard with Mikel Brown Jr. sitting.

But with both teams looking to run early, and this being the first of a back-to-back for Brooklyn, those extra bodies were needed. There weren't a lot of statistical fast break points, but both teams moved up the floor with a sense of urgency off misses in the first. Michael Porter Jr., who had a quiet night vs Detroit, was aggressive there and in the halfcourt, pitching in seven points while shooting 2-5 from deep in the opening frame. He ended up as our leading scorer by the end of the game with 23 points on 7-16 shooting from the field and 3-10 from deep. He also pulled down nine rebounds and dished three assists.

But outside of what MPJ provided early on, makes from beyond the arc came at a premium. When Indiana smoked Brooklyn last week, the triple was their primary weapon, as they shot 23-41 from downtown. But today, the Nets kept the safety on, limiting the Pacers to a 2-15 start from deep.

Nobody shot well from the other parts of the floor early either. In the first half, Brooklyn posted .385/.250 splits while Indiana put up .324/.200 marks. Both teams also combined to miss 10 free throws between the first two periods.

"We took good shots and they were not falling," Fernández said. "A lot of times, the anxiety hits, but the team stayed together and won the first quarter, stayed together and won the second quarter, stayed together and won the third quarter, and I think that's what matters the most."

Indeed, even as eager participants in the rock fight, the Nets led after one. Despite their 0-5 record going into tonight, they've now won the first quarter in four straight games, and are 4-2 in the period on the season. They maintained that advantage heading into the break too, up by a 43-40 score. Porter Jr led the team offensively at that point with 13 points on 5-10 shooting, followed by Mo Wagner, who added 10 after a perfect 4-4 start.

"Just taking what the game gives me," Wagner said at halftime. "I think we're playing really hard. We really need one."

Get one, they did. After another string of misses, Porter Jr. began the scoring for both teams in the second half with a four point play…

Then, Traoré hit his first triple of the game off a back-tapped offensive board from Day'Ron Sharpe. That gave Brooklyn their first double digit lead of the ball game. Day'Ron finished tonight with 15 points and 13 rebounds. Fernández called his effort tonight "unbelievable" postgame.

"I mean, he he was big on the boards," Fernández said. "He did a little bit of everything. He made his three. He set screens. Very happy for him. We don't win this game without him, and the reality is how much he cares about winning and impacting winning. And this game, 5-7, 13 rebounds, four assists, two turnovers? I think that line is impressive. So, all his energy and willingness to do the right things, it paid off."

"Day'Ron's a beast, for real," Traoré added. "Like, he's so strong, and when he's playing like that he's unstoppable. Me and him in the pick-and-roll is a really great thing for us, and if I keep finding him like that, he's gonna be great like that every time."

However, that make from MPJ seemed to open the floodgates for everyone. Sharpe buried his own three from the corner a few minutes later. He's now shooting 3-7 from deep on the season. But the Pacers also nailed three triples in the third after making just four in the entire first half. That kept Indiana in striking distance, and with Porter Jr. picking up his fourth foul with 4:39 to go, Brooklyn's lead, which already felt like something out of a dream, looked at risk of fading. It might've too if not for Traoré and Randle, who continued to hit shots with MPJ on the bench.

But while the Nets went into the fourth up nine, the Pacers wouldn't go away. Triples from Andrew Nembhard and Larry Nance Jr. made it a three point game with 9:29 to go.

And, Randle and Traoré stiff armed Indiana again, closing out the game in the process as well.

After two made triples from Quenton Johnson, Julius Randle swung back with an isolation three from the top of the key before driving and assisting on two for Traoré during back-to-back possessions. That reestablished a 10-point lead for Brooklyn with about two and a half to play.

Traoré finished with 16 points and six assists while shooting 6-14 from the field and 4-8 from deep with just one turnover. Randle added 20 points, six assists, and five rebounds while shooting 5-of-15 from the field and 4-of-9 from deep. He was also a +17 for the game. Over the past four games, the 6'4" 20-year-old is averaging 14.3 points on 46/39/72 splits along with four assists and a steal in 26 minutes a game.

"Great spacing," Traoré said. "I know they want to double Julius, so we take advantage of this and make shots. I'm happy and proud of my teammates."

"I thought Nolan had great moments, especially in that second half," Fernández added. "You know, he shot the three well, but those were very good threes. I think that all of them are like Julius spraying the ball, and I think that's huge."

They didn't have the strongest competition with Tyrese Haliburton, Obi Toppin, Ivica Zubac and T.J. McConnell out, but the Nets defense was stout all night, and it stayed that way down the stretch. After those makes from Traoré, the Nets forced Indiana to miss four of their next five shots. It was tough, disciplined, and a crafty effort, as Noah Clowney sold an offensive "push off" foul on Nembhard to get a final stop 52 seconds to go.

Already up seven, that gave the Nets a technical free throw, as Nembhard got hit with a technical with after arguing against the call, and eventually, their first win of the season.

Final: Brooklyn Nets 98, Indiana Pacers 90

With 15 points and 13 rebounds in tonight's win vs. Indiana, Day'Ron Sharpe recorded his fourth career game with 13+ PTS and 13+ REB, two of which have now come against the Pacers (16 PTS, 13 REB on 1/5/25 at home). It was also his 20th career double double, and his first since February 26th of last year.

For the first time this season, the Nets will play the defending champs on Monday night at Madison Square Garden. New York's title defense hasn't gotten off to a great start with the team 2-4. The Nets have their unprotected first rounder this year, so a win here helps increase the value there. Also, it's just good to win against the Knicks. The Nets haven't done that since January 2023, a rivalry record of 14 straight.

"We need to get a win," Traoré said of the matchup. "I think it's been a long time since we've won against the Knicks, so we got to do it."
