#2949. Judge dismisses Alaska Senate election lawsuit; DOJ vows appeal

A federal judge on Sunday dismissed the Department of Justice's extraordinary lawsuit seeking to delay certification of the Alaska Senate election if incumbent Republican Dan Sullivan loses, citing possible voter confusion from another candidate with his name on the ballot.

The ruling came two days before Election Day and two days after the U.S. Attorney for Alaska said ongoing criminal and civil investigations into non-incumbent Dan J. Sullivan's decision to run for the Senate.

The campaign of Democratic nominee Mary Peltola, state election officials and outside advocacy groups had asked Judge Sharon Gleason to dismiss the DOJ's lawsuit in U.S. District Court in Anchorage.

The Department of Justice quickly filed a notice of its intention to appeal the decision to the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals.

Peltola is locked in a tight contest with Sen. Dan Sullivan, whose winner will be determined by a ranked-choice system.

The outcome of the race is one of several contests that determine which party has a Senate majority, where Republicans currently hold a narrow lead.

Neither the other Sullivan nor the fourth candidate on the ballot, Gerald Heikes, is considered a contender.

But voters who rank them as a first choice could swing the outcome by ranking Peltola or Sen. Sullivan second.

Peltola's campaign has been accused of encouraging retired schoolteacher Dan Sullivan to run for the Senate against the incumbent Sen. Dan S. Sullivan and Peltola.

"No sham investigation or baseless lawsuit from a weaponized Department of Justice is going to stop Alaskans from electing Mary on November 3rd so she can lower costs for Alaska's working families," Peltola's campaign said in a statement after Sunday's ruling.

"Alaskans will choose their next U.S. Senator – not Dan Sullivan or his DC allies," the campaign said.

In her 20-page decision, Gleason did not resolve whether another Sullivan had been recruited to confuse voters.

Instead, she said the federal government had failed to establish that it had legal standing to file a civil complaint to postpone certification of the election if Sen. Sullivan lost.

In that event, the DOJ wanted an investigation into voter intent and possible confusion from having two Dan Sullivans on the ballot.

"The absence of any explanation from the United States as to how its requested relief could redress its alleged injury while simultaneously maintaining the secrecy of the ballot is telling," Gleason said in her ruling.

She noted that early voting in the state was already underway, and that ballots already cast have been commingled in a way that would prevent officials from linking individual ballots to voters.

"More fundamentally, the United States provides no workable plan as to how it proposes to segregate ballots cast for Daniel J. Sullivan on Election Day and then conduct a court-ordered unspecified 'investigation' of those ballots in a manner that would preserve voter secrecy," Gleason wrote.

The judge also cited a Supreme Court decision that established the so-called Purcell principle, which "strongly cautions" against courts altering election rules on the eve of election.

She noted that DOJ claimed it was not seeking to alter the "time, place or manner of Alaska's election."

But at the same time, Gleason said, the DOJ was asking her for an order "preventing the Statefrom certifying the election if certain circumstances occur; a court order requiring the State to segregate and preserve certain ballots; and a court order permitting the United States to conduct an investigation into the intent of those voters who cast ballots for Daniel J. Sullivan."

"These requests certainly involve the time, place, and manner of Alaska's election, which are the responsibilities of the State, not the executive branch of the federal government," Gleason said.

The judge said she would not allow the DOJ to amend the lawsuit, which was revised last week to add a new legal claim under the Supremacy Clause of the U.S. Constitution to the first claim under the National Voter Registration Act. The amended complaint also added details of a New York Times article about the alleged recruitment of the second Sullivan.

"The Court finds that further amendment would be futile because this Court lacks the authority to order that certain ballots cast by Alaskans be segregated so that federal law enforcement officials can conduct an unspecified investigation of the voters who cast them," Gleason said.

A DOJ spokesperson said in a statement, "The court dismissed the case on procedural grounds but did not dispute the argument of the United States that Daniel J. Sullivan (a/k/a Decoy Dan) entered the race as a frivolous candidate to confuse voters."

"DOJ plans to file a notice of appeal," the spokesperson said. "Alaskan voters deserve a fair election, not to be misled in an attempt to rig outcomes."

The White House, when asked for comment, referred CNBC to the DOJ's statement.

President Donald Trump has said he ordered the lawsuit, which was filed Oct. 22.

Trump argued that Peltola and other Democrats had recruited the former school teacher to run in hopes of confusing voters who wanted to cast their ballots for Sen. Sullivan.

Trump, in his first comments after the ruling, said: "So we have two Dan Sullivans. This is voter fraud at the highest level. But the Democrat candidate who is involved in the scheme, Mary Peltola, should be removed from the ballot and prosecuted."

The New York Times on Wednesday reported that Peltola campaign aides and DSCC aides had been involved in efforts to recruit the retiree for the race.

Peltola's campaign and the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee have denied having any involvement in his campaign.

DSCC Executive Director Devan Barber said in a statement, "Today's ruling is a victory for democracy and a clear message to Donald Trump and Senate Republicans: No one, no matter how powerful, can toss out free and fair elections just because they don't like the result."

"We will never stop fighting back against the shameful, brazen efforts from the Trump administration and their weaponized Department of Justice to subvert the U.S. Senate race in Alaska, or any other election as voters reject Republicans at ballot boxes across the country – and we are confident Alaska voters will elect Mary Peltola to the Senate," Barber said.

Sen. Sullivan, in an interview on CNN before Gleason issued her ruling, said he would accept the election results if he lost.

But he also blasted Peltola's campaign and the DSCC.

"Recognizing they couldn't run on their record, so they had to cheat," Sullivan said. "I think a lot of Alaskans, even Democrats, are thinking she is not fit for office; she's continually lying to the Alaskan people, and I guarantee you she's lying right now."

Alaska election officials have pointed to steps they took to avoid voter confusion on the ballots for the race.

The ballots list Sen. Sullivan as "Dan S. Sullivan," with the words "incumbent" and "registered Republican" next to his name.

The other Sullivan is listed as "Daniel J. Sullivan," with no other words next to his name.

Election officials originally had blocked the retired teacher from the primary ballot. But a state judge allowed him on the ballot. Alaska's Supreme Court upheld that ruling.

#2947. What’s next?

The 3-4 Carolina Panthers held on to first place in the NFC South thanks to a16-0 win by the 3-5 Tampa Bay Buccaneers over the 3-5 Atlanta Falcons that featured 324 yards rushing and -2 yards passing by the Bucs. This is the company that we keep.

The company we don't keep anymore includes former defensive coordinator Ejiro Evero and former defensive passing game coordinator Jonathan Cooley. The defense has been a point of contention for Panthers fans in recent years as a sort of chicken or egg scenario. Limited investment and limited success have made it difficult to fully evaluate Evero's work on a game-by-game basis. The full course of his tenure in Charlotte, however, clearly left head coach Dave Canales dissatisfied.

Some will say this firing is overdue. Others will question the timing and ultimate purpose of it at this point in the season. Regardless, we shouldn't hold our collective breathe in anticipation of major changes for the Panthers porous defense. The fact remains that with Jaelan Phillips in and out of the lineup with injuries and Cam Jackson, Mike Jackson, Jaycee Horn, Akayleb Evans, Nic Scourton, and Thomas Incoom all on injured reserve, the Panthers are basically playing preseason football out there.

That is not to say that the defense is the only problem unit the Panthers have. Their offensive line has been a specific and obvious weakness for some time. Starters Taylor Moton, Ikem Ekwonu, and Damien Lewis have all been sorely missed throughout their various injuries. Free agent acquisitions Rasheed Walker and Luke Fortner have simply been complete disappointments. Right guard Robert Hunt's play and mental focus may have you questioning if he ever actually did return from his 2025 torn bicep. Rookie offensive tackle Monroe Freeling, meanwhile, is doing his best out there. He could be worse, the unit as a whole however could hardly be. They helped prove two weeks in s row that the Panthers cannot win a shootout if they don't let their quarterback shoot.

Enter the 6-2 Denver Broncos, a team with twice as many wins as any team in the NFC South. The Panthers' Week 9 opponent has a capable passing game and a relatively fearsome defensive line that sacked Patrick Mahomes twice yesterday afternoon. While they only have ten sacks on the season, they may expect to double that total against a Panthers offensive line that has surrendered 11 sacks in the last two games.

They are, in short, a competent, competitive NFL team who will provide a solid test for the Panthers. The Broncos are probably the best team the Panthers have faced so far.

Early season success against backup quarterbacks may have falsely inflated expectations for Carolina, but they still have limited talent on both sides of the ball. There is enough talent that they could steal a few games if they can shore up their obvious weakenesses at corner, right guard, center, left guard, and left tackle. That will be the test that they have to pass against a hungry Broncos team that is looking to build on their own divisional lead.

What us next, in this context, is that test. The next few weeks will be an inflection point for these Panthers. Either they will pull themselves together through adversity or they will squander the hottest start they have had in a season since Sam Darnold was here.

I'm not saying that they have to beat the Broncos to save their season, but they absolutely have to look like they belong on the same field as them. If they can do that then they can at least play a competitive December, further injuries permitting.

#2946. 3 bright spots hiding beneath the Suns’ ugly start

I feel as if much of my focus lately has been doom and gloom as the Phoenix Suns have stumbled out of the gate to a 1–3 record, doing so in a manner that has been particularly frustrating. If there's one thing I appreciate when watching any sport, and one of the primary reasons last year's team resonated with the fan base as a whole, it's defense. I value the ability to stop the opposition from executing its game plan.

Defense travels. Defense wins championships. Defense changes the outcomes of games. And thus far, the Suns' defense has more holes than Swiss cheese. Yes, I spent about as much time putting together that analogy as the Suns have spent playing defense through their first four games.

As I sit here looking out my window at the literal gloom hanging over the Valley, I can't remember a time in my life when we've experienced rain this consistently. And rather than allowing the weather to dictate my mood, I've decided to do what the motto of this very site encourages us to do. I'm going to look at the bright side. That's right. Here are three things I've appreciated about the Phoenix Suns through the early portion of this season.

One of my biggest fears about this team entering the season was how they would perform from beyond the arc. When you ship off Grayson Allen and Royce O'Neale, who are currently shooting a combined 41% from deep with the Charlotte Hornets, there's a legitimate concern that your team might end up sucking from three-point range.

When you look at it holistically, the Suns rank 17th in the NBA, shooting 34.8% from deep. They're certainly maintaining their three-point volume, however, ranking seventh in the league with 40.3 attempts per game. But like so many things early in this season, those numbers are being dragged down by one particularly ugly game.

In the season opener against the Portland Trail Blazers, Phoenix shot 6-of-31 from beyond the arc, a miserable 19.4%. Since that game? The Suns are shooting 38.8%, connecting on 50-of-129 attempts. That 38.8% would rank fifth in the NBA. Damn you, Portland game. Damn you! It feels like we're going to spend the next 15 games waiting for that statistical disaster to work its way out of the numbers.

Even when you include the Portland game, the Suns are doing something that shouldn't surprise anybody. They're getting quality three-point shooting from their bench. Phoenix's reserves are shooting 38.8% from beyond the arc, the sixth-best mark in the league. Remove that Portland game from the equation, and the Suns possess the best shooting bench in the NBA at 45.9%.

Luke Kennard is shooting 53.8% from deep this season, Ryan Dunn is at 40%, Dillon Brooks is connecting at 42.9%, and Jordan Goodwin is shooting 39.1%. And if Collin Gillespie hadn't gone 0-of-8 in that Portland game, he'd be sitting at 42.9% himself.

All of this bodes well for Phoenix moving forward. The Suns have demonstrated that they possess the ability to knock down shots from beyond the arc, and as the season progresses, those numbers should begin to better reflect what we've seen since opening night.

Don't get me wrong, his defense has left plenty to be desired as he continues trying to understand where he needs to be positioned and how to properly support those around him. His missed assignments have been atrocious, and there's no world in which I'm going to sit here and consistently defend his defensive performance throughout the early portion of this season. But holistically, I've liked what I've seen from him thus far.

He's third on the team in scoring at 16.3 points per game, leads the Suns in blocks, and is their top rebounder. Yes, that 22.2% shooting from beyond the arc sucks, and it certainly doesn't help the argument I made earlier about the team's three-point shooting. My assumption, however, is that those numbers will begin to normalize with time.

What I've appreciated most about Miles Bridges is that he reminds me a little bit of Shawn Marion, somebody you don't necessarily have to draw up offensive plays for. His offense is generated through effort, whether that's putbacks, hustle points, or simply finding opportunities within the flow of the game. Every roster needs somebody like that. Somebody who can generate those junkyard points without demanding the basketball, yet still provide meaningful offensive production.

Think about it. Dillon Brooks is averaging 16.5 points per night, but he has to be incredibly ball dominant for those points to materialize. Those are proactive points, whereas much of what Bridges provides comes reactively, taking advantage of opportunities that develop around him. And through that lens, I can see the vision of what Miles Bridges is designed to be within the context of this Suns lineup.

The shooting absolutely needs to improve, as does the defense, but there's something valuable about having a player who can contribute offensively without requiring the entire offense to revolve around him.

Without a doubt, the bench has been the bright spot for the Phoenix Suns. The connectivity that this group possesses is something the starting unit should envy, and a big part of that comes from the continuity the organization talked about throughout the offseason. Much of that continuity has manifested itself within the second unit, where the only new addition to the rotation has been Luke Kennard, who has stepped into the Grayson Allen role pretty seamlessly.

The Phoenix Suns' bench ranks 10th in the league with a +2.2 net rating, third with a 72.5% assist percentage, and fourth with a 1.94 assist-to-turnover ratio. And that's where, as a Suns fan, you begin to realize that this team might not be as far away from being successful as it feels after watching those first four games.

The starting unit certainly needs to pick up the slack, and those players need to learn how to play together. But with 40% of that group being new to the lineup, it's going to take some time for that chemistry to develop. Meanwhile, the backbone of this team has been the bench and the way it operates.

So even as I sit here pounding the table for Collin Gillespie to become a starter, perhaps the stability he provides to the second unit is something that needs to be considered as part of that equation. Because if the starting unit can figure things out, the bench can continue delivering punch after punch after punch when it enters the game, beating the opposition into submission.

Of course, there are multiple issues with that starting unit, and we don't know whether time will ultimately solve them. But you know it, and I know it, because we've seen it with our own eyes while watching these games. When the second unit checks in, you immediately feel a sense of responsibility entering the game.

The Suns have legitimate problems, particularly defensively, and those aren't going to disappear because I decided to write something positive. But the shooting is encouraging, Bridges is finding ways to contribute, and the bench has established an identity worth building upon. There's work to be done, plenty of it. But even with the clouds hanging over the Valley, there's still a little sunshine breaking through.

#2944. The market view of the oil, AI and bond trades went through a major change in October

If there was one safe bet over the course of 2026, it was that treasury yields would rise alongside a move up in oil futures contracts. Over the past month, that ended, and according to Jeffrey Rosenberg, senior portfolio manager of the Systematic Alternatives ETF (IALT) at BlackRock, the world's largest asset manager, the breakdown in that trade has big implications for the market.

"Oil and treasury yields had this amazing correlation and in October it just stopped," Rosenberg said on the most recent CNBC "ETF Edge."

The relationship between oil and bond yields was featured in Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh's FOMC press conference on Wednesday, with Warsh noting how oil futures have continued to rise, a factor the central bank must consider in its fight against inflation. But Rosenberg said what Warsh didn't talk about was "the most important takeaway."

"Treasuries and oil had an 80% correlation this year," Rosenberg said, which is "outsize" relative to normal correlations. "We've basically been trading off oil for good reason." "

What happened? Rosenberg says you can sum it up by looking to the deflated AI trade. As the market moved away from that oil-treasuries trading correlation in October, "It told us something important," he said. "In the market's mind, the geopolitical risk is secondary to AI risk," he said.

Over the past month — which became most painfully clear in Friday's Nasdaq market wipeout and, earlier the week, Google's biggest single-day decline in its history, wiping out half a trillion dollars in value — the AI trade experienced a retracement. Bond yields went lower, Rosenberg says, because the market is equating weakness in the AI trade as a broader weakness in the U.S. economy. And he said, last week's GDP report backed up that thinking. "AI is everything, everywhere, all the time," he said. Both the growth for the economy on the investment side and the wealth effect on the consumption side are all AI, he said.

"Q3 GDP was a blowout [number] and all AI-related," Rosenberg said on the "ETF Edge" podcast extended conversation. He pointed to equipment investment increases and consumption well above expectations and both fueled by the AI wealth effect, which with the latter is even more notable given that real incomes have been coming down in the U.S.

Business investment of 12.7% in Q3 was the biggest increase outside the pandemic since 2011, led by a 37% rise in information processing equipment. Industrial equipment investment rose nearly 25%, which while not a straight AI trade, does partially reflect spending on data centers.

Warsh did not agree in his assessment of the economy at the FOMC press conference. "It is convenient to say that the strength of the economy is all about this new technology wave. In fact, if we look at the data, really including data from the last meeting until this, the capex story is not just robust, not just strong, but it is broadening. And so if you wanted to tell the story somehow that this is strength we're seeing only in narrow pockets, that's a much harder story to tell now than it would have been several quarters ago," he said.

It is true that market breadth, a measure of how many stocks within the S&P 500 were showing positive trading momentum, improved significantly in October after having reached a 2026 low by late September, and as defensive sectors of the stock market like healthcare and staples rallied. But Rosenberg says the conclusion that the broader October market came to is that any "wobble" in the AI trade is going to be seen as a wobble in the growth outlook for the U.S., and as disinflationary. And in a disinflationary environment, there is "only one thing that hedges," he said, and that is "the thing everyone wants to hate. ... the long end of the treasury curve."

"That's the really important lesson from last month of market performance," he said.

Despite this view, Rosenberg is cautious about suggesting that this dynamic makes bonds a safe bet. "The simple answer is it's not a return to normal, where normal was bonds hedging stocks," he said.

That is because of the conflicting forces on bonds. Oil price increases and the persistent level of inflation above target are not going away as challenges to fixed-income assets and the "efficacy," Rosenberg said, of fixed income as a hedge.

"There's too much inflation and that limits the ability of the Fed to provide accommodation, and we haven't gotten away from that," he said. "But what we've learned in the last month is if it's big enough, if the AI trend turns against the macroeconomy and inflationary trends, it can bring about a wholesale change in that direction that's disinflationary and bonds may react like they did in October," he said.

Still, he says investors should not bet the trade will be one-directional or come without a potential cost. Investors need to weigh the potential diversification benefit from bonds based on the October action versus a scenario in which AI does not unravel the economy. And the longer the AI boom persists, coupled with the broader fiscal challenges in the U.S., "bonds will be challenged," he said.

The AI trade itself is a very big piece of the bond market, and that showed up over the past week in a major way between both the Google earnings decline and Microsoft coming back into the public bond market. "It was a surprise to the markets. It hadn't been back in close to a decade," Rosenberg said.

As the capex needs of the tech giants begin to overwhelm their free cash flow more regularly, the public bond markets are coming to terms with an expected flood of issuance. "When the bonds markets, the public bond markets, look at where it is all coming from ... it's a huge marginal issuer, and its increasingly all coming again and again," he said.

The hyperscalers are reaching the limits of the financing that bank loan and private credit markets can offer and while Rosenberg stressed that a $40 trillion to $60 trillion global bond market can absorb an expected $4 trillion in AI debt over the next four years, "the market is not unlimited in capacity."

Rosenberg said it's a good thing that more of the hyperscale debt is being pushed into the public bond market because it means there will be more transparency in the form of bond prices and CDS spreads. While spreads on Microsoft credit default swaps are currently trading near a five-year high, roughly 55, they are still a world away from Oracle CDS spreads, by comparison, which are closer to 300. That presents another trading opportunity for investors who may not want to buy bonds outright given the inflationary environment and fiscal challenges in the U.S., but may still be looking for ways to hedge bets on the AI boom.

"That old tool from the Great Financial Crisis," he said. "Short it. You can trade two ways in [public] credit markets and you can't do that in bank loans and private credit. But once they start going in public markets, you can do that."

Rosenberg said a 12% down day in Google is "not even on the chart" historically. "So it's moving into a different zip code, if you will, of market pricing and market volatility and market indigestion. It's not the end of the world, it's just a different world of the AI buildout," he said, with the greater transparency in the public bond market giving investors more ability to trade "the emotional swings between fear and greed," he said.

What the CDS market is telling investors with the large-cap hyperscaler names, according to Rosenberg, is not that the prospect of default is high, but that as all the companies try to access more debt financing, the capacity of the banks and financial intermediaries to find counter-parties for the risk is becoming more limited.

"Think about financial markets and these big investment booms. They don't move in one direction. They ratchet up, reset." But with the AI boom, it's only been "the positive part," Rosenberg said. "It's still an incredibly positive story, but you get a little of the balance with the AI pessimism."

#2941. Luka Dončić knows the Lakers have to be ‘way better’ when teams blitz him

Any team that rosters Luka Dončić is going to have to learn how to adjust to defenses blitzing ball screens.

Few players in the league, if any, are as dangerous in the pick and roll as Luka. The easiest way for opponents to keep him from doing damage is to stop him before it can really start. That typically manifests itself in blitzing Luka with two defenders on ball screens.

When things are clicking, the Lakers can quickly turn that into an advantageous 4-on-3 scenario. Things have not been clicking for them yet this season, though, and they certainly weren't in the team's loss to the Warriors.

While Luka had a huge night with 37 points, the rest of the Lakers struggled as they blew a big early lead and dropped their second game of the season. After the contest, Luka spoke about teams doubling him in the pick and roll and how the team needs to adjust.

"We just need to do better with that," Luka said. "You create an advantage with two on the ball and you got to go score. It's four on three so we have to be better there. …We have to be aggressive to score. It's an advantage, four on three, and I think we have to be way better at this."

At the beginning of that process is the chemistry with Luka and Walker Kessler. But nearly every aspect of the process is a work in progress, including making good on the opportunities they create when they have an advantage.

"I think the biggest thing is just shooting the shots that we get," Jake LaRavia said postgame on Friday. "A lot of times when you pass up open shot opportunities, it turns into a disadvantage for us and it ends up into either a missed shot or a turnover and we'd much rather take the good shot over that. It's just something that we're going to learn more as the season goes on and as we see this more often."

The irony of LaRavia being the one saying they can't turn down shots considering how he ended last season. Even on Friday, there were multiple open looks he did not take from three. But, if nothing else, that just means he's the one who has heard the most about the need to take advantage of those opportunities.

The good news is that it's only just November and this isn't a surprise that the team needs to build chemistry. But once they are able to get things to click out of the pick and roll, then it feels like things will really take off to another level.

#2940. 5 takeaways from Bills win over Ravens

The Buffalo Bills beat the Ravens 36-10 in Week 8 at home. Baltimore used three quarterbacks in the contest as Lamar Jackson got hurt his first offensive play of the game and Tyler Huntley suffered a turf toe injury later. Nonetheless, the Bills are now 6-1 entering Week 9.

These are my takeaways from Buffalo's victory.

Of course, I'm not expecting Buffalo's defense to allow 144 points at 2.9 yards per play very often. Playing a third-string quarterback made things exceptionally easier for that unit in this game.

BUT… Buffalo's defense did exactly what it should hav against an undrafted rookie quarterback thrust into an emergency relief appearance. That's a positive sign.

Even Derrick Henry was held in check — only 11 carries for 53 yards — and Zay Flowers did not have a catch. Joey Fagnano demonstrated some moxie on a handful of throws yet none were completed to the receiver who entered this game with the NFL's highest yards-per-route-run figure among all qualifying wideouts.

So while it's absolutely correct to mention the caveat regarding this performance from the Bills defense, it was placed in a no-win situation because of the competition level it faced at quarterback.

The Bills have an incredible ability to put forth a C+ / B- performance and either score 30-plus points and/or win by more than one score, both of which happened in this game.

Despite the 16-7 lead at the half, Buffalo absolutely squandered quality scoring chances and made mistakes in the first two quarters. The end of half clock management wasn't ideal. Worked. But the process was off. Overall, it was very disjointed. No doubt.

The Bills went from a somewhat pedestrian 2.0 points per drive in the first half to a massive 4.0 points per drive in the second half. And the vibe of Buffalo's offense and ease at which it moved the football matched those splits. In the third and fourth quarters, the Bills ran it more efficiently and got more pass-catchers involved at all levels of the field.

In the end, Josh Allen finished 21-of-34 for 289 yards — 8.5 yards per — with two passing touchdowns, one interception, one sack, and a respectable passer rating of 96.3.

While, yes, a team's own offense does have an impact on the defense, it's not as if the Ravens third-string unit was out there against Allen and Co.

Finally, the Bills had one of their receivers make a spectacular grab in a low-percentage situation. Moore's 49-yarder down the middle of the field was a thing of beauty we frankly haven't seen much of from Buffalo's pass-catching group over the past few seasons.

His four snags, 82 yards and a score were a jolt to Buffalo's aerial attack.

On the defensive side, Oliver finished with four pressures on 18 pass-rush snaps — good for a whopping 22.2% pressure rate. His bull rush was outstanding throughout, and his first-step quickness gave Baltimore's interior blocking unit problems all game.

The Bills were playing without two key pieces on either side of the ball in Los Angeles and Las Vegas, and their impact was certainly felt in this win.

Despite Moore's production — and outrageous catch on the long ball — it still feels like, collectively, the Bills receivers aren't moving the needle enough on a regular, drive-to-drive basis.

Now, sure, there's Dalton Kincaid at tight end, who had an up-and-down contest, James Cook on the ground and through the air and Ty Johnson making something happen on a critical third down.

But this offense still doesn't want to feature Khalil Shakir — despite his awesome dependability — and Coleman disappeared in this game with one catch for four yards.

And now, Josh Palmer is working through another injury.

I highly doubt the Bills make a move at receiver before the trade deadline. But I would love for them to at least explore that position. As a last resort, how about some Greg Dortch in the offense? I want Allen to be able to lean on his collective of wide receivers a little more this season.

Leonhard likely was licking his chops when Huntley entered the game, and his eyes probably lit up when Baltimore's undrafted rookie quarterback was forced into action. Those developments could've skewed Buffalo's defensive philosophy in this one.

But nonetheless, Leonhard went blitzing berserk in this victory, and I view that as a positive sign.

Interestingly, Leonhard decided to call the game from the box for the first time this season as well. Should we expect the Bills to blitz on over 50% of the opposition's dropbacks every week? Probably not.

But remember, before this game, Buffalo had the NFL's 20th-highest blitz rate at just 27.9%.

Those blitzes were more creative against Baltimore and, sure, Jackson likely could've made some defenders miss in the pocket, but Leonhard's blitz designs regularly got a free runner to the quarterback.

Overall, Fagnano was pressured on 56% of his dropbacks. Huntley was pressured on four of his five dropbacks, and Jackson was pressured on his only one in this game.

I wonder — and will be tracking — if this game acts as a springboard for a genuinely aggressive, blitz-heavy defense the rest of the way.

#2939. Trump defamation suit against Murdoch, WSJ over Epstein letter dismissed with prejudice

A federal judge in Miami on Saturday dismissed with prejudice President Donald Trump's amended defamation lawsuit against media baron Rupert Murdoch and The Wall Street Journal over the newspaper's reporting on a purported 50th birthday letter from the president to notorious sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

District Court Judge Darrin Gayles ruled that Trump had again failed to plausibly allege the newspaper and other defendants acted with actual malice when they published the story in July 2025.

"As with his first attempt, he has failed," Gayles wrote in the 20-page ruling, which came more than six months after he dismissed Trump's first version of the suit. "Grasping at straws, the Amended Complaint attempts to manufacture flaws with the investigation."

By dismissing the suit with prejudice, the judge blocked Trump from filing another suit alleging the same claims about Murdoch and the paper in federal court.

Gayles said the Journal had conducted a "thoughtful and detailed investigation," and he rejected Trump's argument that the newspaper should not have published the story after he denied writing the letter.

The judge did not determine whether Trump actually authored the disputed letter. Trump has adamantly denied writing it.

When Galyes first dismissed the suit in April, he allowed Trump to file an amended version of the complaint to cure legal defects the judge had identified.

Trump was seeking $10 billion in damages from Murdoch and the Journal, which is owned by Murdoch's News Corp.

A spokesperson for the newspaper's publisher, Dow Jones & Co., in a statement to CNBC on Saturday, said, "We're pleased with the court's decision."

A spokesman for Trump's legal team confirmed to CNBC that the president plans to appeal the ruling.

"President Trump will continue to press forward in his powerhouse lawsuit against the Wall Street Journal and all of the other defendants," the spokesman said in a statement. "The President will continue to hold those who mislead the American People with Fake News and smears accountable for their actions."

Gayles, as he did when dismissing the suit for the first time, noted Trump, who has adamantly denied sending the letter to his then-friend Epstein in 2003, had "not plausibly alleged that the Defendants published the Article with actual malice."

Plaintiffs who are public figures like Trump must show a defendant acted with actual malice when making allegedly defamatory statements, according to Supreme Court decisions that Gayles cited at the beginning of his ruling.

Trump's amended lawsuit contained new details and claims, including that Murdoch told the president "I will handle it" during a phone conversation they had about an article describing a birthday letter to Epstein that allegedly bore Trump's signature, Newsweek previously reported.

Gayles rejected each of Trump's arguments in the amended complaint, including that the Journal did not adequately investigate the letter, omitted relevant details, and that the article was published after the president and his representatives denied it was authentic.

Gayles said that accepting that last argument would effectively allow public figures to block unfavorable reporting, which "runs afoul of the First Amendment" that protects freedom of speech and the press.

Saturday's ruling is the latest setback in litigation between Trump and the media.

Another federal judge in Miami on Oct. 22 ordered Trump to sit for a deposition in his $10 billion defamation lawsuit against the BBC by Dec. 11.

The Journal's article on July 17, 2025, said a letter bearing Trump's signature was included in an album of letters Epstein was giving for his 50th birthday.

The article said Trump sent the letter at the request of Epstein's close friend, Ghislaine Maxwell, who two decades later was convicted of procuring underage girls to be sexually abused by Epstein.

The Journal noted that the letter "contains several lines of typewritten text framed by the outline of a naked woman, which appears to be hand-drawn with a heavy marker."

"A pair of small arcs denotes the woman's breasts, and the future president's signature is a squiggly 'Donald' below her waist, mimicking pubic hair," Journal reporters Khadeeja Safdar and Joe Palazzolo said in the article.

"The letter concludes: 'Happy Birthday — and may every day be another wonderful secret,'" they wrote.

Trump angrily denied writing the letter, saying, "This is not me. This is a fake thing."'

"I never wrote a picture in my life. I don't draw pictures of women," the president said at the time.

#2936. Jonah Coleman makes statement as Broncos RB of the future

During Sunday's game against the Kansas City Chiefs, it became clear a shakeup was underway at the running back position. When the game began, JK Dobbins was given plenty of opportunity to lay the ground game foundation on which Bo Nix's passing attack would be predicated. None of Dobbins 7 carries would go for longer than 6-yards and he would finish his night running for just 13-yards, averaging an unacceptable 1.9 yards per carry. His long of 6-yards was only eclipsed by the -8-yards he managed during a backwards run in the 2nd quarter.

It was clear last week against the Cardinals, with his 10 carries for 39-yards and at touchdown, that rookie Jonah Coleman was poised to take a hammer to the running back depth chart. Against the Chiefs on Sunday, he did just that. While patiently waiting for Dobbins to self-immolate on the field, Coleman muscled out 41-yards on 11 carries for a touchdown. While a 3.7 yards-per-carry isn't anything to get excited about, it's nearly twice the 1.9 that Dobbins offered. When you factor in Coleman's receiving stats, 4 receptions for 63-yards compared to Dobbins' grand total of zero, Broncos Country should have no doubt just whom their bell cow will be going forward.

So, why did it take so long for Sean Payton to embrace what all of Broncos Country knew early in the 1st quarter?

Coleman was unable to hide his 'it' factor just as much as Dobbins couldn't contain the 'not-it' oozing from his every pore. Sure, Dobbins is a seasoned veteran who has earned every amount of respect and efference he's been given, but in the face of the 100+ yards that Coleman produced (especially with carries primarily given to him in the second-half), there is no putting the toothpaste back in the tube.

Jonah Coleman is the Broncos future at running back.

This doesn't mean that Dobbins shouldn't play, be released, or anything like that. If anything, having an explosive playmaker like Coleman in the backfield is a good thing for him. With Coleman, defenses will have to show a greater respect the run and that will translate to better opportunities on the ground (and not to mention in the air) when Dobbins is in. With RJ Harvey and Tyler Badie on IR, it's going to be the Coleman-Dobbins show for the foreseeable future (barring some blockbuster move as we close in on the NFL trade deadline).

Broncos Country saw what JK Dobbins was capable of during a solid performance before being injured last season. While he's struggled to regain that form and dominance this season, it's not impossible to think that he can't regain some of that form in the coming weeks. That Kansas City defense, with all the deflected balls and constant pressure Bo Nix endured, managed to play the Broncos as tough as anyone with Dobbins being the primary victim. The Broncos schedule gets less difficult going forward and with opposing teams keying in on Coleman, Dobbins might find some opportunities to get his game going.

However, that maturation will be up to Dobbins. The Broncos have found their star back and it's Jonah Coleman. Will Dobbins shrink and pout in the face of competition or will he jump in and embrace the future with both legs churning? The future of the Broncos running attack is looking exceptionally bright. Dobbins will have to find a way to complement that or he'll end up watching from the sideline.

#2935. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2934. SpaceX loses bid to toss NAACP lawsuit over Memphis data center air pollution

SpaceX's AI division, SpaceXAI, lost its bid to dismiss an NAACP lawsuit, which is proceeding in federal court in Mississippi, according to a judge's opinion released late Friday.

The lawsuit claims that the AI division of CEO Elon Musk's aerospace and defense tech conglomerate violated the Clean Air Act with its use of gas-fired combustion turbines to power its massive AI data centers in Greater Memphis.

The Colossus data centers and a power plant that SpaceXAI is also building in Southaven, Mississippi, have faced community backlash for years.

The Southern Environmental Law Center and Earthjustice filed the suit on behalf of the NAACP in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of Mississippi in April 2026.

The suit alleges that between August and December 2025, xAI and its subsidiary MZX Tech, installed and operated 27 gas turbines in Southaven, Mississippi, "without an air permit or regard for the health and safety of people living nearby."

(SpaceXAI is owned and operated by SpaceX, which has rebranded it as SpaceXAI or SpaceXSI.)

The turbines emit smog-forming pollutants and particulate matter that can increase health risks and cause an unpleasant odor, among other unwanted impacts, according to the suit.

Lawyers for SpaceXAI argued in their motion to dismiss, filed in June, that the NAACP suit should be tossed because the company's use of the turbines, which it characterized as "mobile," was exempt from permitting requirements. They also argued that NAACP didn't have a right to sue under the federal Clean Air Act, and suits by such community groups were unconstitutional.

The judge presiding over the case rejected those arguments, writing: "Because the plaintiffs have statutory standing under the Clean Air Act, because the combustion turbines are not 'mobile sources' under Mississippi's SIP, and because the citizen suit provision does not violate Article II of the Constitution or the private nondelegation doctrine, the plaintiffs' claims will proceed."

The decision comes about two weeks after the same court rejected a Department of Justice's request to end the case against Musk's company.

SpaceX did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

#2932. ‘Pop-up’ super PACs pour $637 million into 2026 midterm races

A super PAC called Our Heritage Matters registered with the Federal Election Commission on Oct. 15.

Five days later, it began spending to boost third-party candidates and attack Republicans. By Oct. 29, the group had spent about $1.2 million across 10 House and Senate races, according to a CNBC analysis of FEC filings.

What voters still cannot see is who supplied the money.

Because Our Heritage Matters formed after a key campaign finance reporting deadline on Oct. 15, its first regular report identifying its donors is not due until Dec. 3, a month after Election Day.

The group is an extreme example of a broader surge reshaping the midterm election: late-forming super PACs, including the latest-forming "pop-up PACs," pouring money into races before voters know where it came from.

Federal law requires super PACs and political committees to report large independent expenditures within 24 or 48 hours depending on how close to Election Day the spending occurs, showing how much they spent and which candidates they supported or opposed. Their donors, however, are disclosed less frequently through periodic campaign finance reports.

And in 2026, the practice of creating new, last-minute PACs has exploded. Both parties are using the tactic, with Republican-linked PACs spending about twice what those connected with Democrats have disbursed.

Twenty-two super PACs that registered with the FEC during the final 10 weeks before Election Day have each spent at least $1 million on congressional races, according to CNBC's analysis. Together, they had reported roughly $637 million in independent expenditures by Oct. 29 — about one-sixth of all independent spending reported in House and Senate races this election cycle.

The number of million-dollar late-forming super PACs has more than quadrupled since 2022, while their spending has grown nearly 35-fold.

At the same point in the 2022 midterms, just five comparable groups had spent about $18 million combined, according to CNBC's analysis. In 2018, three had spent about $12 million. The total has even eclipsed the past two presidential elections, when comparable late-forming super PACs spent less than $50 million combined on congressional races in both 2020 and 2024, according to CNBC's analysis.

"The reason for this is almost entirely to avoid disclosure, even if just temporarily," said Michael Kang, a Northwestern Pritzker School of Law professor who studies campaign finance. "Many times, if the donors were known, then it would undercut their cause."

CNBC's analysis includes super PACs formed in the final 10 weeks before Election Day that spent at least $1 million on House or Senate races by Oct. 29. Spending includes regular FEC filings and 24- and 48-hour reports, with duplicates removed.

One of the defining features of late-forming super PACs is speed.

Among the 22 groups in CNBC's analysis, 18 began spending within 30 days of launching, and almost all started spending before filing their first regular campaign finance report showing where their money came from.

“It’s happening more often, and they’re spending more money doing it because it works, and they’re getting away with it,” said Saurav Ghosh, director of federal campaign finance reform at the Campaign Legal Center. The group is a nonpartisan nonprofit founded by a former Republican FEC chairman.

Despite their late start, the groups accounted for more than 22% of independent expenditures in congressional races during CNBC's 2026 window, up from less than 2% at the same point in each of the previous two midterms.

Many are new only on paper. CNBC found financial or organizational ties between nine of the 22 groups and established political networks.

Lower Costs Now, for example, formed on Sept. 10 and disclosed an affiliation with the Future Forward PAC, the main outside group that backed former Vice President Kamala Harris' 2024 presidential campaign. Nineteen days later, Lower Costs Now began spending in Senate races.

But voters did not get a fuller picture of its financing until Oct. 15, when its first regular financial report showed that Senate Majority PAC, the main super PAC working to elect Senate Democrats, had given the group $23 million, according to FEC filings.

By Thursday, Lower Costs Now had reported about $75.5 million in independent expenditures across eight Senate contests, including Iowa, Texas, Kansas and Ohio, according to CNBC's analysis.

No Going Back PAC formed Sept. 1 and began spending three days later. Public reporting soon linked it to MAGA Inc., President Donald Trump's marquee super PAC. But its formal financial report did not arrive until Oct. 20 — more than six weeks after the spending began.

By then, No Going Back had already spent roughly $187.8 million, according to FEC filings, which revealed that MAGA Inc. had supplied the group with $200 million. By Thursday, the super PAC had spent roughly $242.2 million, according to FEC filings.

“They are a vehicle designed to emerge from nowhere and spend a bunch of money on an election” before voters can learn “who’s supplying that money and what their motivations might be,” Ghosh said.

Many of the new groups are also narrowly targeted.

Fourteen of the 22 million-dollar super PACs identified by CNBC spent all of their money in a single congressional contest.

Take The Maine Standard. It formed Sept. 11 and began spending five days later. Since then, it has spent $8.2 million, all of it opposing Democrat Troy Jackson in Maine's Senate race, according to FEC filings.

The Cornhusker Majority followed almost the same pattern. It formed Sept. 10, began spending Sept. 16 and has spent about $7.1 million in Nebraska's Senate race, almost all of it opposing independent Dan Osborn, according to FEC filings. Osborn is running against Republican incumbent Sen. Pete Ricketts in a tight race.

The connection between the two groups did not appear in their regular financial reports until Oct. 15: Both were financed by Senate Leadership Fund, the GOP's main Senate super PAC.

On the Democratic side, Texas Forever launched Sept. 24 and began spending five days later, according to FEC filings. By Thursday, it had spent about $27.5 million to help Democratic Texas Senate nominee James Talarico, according to the FEC. Unlike some other pop-up groups, however, its initial FEC filing identified the Senate Majority PAC as an affiliate.

"A locally branded PAC doesn't necessarily hide where the money comes from, but it fragments the picture. Instead of seeing one national organization spending tens of millions of dollars across the country, voters see a series of different groups operating race by race," said Hilary Braseth, executive director of OpenSecrets, a nonpartisan nonprofit that tracks such spending.

But even identifying the national group behind a pop-up PAC can still leave another layer of the money trail hidden.

Texas PAC formed Aug. 28 and soon began spending to help Republican Ken Paxton in Texas' Senate race. The money flowed through the SLF, but Elon Musk's role was not public until Oct. 12, more than a month after the spending began.

On Oct. 15, FEC filings showed Musk had given the SLF $158 million in two contributions, with the group transferring matching amounts to Texas PAC the day after each donation.

By Oct. 29, Texas PAC had spent about $181.6 million in the race, according to FEC filings, making it one of the largest late-forming super PACs CNBC identified.

Together, Texas PAC and No Going Back accounted for roughly two-thirds of all spending by the 22 million-dollar groups. But the surge was much broader than those two giants: Even excluding them, the remaining groups spent nearly 12 times as much as comparable late-forming super PACs did at the same point in 2022.

"The growth is huge this year," Kang said. "Maybe you don’t want to be known this election, or want to hide away for a period while you might be unpopular, like AI is right now, but you kind of want to build a reputation for the players inside the system."

#2931. The Good, Bad & Ugly from the Miami Dolphins Week 8 loss to the New England Patriots

The Miami Dolphins entered Sunday's matchup against the New England Patriots looking to build on their first victory of the 2026 season. For 30 minutes, it looked like Miami might actually be capable of putting together back-to-back wins.

The Dolphins took a 13-3 lead into halftime before watching the Patriots score 21 unanswered points to take control of the game. Miami's offense disappeared, its defense couldn't find an answer for Drake Maye, and running back Ollie Gordon II provided an unfortunate reminder that the Dolphins still have some growing up to do.

The Dolphins and head coach Jeff Hafley are now left wondering what could have been after another frustrating afternoon.

Let's take a look at the good, bad, and ugly from Miami's 24-19 loss to the Patriots.

Miami entered halftime with a 13-3 lead over New England with strong performances on both sides of the football.

The defense created an early turnover when Jacob Rodriguez forced an Efton Chism fumble with a thrilling peanut punch, and Miami's offense took advantage of the opportunity with a field goal. Malik Willis and the offense eventually put together a touchdown drive late in the second quarter when Willis completed a one-yard touchdown pass to Malik Washington with eight seconds remaining in the half, giving Miami a 10-point advantage.

It was the type of drive the Dolphins desperately needed. The touchdown gave Willis and the offense some much-needed momentum heading into the locker room.

Unfortunately, the momentum quickly faded, as did the Miami Dolphins' lead…

For as good as the Dolphins played in the first half, the second half saw the team come crashing back down to earth. Miami was outscored 21-6 in the second half, allowing Drake Maye and the Patriots to walk (and throw) all over them en route to a crushing loss.

Maye and the Patriots stormed out of the locker room looking like a completely different offense. The Patriots' quarterback connected with Kyle Williams for a long 43-yard touchdown early in the third quarter, then led another scoring drive that ended with a two-yard touchdown run of his own.

The biggest problem wasn't that the Patriots scored 21 points during the final two quarters of play — it was how quickly Miami lost control of a game it had spent the first half controlling.

Hafley and his coaching staff will need to go back to the drawing-board to figure out how to keep Miami from losing another double-digit halftime lead as the season progresses.

Ollie Gordon II has ugly display on Miami's sideline following penalty

There is a difference between being frustrated and allowing that frustration to boil over into something that costs your team.

Ollie Gordon II crossed that line on Sunday.

Early in the third quarter, Gordon was called for a taunting penalty after a short run against the Patriots. The 15-yard penalty was crushing, and head coach Jeff Hafley responded by pulling Gordon to the sidelines and admonishing the young player for his mistake.

Gordon did not take kindly to that decision.

The running back was seen screaming at Hafley on the sideline, with Malik Willis also getting caught up in the confrontation. Nobody is going to criticize Gordon for being competitive. The young man plays hard and wants to win. Those are good things.

But there is a time and a place for that kind of emotion.

Gordon had just committed a penalty that hurt his team. Instead of accepting the consequences and moving on, he turned the situation into an ugly sideline spectacle. Gordon has been thrust into a significant role following De'Von Achane's season-ending knee injury, and the Dolphins need him to be productive. But they also need him to be accountable for his actions.

Gordon has shown plenty of promise this year — he has the talent to become an important piece of Miami's offense, not just this season, but moving forward as well.

Now he needs to show that he can handle the emotional side of the game too.

The Dolphins snatched defeat from the jaws of victory on Sunday against the Patriots. The loss drops the team's record to 1-6 on the year. Do you think they can get back on the right side of the scoreboard with a victory against the visiting Detroit Lions next week? Let me know in the comments below or on Twitter at @MBrave13. Fins up.

#2929. For JPMorgan, Amazon, fighting AI bots like Meta Muse may not be a winning long-term strategy

The biggest names in banking and retail are starting to fight the tech titans, and one another, over a new kind of customer: the AI personal agent.

JPMorgan Chase, Charles Schwab, Amazon, Neiman Marcus and Kohl's have moved to block personal agents like Meta Muse just as consumers start sending them out to find better savings rates, shop for deals and cancel subscriptions.

The era of agentic AI has arrived, but there is uncertainty about what the landscape will look like when virtual storefronts become flooded with AI agents or when a business tries to block them.

Reports last week of AI agents going rogue in customer accounts — and prominent politicians including Sen. Mark Warner and Sen. Bernie Sanders calling for bot legislation in response to the headlines — come on the heels of JPMorgan Chase CEO Jamie Dimon saying the bank currently blocks external AI agents from operating on behalf of retail customers, citing security risks.

Some other retailers are leaning in. Walmart announced last week it is opening the virtual doors to AI agents that customers prefer to use. "We price products, not people, so the prices that an AI agent sees are the same prices that a human would see on Walmart.com, with applicable offers and fees shown before purchase," the company said in a blog post announcing the decision.

A recent audit of 51 large U.S. retailers' published access rules by identity-verification company Billions Network and affiliated company AmanTrue, found only 11 have taken a deliberate position on AI agents. Five, including Amazon, Kohl's and Neiman Marcus, explicitly restrict them, while 37 block shopping agents largely by accident, through older bot rules written for scrapers. None has a way for a verified agent to complete a purchase.

Based on Billions Network's analysis, blocking an agent can occur in multiple ways: blocking a specific bot from a third-party by its rules (e.g. as Chewy does with an Amazon bot), and employing more general blocks on any third-party bots attempting to access login, registration and checkout functions. Its analysis of published access rules comes with the caveat that those rules are not proof of technical enforcement.

Evin McMullen, Billions Network and AmanTrue co-founder and CEO, said at this point, "Most of the retailers in our audit have not made a decision about AI agents at all."

Michael Abbott, global banking lead at the consulting firm Accenture, thinks that businesses blocking AI agents will find it difficult to win with that approach over the long term.

"If customers explicitly authorize an AI agent to act on their behalf, that's ultimately an extension of customer choice. The more important question isn't whether institutions can block agents, but whether they can create a secure and trusted way for them to engage," Abbott said.

Abbott said that Accenture research found that 65% of consumers are open to using a GPT-like financial assistant through a Gen AI platform or digital wallet, and 71% would welcome an AI assistant embedded within their primary bank's mobile app.

"We've seen this movie before … online banking, mobile banking and digital wallets. Customers tend to gravitate toward experiences that reduce friction and save time. The institutions that win are usually the ones that find a way to participate safely rather than resist the change," Abbott said.

The real opportunity for banks is to become the trusted provider of the data, intelligence and execution that those agents rely on.

"As interactions increasingly happen beyond bank-owned channels, banks still need to be present at those critical decision moments, even if they don't own the interface," Abbott said.

When it comes to distinguishing legitimate security concerns from companies protecting profits by making it harder to move money or comparison-shop, Abbott said both things can be true at the same time.

"There are very real concerns around fraud, identity verification, liability and consumer protection. And customers expect their banks to safeguard their assets and data — those responsibilities don't disappear simply because an AI agent is involved," Abbott said, adding that at the same time, consumers have historically benefited from technologies that increase transparency and make it easier to compare options.

"Our research found that 82% want to approve every AI action and 79% want the ability to immediately pause or stop AI activity. That tells us consumers are looking for convenience, but not at the expense of control," Abbott said.

Mike Danford, owner and chief revenue officer of Adverio, which runs Amazon, Walmart and Target marketplace operations for e-commerce brands, said that some of the security fears businesses, including banks, cite are less about security and more about whose sandbox a customer plays in.

"Amazon blocked Muse and other outside agents, but its own Buy for Me feature uses Amazon's agent on other brands' sites. Amazon doesn't appear worried about the agent. It's worried about who holds the data," Danford said. "Amazon now lets shoppers set rules that auto-purchase when a price threshold is met, and that's the version of agent buying consumers seem to accept, because the shopper still approves the decision within predefined parameters," he added.

"We think it's fairly straightforward that third-party applications that offer to make purchases on behalf of customers from other businesses should operate openly and respect service provider decisions about whether or not to participate," an Amazon spokesperson said. "Agentic third-party applications such as Muse have the same obligations, and we've requested that Meta remove Amazon from the experience," the spokesperson added.

Danford says banks are similar, just with a fee instead of a wall.

"JPMorgan now charges aggregators like Plaid for pulling account data, so the toll lands on the apps a consumer uses and may trickle down in subscription fees," he said.

Plaid said at the time of the September 2025 deal that it would not impact any of its existing pricing agreements with customers. Plaid does not charge consumers, though apps and services that use Plaid determine their own customer fees and subscription prices.

Michael Jabbara, head of payment ecosystem risk and control at the payments network Visa, declined to speak to any single bank's motives for blocking agentic AI, but he said the fraud risks from agents are "very real and very tangible."

Consumers can be fooled by fake agents built by fraudsters to steal their credentials and payment details, he said, and a legitimate agent can act in ways the customer never intended, like buying a sofa the shopper had only asked it to find.

Agents can also behave unpredictably: Jabbara described a user who asked his agent to book a gym slot, and when the slot was taken, the agent hacked the gym's scheduling software, deleted the existing appointment and put the user's name in its place. The concerns, he said, come down to knowing "who the agent is, who the agent is acting on behalf of," and whether it has "the appropriate permissions."

"Agents are not just exchanging data. Agents can also take an action on behalf of a user," he said.

Like other experts, Jabbara says past shifts from online to mobile shopping are instructive, but for another reason: "None of those innovations really scaled until people were able to build that foundational security layer that enabled trust for that scale to happen."

Another worry is volume. Millions of agents doing legitimate work for customers could swamp websites and customer-service systems, a phenomenon some call "agentic flooding."

Amid the sudden rise of personal agents like Muse, Wharton School AI expert Ethan Mollick posted, "We will soon see what will be, in effect, a perfectly legal distributed denial of service attack on customer service departments authorized by millions of consumers when it becomes widely known that Muse/Dot/etc can make unlimited requests for old refunds, negotiate deals, etc."

Abbott calls it a possibility, not a crisis. "I see it more as an infrastructure challenge than an existential threat," he said. "Most digital systems today were designed for human interactions. Agent-driven interactions could occur at a completely different scale and frequency. One person might have multiple agents continuously monitoring rates, researching products or evaluating alternatives."

Nevertheless, he says the answer isn't to block them. "It's to build the next generation of infrastructure that can authenticate trusted agents, verify permissions, manage identity and support machine-to-machine interactions efficiently," he said.

Which, he added, is nothing new for banks.

"Customers used to interact with their banks a couple of times a month and now with mobile it is nearly daily. AI agents are just the next evolution. The organizations that prepare for that reality will be better positioned than those that try to prevent it," he said.

While many financial sites already have defenses against overuse, to protect against the types of mechanized denial-of-service attacks that have long been possible, agents "never sleep and never lose focus," said Ed Felten, a Princeton professor emeritus of computer science who served as chief technologist at the Federal Trade Commission and deputy U.S. chief technology officer in the Obama administration. And the more agents use a site, the more the costs could rise for companies like banks.

But Felten doubts the blocking will last. "When a button is clicked in the user's browser, it will be hard for a remote site to tell whether this was done by a human hand or directed by an AI agent on the user's computer," he said. "If there is a battle over AI access, it will slow the adoption of AI agents. But in the end, agents will win out because they will be so useful to users, so that sites that are too effective in blocking agents will lose business," he added.

As the market forces sites to accept their customers' agents, common expectations will emerge about what is reasonable behavior by agents, experts said.

But Joseph Chalom, CEO of SharpLink, one of the largest corporate holders of the cryptocurrency ether, and a former BlackRock executive, expects the gates to stay shut for a while.

"These large incumbents effectively control the access points and will seek to keep their gates closed to fend off this innovative threat. Disruptors have fought the same battle for access for decades across travel, telephony, and financial data consolidators," Chalom said, adding that in each case, the incumbent has cited security concerns, but in fact they also face significant business model interruption.

"For example, an AI agent working for users can easily rebook flight tickets at a lower cost, eating into the margins of airlines. And more significantly, an AI agent can move idle cash from checking and savings accounts earning little interest to a money market account earning 10x the interest rate," Chalom said, noting that these types of "switching costs" are being reduced to near zero as agents have full financial literacy and attention span.

"For this reason, I don't expect any of them to be in a hurry to lower the drawbridge," Chalom said.

In the near term, Abbott says, some institutions will welcome agents while others stay restrictive as they sort out authentication, liability and governance. Longer term, he says, the data shows that companies should expect people to hand the administrative work of financial decisions to their agents. "Defense can prevent you from losing in the short term but to win banks will need an offensive strategy," Abbott said.

Proprietary bots will need to be good enough to not only keep customers but keep them from wanting to use other agents, and that is how CEOs like Amazon's Andy Jassy are talking about it. "We believe Alexa for Shopping is going to be the primary shopping agent customers use to shop Amazon. Having a shopping assistant constantly by your side as you browse, get inspired, have questions on product detail pages, and that you can simply ask to buy something based on your prior purchases that already have your history, your shipping address, and payment info, is uniquely helpful and convenient for customers," he said during last week's latest quarterly earnings call.

Ultimately, brands are about to be tested in ways that haven't been possible before. Human customers become attached to brands for reasons beyond price: a nostalgic cereal that evokes warm fuzzy childhood memories, an airline soaked in the nostalgia of family vacations, or a bank with a favorite teller. An AI agent doesn't care about any of that.

"Brand loyalty and customer relationships are getting tested. An AI agent has no feeling about a logo or a ten-year relationship. It has a mandate and cares purely about outcomes," said Nikhil Behl, president of software at FICO, the analytics company best known for credit scores. "The levers that used to create stickiness get replaced by levers an agent can actually read, such as consistent approvals, transparent pricing, fast resolution, and terms that hold up through comparison," Behl said.

#2928. Why AI is boosting Meta's ad business while hurting Google search

Google and Facebook have dominated the internet for the past two decades, turning billions of eyeballs into trillions in advertising dollars over that stretch. But with the ChatGPT era altering how consumers engage online, the companies are diverging.

In the third quarter, Meta's ad business grew more than twice as quickly as Google's Search and other unit, backing up Mark Zuckerberg's claim that his company's investments in artificial intelligence are leading to better targeting tools for brands to promote goods and services across Facebook and Instagram.

Google's problem is that chatbots are becoming an alternative to search, leading consumers to click far fewer blue links and hurting Google's ad revenue. Anthropic's Claude chatbot and Meta's recently launched Muse assistant are adding to the mix. Meanwhile, users are spending more time scrolling on Instagram, giving Meta more advertising opportunities.

"It's a double-edged sword, because as AI revolutionizes discovery, people might come across tools they like better than Google," Nate Elliott, an analyst covering AI at Emarketer, told CNBC in an email. "If ChatGPT or Claude or Muse is better at helping them find what they're looking for, people's search habits might change for the first time in a generation. That's potentially catastrophic for Google's ad business."

Meta's ad revenue rose 26% in the quarter to $63.1 billion, up from $ 50.1 billion a year earlier, compared with 12% growth for Google's search division, which brought in $63.4 billion. That 14-point growth gap is the widest in Meta's favor since 2021, and it puts the two businesses neck and neck by size, though Google got an additional $11 billion in third-quarter revenue from YouTube.

Following its disappointing third-quarter earnings report on Wednesday, Alphabet had its worst day ever on the stock market, plunging more than 12% and wiping out about $500 billion in market cap. Meta isn't exactly a Wall Street darling at the moment either, as the stock fell 14% in October for its worst month since October 2022. That followed a 27% surge in September, which was its best month since November of that year.

Investors' reactions reflected market jitters surrounding the companies' AI buildouts, which have pushed both businesses into negative free cash flow territory. Questions are swirling about whether their historic spending on AI infrastructure will generate enough revenue.

Meanwhile, both Meta and Google are aggressively trying to diversify their revenue streams. Meta, which still relies on ads for nearly all its revenue, is pouring money into AI models and agents to build a subscription business. Google already has a great and rapidly expanding cloud computing division that just reported over 100% growth to $31 billion in quarterly revenue, driven by demand for its infrastructure and its custom AI chips called tensor processing units, or TPUs.

The boon in online advertising, and the hefty margins that come along with it, catapulted the two companies into leadership positions and brought their market caps into the trillions.

Late 2022 brought the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT and ushered in a new era of generative AI technology. While Meta initially struggled to find a consistent AI strategy and fell far behind OpenAI and Anthropic in developing frontier models, the company has successfully infused AI into its ad products, giving brands advanced tools to reach users more efficiently.

A year ago, the company unveiled the Meta AI business assistant, which advertisers can plug into the Ads Manager system to help build campaigns and improve performance. Meta CFO Susan Li said on last week's earnings call that advertisers adopting the system's recommendations "drove almost 5% more conversions" and "increased their advertising investment by 9% compared with similar non-adopters."

Li gave the example of syrup producer Simply Organic Maple, and said the company, in using the assistant, saw a 57% increase in purchases and a 36% decrease in cost per purchase. She added that in the third quarter, almost 10 million advertisers used at least one of its AI tools.

Google has a similar opportunity with AI in ad products across its properties, including YouTube.

Philipp Schindler, Google's chief business officer, said on the earnings call that the company is deploying its Gemini models "across our entire ads infrastructure," adding tools for advertisers and improving ad quality.

However, Google's search engine is competing directly with ChatGPT and Claude by surfacing more results through AI Overviews and AI Mode, where users primarily get summaries rather than links to other sites. Search queries hit a record high in the quarter, Google said, but revenue growth is slowing.

"That's going to bring Search cannibalization fears right back to the forefront, made worse by META's ad strength this quarter and the lingering Muse/agent threat," analysts at UBS wrote in a note after Google's results.

Both companies are so bullish on AI's potential that they're pumping hundreds of billions of dollars into the infrastructure needed to build new models and services. Google projects up to $205 billion in capital expenditures this year, while Meta expects to spend up to $145 billion.

Advertising remains key, but both are investing in a future that could look very different. Google is much further along on diversification, as ads now account for less than two-thirds of its revenue, while Meta still counts on ads for 98% of sales.

Still, analysts at Wedbush see Google's search monetization as critical to the investment thesis.

"GOOGL shares are unlikely to work until the search narrative changes," the analysts wrote in a report on Thursday. "And it will likely require new ad products and proof points that it can monetize in the evolving AI era as it did in the past."

— CNBC's Jonathan Vanian and MacKenzie Sigalos contributed to this report.
