#2926. OpenAI deepens Abu Dhabi foothold as Sam Altman inks deals spanning energy, government and investment

OpenAI has signed deals across energy, investment and government sectors in the United Arab Emirates as the AI lab works to secure funding from investors in the region as part of a $20 billion round.

CEO Sam Altman met with Khaled bin Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan, crown prince of Abu Dhabi and Chairman of the Abu Dhabi Executive Council, as the artificial intelligence lab secured arrangements for a number of sectors to use its models, the Abu Dhabi Media Office said on Monday.

While the Middle East has emerged as a key AI hub in recent years, with U.S. big tech companies signing deals to develop huge data centers in the region, ongoing conflict has tested that appeal.

OpenAI and Anthropic have turned to Middle East investors as they've looked for ever-larger pools of capital to fund growth.

MGX, an Abu Dhabi-based investment firm, has backed both labs, including co-leading Anthropic's $30 billion raise in February and OpenAI's $122 billion raise in March. OpenAI is currently seeking to raise $20 billion in a new funding round co-led by investors from the United Arab Emirates, CNBC reported on Saturday.

"Abu Dhabi and the UAE are setting a bold, global standard for AI integration across both the public and private sectors to make sure the benefits of AI reach everyone," Altman said in a statement. "We are proud to support the UAE's ambition, anchored in a shared commitment to safety, accessibility and responsible innovation."

The Abu Dhabi National Oil Company, or ADNOC, G42 signed an agreement to deploy OpenAI's frontier technology. Sovereign investor Mubadala, Inception42 and OpenAI signed an agreement to apply advanced AI capabilities to investment analysis.

The Abu Dhabi Government announced an initiative with the AI lab to translate government policies and regulations in machine-readable form. Inception42 and OpenAI also signed a five-year agreement to enable the adoption of frontier AI across government, enterprise and education.

#2925. These Senate races may help determine the fate of Social Security

Senators elected this November must answer a crucial question before their six-year term ends: What will they do to fix Social Security?

The program's trustees project that the trust fund Social Security relies on to help pay retirement benefits may be depleted in the fourth quarter of 2032. Without action from Congress before then, those benefits could be cut by 22%.

Lawmakers may enact reforms to prevent those automatic cuts. Or they may kick the can down the road.

Most voters — 81% — say they are more likely to support a candidate who has a plan to avoid automatic benefit cuts to Social Security over a candidate who promises not to touch the program, according to a recent survey of voters in states with competitive Senate races by the Peter G. Peterson Foundation, a nonpartisan organization.

Even so, many Senate candidates' campaign platforms lack specifics on plans for the program's future.

While 35 Senate seats are up for grabs in November, including in special elections in Florida and Ohio, CNBC identified nine races where the candidates have mentioned their plans for the program's future. MS NOW says all nine races are closely contested battlegrounds, and it rates the possible outcomes.

Here is a closer look at those nine contests and what the candidates in those races have said about Social Security reform.

Alaska voters will choose between incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan and former Democratic Rep. Mary Peltola, who served in the House from 2022 to 2025.

During his latest term, Sullivan voted yes on the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, which provided a new temporary senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per eligible individual. Sullivan also voted for the Social Security Fairness Act, which ended reductions to certain government pensioners' Social Security checks. While both laws helped beneficiaries, they increased the program's costs.

Most older Alaska voters — 90% — say they are more likely to support a candidate who will ensure they receive the Social Security benefits they've earned, according to a September AARP survey.

During the 2014 Alaska Senate campaign, Sullivan said he would consider options to cut Social Security costs, such as raising the retirement age or means-testing benefits — a point his opponent raised during the 2026 race.

Sullivan's campaign did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment. During an Oct. 15 debate, he said benefits for retirees and near-retirees should be protected and that program reforms must be bipartisan.

Peltola's platform calls for eliminating taxes on Social Security benefits, according to her campaign website. Peltola also co-sponsored the Social Security Fairness Act.

"Mary will stand up to anyone to fight for Alaska seniors and protect the benefits Alaskans have earned," a spokesperson for Peltola's campaign said in an email statement to CNBC.

MS NOW rates the Alaska Senate race a toss-up.

In the Hawkeye State, sitting Republican Sen. Joni Ernst is retiring, and the open seat's winner will be decided in a contest between Democratic state Rep. Josh Turek and U.S. Rep. Ashley Hinson, a Republican.

"One of the best ways to keep Social Security solvent is we have to remove the cap," Turek said at a Sept. 9 Des Moines town hall, referencing the maximum annual earnings subject to Social Security payroll taxes.

Raising or eliminating the cap would mean high earners pay more into the program. In 2027, that threshold, adjusted annually, will rise to $192,300 from $184,500 in 2026. Proposals to raise the cap would generally leave that first threshold in place, and then reapply Social Security payroll taxes at a proposed earnings level, such as $250,000 or $400,000.

In a recent AARP interview, Hinson said, "Anyone who's going to propose cuts to Social Security is going to get a 'hell, no' from me." In 2024, Hinson voted on the House Appropriations Committee to cut Social Security Administration funding by up to $452 million.

In 2020, Hinson noted she was "open" to raising the Social Security retirement age but has since said she opposes that change. In the House, she voted for OBBBA and the Social Security Fairness Act.

When the candidates were asked during an Oct. 13 debate about Social Security fixes, both said they would support a bipartisan commission.

A Social Security field office in Hinson's district in Decorah, Iowa, recently closed for six months. Some reports indicated low staffing, and political opponents and advocacy groups blamed Hinson's support for staffing cuts backed by DOGE, President Donald Trump's so-called Department of Government Efficiency. In a June 10 statement, Hinson called the six-month closure "unacceptable" and said she advocated for the office to reopen.

Turek "will protect Social Security by lifting the cap to make billionaires pay their fair share," a campaign spokesperson said in an email statement to CNBC.com.

Hinson's office did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

MS NOW rates the Iowa Senate race as "leans Republican."

Maine's incumbent Sen. Susan Collins, a Republican, will face Democrat and former Maine Senate President Troy Jackson on the ballot.

During an Oct. 8 debate, Collins said the payroll tax cap should be "adjusted," signaling support for a largely Democratic proposal that has recently gained bipartisan backing. She also said Congress should create a commission of independent financial experts to advise on how to address the program's funding shortfall.

A September AARP survey found 81% of Maine voters age 50-plus say Social Security is an important issue for deciding their vote. Moreover, 93% said they are more likely to support a candidate who will make sure workers receive the Social Security benefits they've earned.

At the debate, Jackson said the threshold should be raised.

In a recent Bangor Daily News op-ed, Jackson said he wants to increase retirees' Social Security benefits. He also said he opposes privatizing the program and other benefit cuts.

In the op-ed, Jackson also said he will "never support" raising the retirement age. Collins said in a 2023 News Center Maine interview, in response to potential reforms being discussed at that time, that she does not support raising the age to 70.

Collins co-authored the Social Security Fairness Act. She voted against OBBBA in its final vote, citing its Medicaid cuts. However, she did vote "yes" on an earlier motion to further the bill.

Collins' campaign did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

"Troy will work to scrap the cap and require the wealthy to pay Social Security taxes on their whole income and expand benefits — not raise the retirement age," a Jackson campaign spokesperson said in an emailed statement.

MS NOW rates the Maine Senate race a toss-up.

Michigan voters face a choice between Democrat Abdul El-Sayed and Republican Mike Rogers for Senate. El-Sayed most recently served as Wayne County public health director, and Rogers served as a U.S. representative for Michigan from 2001 to 2015.

El-Sayed said during an Oct. 8 television debate that the Social Security payroll tax cap should be eliminated. "It's only fair that the rich should pay their fair share," he said.

He also said during the debate that taxes on Social Security benefits should be eliminated.

Rogers said during the debate that he supports a "bipartisan solution" through a committee to address the program's funding issues. "Put them in a room, lock the doors and say, 'Don't come out until we have a real solution that can get bipartisan support,'" he said.

An August AARP poll found that 93% of Michigan voters ages 50 and older said they are more likely to support a candidate who will ensure workers receive the Social Security benefits they've earned.

Rogers has not proposed raising the retirement age or privatizing the program during this campaign. However, he has said in the past that he would support raising the retirement age, according to PolitiFact. As a congressman, Rogers previously supported proposals to allow some Social Security payroll taxes to go to private investment accounts, according to the fact-checking website.

A campaign spokesperson said via email that Rogers understands Social Security is "a lifeline for seniors who've spent their entire lives working hard and investing into this state ... Mike will ensure that we invest in them."

El-Sayed's campaign did not respond to CNBC.com's request for comment.

MS NOW rates the Michigan race as a "toss-up."

Nebraska voters may choose between Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts and independent candidate Dan Osborn, a labor union leader backed by state Democrats.

Ricketts has said Social Security benefits should be "completely tax-free."

As Nebraska governor, Ricketts signed a 2022 law to phase out state taxes on benefits. As senator, he introduced a similar federal measure, which remains with the finance committee. He also voted for OBBBA. While eliminating taxes on benefits helps recipients, it reduces revenue for the program.

"Nebraskans reaching retirement age deserve to receive the benefits they have earned," Ricketts said in an emailed statement. "I am committed to protecting our seniors' benefits and working to improve the Social Security program for all Americans."

To extend Social Security's solvency, Osborn has called for raising or eliminating the payroll tax cap, according to his campaign website. "The solution is very simple — remove the Social Security cap, so everyone pays their fair share," he said in an email statement to CNBC.

Osborn is against Social Security benefit cuts, including raising the retirement age, and privatization of the program, according to his campaign website.

MS NOW rates the Nebraska Senate race "likely Republican."

New Hampshire voters in November may choose between two veterans of Congress: Democrat Chris Pappas and Republican John Sununu. Incumbent Democratic Sen. Jeanne Shaheen is not running for reelection.

Pappas, who has served as a U.S. representative since 2019, will "never vote to cut benefits," according to his campaign website. At an August event, he said high earners should pay their "fair share" into the program.

"I also think that seniors deserve a raise," Pappas said at an Oct. 14 AARP candidate forum. Social Security's cost-of-living adjustments have not kept up with the "true cost of living," he said.

Pappas voted for the Social Security Fairness Act. However, he voted against OBBBA, citing its cuts to social safety net programs, including Medicaid.

Sununu has served terms in the Senate and the House of Representatives. During his tenure, Sununu supported bills that would have diverted a portion of payroll taxes to individual investment accounts instead of the Social Security trust funds under a broader push by then-President George W. Bush.

Pappas has said during the campaign that Sununu led efforts to privatize the program — a statement that fact-checking organization PolitiFact said is "mostly true," noting that Sununu's proposals would have been voluntary, limited by age or both. Had the proposal passed, the program would be solvent today, Sununu said in an August WMUR interview.

"I never voted to change or reduce or cut benefits," Sununu said at AARP's candidate forum.

Sununu's campaign did not respond to CNBC's request for comment. Pappas' campaign referred CNBC to his comments on Social Security during the recent AARP forum.

MS NOW rates New Hampshire's Senate contest as "leans Democrat."

North Carolina's Senate candidates — former Gov. Roy Cooper, a Democrat, and former Republican National Committee Chair Michael Whatley — have raised the issue of Social Security during their campaigns.

Cooper told AARP in September that some members of Congress are "undermining" Social Security by giving the wealthy tax breaks. He told AARP that the program needs to be protected and strengthened: "In the U.S. Senate, I'll work with anyone I can to do just that."

A September AARP poll found 83% of North Carolina voters ages 50 and over say Social Security is an important issue for deciding their vote. Meanwhile, 93% say they are more likely to support a candidate who ensures workers get the benefits they've earned.

Whatley told AARP that his top priority will "always be to preserve and protect" Social Security and that he will not support any proposals that "take away anything from what is owed" to people who have paid into the program.

Whatley also said Congress must work in a "bipartisan way" to improve the program's solvency.

Whatley's campaign did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

In an emailed statement, a Cooper campaign spokesperson said: "Roy Cooper will work to protect and strengthen Social Security benefits for current and future generations."

MS NOW rates the North Carolina Senate race as "leans Democrat."

Former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown of Ohio is pursuing a comeback bid, running against Republican Sen. Jon Husted. The special election will decide who will serve the remaining two years of the term formerly held by Vice President JD Vance.

As senator, Brown advocated for expanding and protecting Social Security benefits. He co-authored the Social Security Fairness Act.

A June AARP poll found that 83% of Ohio voters ages 50 and older said candidates' positions on Social Security will affect how they vote.

More recently, in an October AARP voter guide video, Brown said he opposes privatizing the program.

"We need to make sure that billionaires are paying their fair share by raising the tax cap so we can extend the life of the program," Brown told AARP.

In AARP's video, Husted said Social Security will require "genuine bipartisan solutions."

Husted voted for OBBBA. He also introduced a constitutional amendment to balance the federal budget, a move critics say may prompt large cuts to Social Security.

Asked about the candidates' plans for Social Security, neither Brown's nor Husted's campaigns gave CNBC more detail.

MS NOW rates the Ohio Senate race as a toss-up.

Voters in the Lone Star State may choose Republican Senate candidate Ken Paxton, the state's attorney general, or Democratic candidate James Talarico, a state representative and former middle school teacher.

Talarico said that to shore up Social Security he wants to eliminate the Social Security tax cap for individuals earning over $400,000. "I'll ensure the wealthiest Americans pay their fair share into Social Security," Talarico said in an AARP interview published Sept. 1.

A September AARP poll found 91% of Texas voters 50 and older said they would support a candidate who advocates for workers getting the full Social Security benefits they've earned.

Paxton told AARP that Washington "has no business cutting" Social Security benefits. Paxton also said in the interview that one of his goals is "rooting out" what he said is "waste, fraud and abuse" in the program.

Paxton said in a leaked audio recording that "there's no doubt we have to cut some of the mandatory spending," the Texas Tribune reported on Oct. 8. Social Security is among the federal programs funded through mandatory spending. Paxton told the newspaper in a statement that he would protect Social Security.

Paxton's campaign did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

A spokesperson for Talarico said the candidate will "stand up" against attacks on Social Security and "fight to ensure all Americans can afford to retire."

MS NOW rates the Texas Senate race a toss-up.

#2924. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2921. Mike Brown knows why Knicks championship chemistry hasn’t carried over: ‘Climbing a mountain’

It can be deadly on a hike. It's costly for a basketball team.

Chemistry must be rebuilt. No steps can be skipped, regardless of how long a team has played together.

"You're starting all over," Knicks coach Mike Brown said after practice Sunday. "It's like climbing a mountain. We're climbing a mountain as a team and we conquer the mountain, then you gotta come back down, take some time off, then you gotta climb another mountain. It's the same thing. And when you climb that mountain the second time, you're starting from base camp again. And if you skip steps doing that, you're going to die. It's not that tragic for us. When you're sitting at 2-4, people equate it to that sometimes, but it's the same thing, taking each step the right way and embracing it and trying to get a little better."

Head coach Mike Brown of the New York Knicks speaks to the media at Knicks Training Facility on September 23, 2025 in Tarrytown, New York. Getty Images

The Knicks' continuity from their championship team was supposed to be a major strength for their chances of repeating. This group clicked about as well as any group has ever clicked during the playoffs last year. Their togetherness and selflessness made them new fans across the country. An ability to elevate each other on the court made them an incredibly well-functioning machine.

But with a 2-4 record through the first six games of the year, the Knicks have resembled a group that, as Brown said, is starting all over again — not one that is picking up where they left off last year. It's been most glaring on the offensive end, which has been disjointed. There have been too many turnovers, too much isolation and a lack of playing off one another.

Mikal Bridges shoots a jumper against the Philadelphia 76ers. NBAE via Getty Images

It has looked like a group that is relearning each other despite the core knowing each other better than any in the league.

That's the tricky part about chemistry — it needs to be reestablished and reinforced, like a campfire that constantly needs to be tended to.

"Yeah, that's why I think for us, we just gotta continue to grow," Mikal Bridges said on Sunday. "Last time we played was in June. I think [we were] probably the most polished as a team we were at the end of the year, as we should be. So, I know it's tough to see last time we played to the beginning of the year, and obviously we should be better than we are, me personally. But it takes time and just not skipping steps and just kinda going through the process and keep learning, get back in that rhythm."

Landry Shamet reacts during the second half against the Chicago Bulls at the United Center on October 28, 2026 in Chicago, Illinois. Getty Images

It was similar last year, when the Knicks started 2-3. The offense was a constant — and frustrating — work in progress all regular season. It's hard to remember with how the season ended, but Karl-Anthony Towns' involvement in the system — or lack thereof — was a recurring storyline.

There was ample criticism that he and Jalen Brunson were basically taking turns looking for their own shots rather than utilizing a two-man game and playing off each other. Until, all of a sudden, it all clicked and the Knicks played their best basketball when it mattered most.

Brown likes to experiment in the regular season. As they try new things this year, it means a return to that work-in-progress version of the Knicks.

With the belief that as that chemistry re-forms, those championship results will return, too.

"If we don't go through our growing pains and we can't get through our growing pains, for whatever reason — because we don't want to go through it being that hard again or we think we know or whatever it may be — if we can't get through our growing pains, we have no chance," Brown said. "It's like anything you do in life. When you complete something and you have to do it again, you're not just starting right here [at the top] when you do it again. You're starting here [at the bottom] and you're building up. And sometimes, your second or your third year doing it, you're getting here [at the top] a little quicker. But you still gotta go through all the necessary steps to get there."

#2920. AI is coming to construction sites. But does it make workers safer?

When Shelley Copsey was appointed by the Ontario Teachers' Pension Plan, one of the world's biggest infrastructure investors, her job was to find ways to use technology to solve problems across its portfolio.

She quickly learned that there were persistent problems facing front-line workers.

Four years later, Copsey is CEO of FYLD, a London-headquartered software company that uses artificial intelligence to support workers in construction, utilities and infrastructure.

It comes as AI plays an increasingly important role in some of the most physically demanding jobs, from maintaining roads and railways to working on construction sites.

The aim is to reduce both paperwork — and accidents.

According to Copsey, when fieldwork goes wrong, attention usually turns to budgets or cash flow.

"We always go to the back office and we're like: oh, if we could help them forecast the job better, maybe this wouldn't be a problem," she told CNBC. "I think that what nobody ever did was just sit and say: well, what goes on in those field workers' lives and how do we make the life of the front line easier?"

FYLD uses AI to predict which permits a project will need, helping workers avoid delays. Previously, workers often arrived at a site without all of the information they needed, Copsey said. The system can also help crews plan around the weather, for example, rescheduling heavy lifting from a day when strong winds are forecast.

Workers record short videos describing their day's tasks and safety concerns. AI analyzes the information to help workers and managers anticipate potential problems.

An additional benefit is a reduction in paperwork, according to Copsey. Field workers can spend around 20% to 25% of their time on site on administrative work, she said, whereas now, photos, video and job information can be used to generate draft reports, reducing a task that previously took around half an hour to two or three minutes.

Infrastructure services provider Amey announced in March that it would roll FYLD out to 2,500 field workers as it looks to improve safety and give supervisors real-time visibility of work on the ground. Copsey said FYLD case studies have reported reductions in incidents and injuries of as much as 48%.

AI is also being used to reduce the need for physical infrastructure inspections.

Nathan Marsh, London-based senior vice president for Europe, the Middle East and Africa at U.S. software company Bentley Systems, told CNBC that the company's road maintenance platform, Blyncsy, analyzes images captured by drivers to spot problems, including damaged guardrails, overgrown vegetation and debris.

"Applying AI to existing imagery provides complete asset coverage in a fraction of the time a conventional survey would take," Marsh said.

Steven Elsham, U.K.-based CEO of Dutch maintenance software company Ultimo, told CNBC its AI tools can flag safety incidents that might otherwise go unreported.

The system scans work orders and shift logs for signs of incidents, then drafts a report for a person to review. Elsham gave the example of a warehouse door with damage that suggested it had been struck by a forklift.

"People should still report things themselves," he said. "This is there for what gets missed."

Waleed Umer, an assistant professor at Northumbria University, England, told CNBC that many studies so far have focused on what models can detect, rather than whether they prevent harm.

Construction sites change constantly, making it difficult for cameras to capture every hazard, he said. He also warned that false alarms can lead workers to ignore warnings, while overreliance on AI can create a false sense of security.

Billy Hare, a professor of construction management at Glasgow Caledonian University, Scotland, told CNBC that research projects and pilot studies have primarily focused on productivity gains, often assuming that less exposure to hazards means lower risks.

Bentley Systems, for example, said one rail project it supported cut physical site visits by 50% and manual surveys by 70% using a digital twin, a virtual model of a physical asset.

It's reasonable to assume that less time spent in hazardous environments could reduce risks, Hare said, but establishing whether technology actually prevents accidents needs further investigation.

According to the U.K.'s Health and Safety Executive (HSE), the number of construction workers killed in work-related incidents in Great Britain fell to 25 in 2025/26, down from 35 the previous year and below the five-year average of 37.

"The U.K. has seen the lowest construction fatality figure ever recorded this year, but this may be due to economic slowdown," Hare said, which makes it harder to tell whether technology has made a measurable difference.

Human oversight may not be enough on its own,  Northumbria University's Umer said, especially if AI produces more information than supervisors can realistically review. In that case, recommendations could be approved without proper scrutiny.

"Treat AI as an extra pair of eyes, not a replacement for supervision," Umer said. "And be clear about what it can't see."

For Ultimo's Elsham, the bigger question is whether AI makes difficult jobs more manageable rather than squeezing more work out of the people doing them. That starts with how performance is measured, he said: companies need to decide what any time saved is for, and which parts of the job AI should support.

"Safety teams are already covering more hazards with the same headcount," he said. "If the only plan for the freed-up hours is more tickets per head, workers will see straight through it, and they'd be right."

Companies should instead measure what matters on the floor, he argued, including safety, first-time fixes and unplanned breakdowns, rather than just output.

"A well-run business benefits everyone in it, and the people doing the hardest jobs should feel it first," Elsham said.

#2918. South Korea targets 'world's highest performance' sovereign AI model in record new budget

South Korean President Lee Jae Myung unveiled the country's largest ever budget on Monday, pledging to "boldly invest in AI and future growth engines."

Speaking to the National Assembly on Monday, Lee said total spending for 2027 would rise 12.8% to 820.9 trillion won ($610.14 billion), including 21.3 trillion won for semiconductors, physical AI and AI data centers — nearly double the 2026 level.

A separate 4.7 trillion won will go toward developing a sovereign AI model with what Lee described as the "world's highest performance."

The government also plans to invest heavily into talent, including training 2,000 specialists to "lead semiconductor design and the regional semiconductor industry," while expanding AI education for students, job seekers and existing workers.

South Korea has set up university "contract departments," where students can enroll in company-linked degrees that offer pathways to jobs at firms like Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix before graduation.

Companies help design courses, provide financial support and recruit qualifying graduates.

The record spending plan comes as the International Monetary Fund forecasts South Korea's economy will grow 3.3% in 2026, its fastest pace since the post pandemic rebound in 2021.

Lee said the higher spending would result in "virtually no net increase in government debt," with newly issued bonds used to repay existing debt.

The budget also increases spending on defense and social security spending for the youth. Defense expenditure is set to rise 8.2% to 73.3 trillion won.

"We will rapidly transform into a smart, elite, and powerful military by establishing a hybrid manned and unmanned system based on AI, robots, and drones," Lee said.

South Korea's strong economic growth has been driven largely by semiconductor exports as global demand for computing power rises with the AI boom.

Exports surged 93.7% from a year earlier to a record $115.3 billion in October, according to data released Sunday by the Korea Customs Service.

Shipments totaled $930 billion in the first 10 months of 2026, surpassing the $900 billion mark for the first time.

The country posted a $42.5 billion trade surplus in October, with imports rising 36% year on year to $72.8 billion.

Lee said South Korea was within reach of $1 trillion in annual exports, citing strong performance in key industries including semiconductors.

#2911. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2910. Falcons – Buccaneers Week 8 recap: Defeated in a downpour

The rain made the field sloppy, the rain made the Falcons sloppy. In the end, they couldn't beat a completely one-dimensional Buccaneers team on the road that barely even tried to pass the ball and seemed to relish how embarrassing that became as the game wore on. By the time the dust settled, the Bucs became the first team in over 50 years to win without completing a pass, and the Falcons made unfortunate history in multiple ways before it was over.

If you've followed me for any length of time, you know that I believe one game is one game, and it's best to patiently build the picture of what a team and/or players are over the course of a season. I get caught up in big days and big moments like anybody else and that bleeds into Sunday and even Monday, but then I try to make myself take stock of the bigger picture. I'll be doing that again this week, but it rarely feels as tough as it does after this one.

Michael Penix Jr. threw four interceptions and legitimately could've thrown three more, and he badly missed other passes that might've made a difference in this one. The offensive line didn't do a great job of protecting Penix or opening lanes on a consistent basis. Tommy Rees and company called doomed screens, chose to pass on the rare occasions when the Falcons had short yardage situations and could've run, and just generally seemed ill-equipped to put together a working offense in a rainstorm. Jeff Ulbrich's defense basically had one job—defend the run—and allowed the Bucs to gash them for over 300 yards even so. Nick Folk missed his field goal try and clearly isn't going to be trusted in adverse conditions or beyond 50 yards the rest of the season, and Atlanta's return game was once again basically nonexistent as the team struggled with field position. It's incredibly difficult to find positives because there were so few in a game the Falcons lost 16-0 and barely seemed competitive in.

Again, it's one game, but the Falcons are now 1-2 against the division and that's likely to matter down the line. It's one game, but the Falcons may be asked to play again in the rain or snow or cold, and this will make you wonder if they can handle that. It's one game, but the Falcons have a tough assignment against the Bengals abroad next week. It's one game, but it's at least the third time this year the Falcons have found themselves down early, struggling, and unable to come up with any answers to avoid the game turning into a legitimately blowout. It's one game, but it's a game that feels like the absolute worse games of the late Mike Smith era, the mid-to-late Dan Quinn era, the Arthur Smith era, and the Raheem Morris era. It's one game, but it's just about as terrible a game as you can imagine the Falcons playing.

The Falcons, of course, can't linger on it. They won't be playing in driving rain in Spain a week from now, and may not actually have to deal with wet conditions the rest of the way, and they know that game was the outlier of all outliers in terms of their run defense having to put up with nonstop running and Penix playing about as poorly as he can possibly play. There's only so much you can take away from a game you may not see again for a decade, in terms of conditions and opponent game plans, and getting stuck trying to fix what ailed them on this specific Sunday is unlikely to be a great use of their time. They have to get ready for a pass-first script from Joe Burrow and the Bengals and the weakest run defense they've faced in weeks, which will drastically change how they approach the next game. It pays to have a short memory in the NFL, and the Falcons will under a head coach as even-keeled as Kevin Stefanski.

For those of us in the stadium and at home, though, this will linger and should linger. It's the latest sizeable dent in the notion that this Falcons team is (or even can be) different than the many hapless ones that preceded it; the Falcons in the past few eras were capable of truly astonishing wins against teams they weren't expected to contend against but fell to earth in dispiriting ways the following weeks. For these Falcons to truly be different, they have to have something more than a deeply inconsistent team that follows a dominant week or two with horrors that would make Stephen King blush.

If this isn't rock bottom for the Falcons by a mile, this season is over, no matter how grim the NFC South may be. Whether they continue to "same old Falcons" or find their footing, the Falcons can't dwell on their ugliest losses this year. Unfortunately, it's difficult for us not to do so.

Bijan Robinson's balance was such an asset on a very wet field. Over and over again, he went into the pile or survived an early onslaught to pick up extra yardage, something that would've been impossible for a lesser back with lesser ability. His ugly drop was the only significant stain on a quality (if somewhat quiet by his standards) day, especially because the Buccaneers run defense has long given him fits. If only Bijan had not been mothballed on critical late game short yardage situations, eh?

Jahan Dotson had the biggest play of the game on offense, underscoring how dangerous he can be. He took a risky Penix pass, went around a defender, and turned on the jets for a 76 yard gain that should have set up Atlanta's first scoring drive had the team not frittered it away. No other Falcon had more than 10 yards and Dotson had 95 on two receptions; it's safe to say he bounced back this week even if he'll always likely be inconsistent from week-to-week.

Jessie Bates is on fire right now, and he made the play of the game in this one. With Baker Mayfield escaping the pocket after a busted play and flipping it backwards to Bucky Irving, the Bucs running back had a clear lane to the end zone…right up until Bates arrived and chopped hard at the ball. That forced a fumble that landed out of bounds, and per NFL rules, that meant Atlanta got the ball. It was a massive, six point swing when the Falcons would've been down 10-0, and exactly the kind of play that makes Bates worth every penny.

Mike Hughes had a tremendous play on the first drive, with Baker Mayfield's end zone bomb to Ted Hurst looking caught…until Hughes chopped it out of his hand at the exact right time to prevent a score and force Tampa Bay to settle for three. We've been justifiably hard on Hughes this year, but credit where it's due.

Maason Smith's third down stop with the Bucs driving and threatening to move the game from "likely out of reach" to "entirely out of reach" was a huge play. I wished it had mattered more.

Jake Bailey had a solid day at the office, pinning the Bucs inside their own 20 twice and averaging nearly 50 yards a punt. Paired with stingy work from Falcons special teams—they only allowed two punt returns for a combined eight yards—the punting game did its job.

Michael Penix Jr. had done a good job of taking care of the football, but he had been fortunate not to throw a couple of picks over the past few weeks that bounced off defender's hands. That hot streak ended in this one in excruciating fashion, as Penix threw two first quarter interceptions right at Alex Anzalone, who he appeared not to see on both occasions. It was a gruesome performance that got worse, with Penix badly missing a wide open Kyle Pitts after shrugging off a sack attempt and then throwing his third interception on a half-hearted fourth down attempt in the second quarter. He then threw a fourth interception on another ill-advised throw to Charlie Woerner that was picked off by a leaping Zyon McCollum. The Falcons clearly planned to stay balanced despite the wet field conditions, and sticking with that (as well as the run game running into a tough Bucs defense) led to the worst game of Penix's career thus far. The knock on Penix is that in pressure situations and less than optimal conditions, he turns into a pumpkin, and those criticisms are justifiably going to get much louder after this abysmal effort. He'll almost assuredly bounce back, but it was worrying just how sloppy Penix's accuracy, vision, and general play were in this one.

The line is really struggling against quality run defenses right now, with Tre'Shon Diaz correctly calling out the interior of the offensive line in particular for its struggles. The quality of the opponent is obviously a factor—Tampa Bay has been giving Bijan in particular and this run game more generally fits for a while now—but this group is supposed to enable Atlanta to have a potent ground game. When they have really needed it over the last month, it hasn't happened, and that's more about the blocking than it is about the backs.

The run defense had stretches where it looked quite competent, but given that these Buccaneers were entirely one dimensional, it was upsetting how often they seemed ill-prepared and ill-equipped to stop Tampa Bay on the ground. This was especially evident in the third quarter when Bucky Irving and Kenneth Gainwell slipped through multiple tackle attempts to pick up 46 and 28 yards, respectively, on a pair of carries. By the time Irving pushed his way through contact to score a touchdown and make it 16-0, it felt like all the air had gone out of Jeff Ulbrich's defense, and they ultimately allowed over 300 yards. Even a solid day against the run might not have been enough based on how the offense played, but the fact that the Bucs found so many good lanes, that there were so many missed tackles, and that the Falcons appeared to still be defending the threat of a pass that never came, it was not a banner day for either this D or Jeff Ulbrich.

I don't think Tommy Rees had a good plan Sunday. The focus on the pass when it wasn't working was a huge drag on this team, and that came to a head in the fourth quarter with the Falcons within striking distance and time getting low when the Falcons had a second down, third down, and fourth down and 2 and elected to throw three straight times. Penix nearly threw an interception on second down and then missed on back-to-back throws on third and fourth down, with the Falcons inexplicably putting Bijan Robinson on ice in three straight situations where having a dynamic running back would seem to beg you to run it. Rees has been on balance solid-to-good, but the dwindling success of the run game, the stubbornness at times with putting the ball in Penix's hands when it's not working, and the very spotty success of the screen game has been frustrating for a while now.

Nick Folk had a tough job to kick a 53 yarder in the rain, and he promptly missed it wide left. The misses, the lack of reliability in adverse conditions, and the fact that they don't seem particularly eager to let him have long tries adds up to a shaky picture. He's been doing this so long that Folk can obviously go on a tear and dispel these concerns, but right now I'm not convinced he'll finish the 2026 season as Atlanta's kicker.

The team's late down conversion percentage is woeful, and it's a major issue for this team that has flown under the radar the past two weeks because they A) won impressively and B) lost in such a way that the big, dumb plays will overshadow it. But Atlanta's in the bottom third of the league in third down conversion percentage on the year and are very near the bottom of the entire league in fourth down conversions. Part of that is finding themselves in third and long situations far too often, which goes back to the mixed run game success and some of Penix's misses, but part of it is just not having the right play or good execution when you have to have it. The Falcons' 4th down conversion percentage will actually go up after they converted multiple opportunities today, but the misses were awfully grim.

I did not enjoy seeing the Falcons outmatched by Todd Bowles and Zac Robinson, in particular, but that's what happened. The Bucs leaned on their strengths to an absurd degree and responded to the weather by running until the Falcons could stop them, which turned out to be never. While the Falcons run game wasn't great, it was still better than a borderline useless passing attack until very late, and going away from it and not being able or willing to key on the run hurt them tremendously. Sorry, having a hard time with this one.

Jessie Bates for that touchdown-saving play because it was extremely awesome and prevented this from being even worse than it was, and on a day like today, that's enough.

This is yet another Falcons team that seems destined to be defined by their inconsistency, with the highs soaring enough for them to knock off the league's best teams and the lows bottoming them out to the point that they get shut out by a vulnerable squad that doesn't even complete a pass. What version will we see next?

Hopefully the good one, because the Falcons now have to go up against the Cincinnati Bengals in Spain. Get your coffee ready for a 9:30 a.m. start time.

#2907. Alaska Sen. Sullivan says he'll accept election results, slams Peltola campaign’s reported recruitment of 2nd Sullivan

Alaska Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan said Sunday he would accept the results of Tuesday's midterm elections, while slamming Democratic candidate Mary Peltola's campaign for reportedly recruiting Dan J. Sullivan to run as a spoiler.

"No, I'm going to accept the election results," Sullivan said on CNN's "State of the Union" when asked whether he would accept the results only if he wins. President Donald Trump's Department of Justice has sued Alaska, demanding the state not certify the election if the incumbent Republican loses.

The lawsuit cites theoretical voter confusion over the appearance of the second Sullivan on the ballot, which DOJ says could siphon votes away from the incumbent Republican Sullivan and benefit Peltola. Alaska's Senate race is rated as a "toss-up" by the nonpartisan Cook Political Report with Amy Walter, and Peltola holds a slight edge in the polling average compiled by FiftyPlusOne.

Peltola has repeatedly denied any involvement with the second Sullivan. However, The New York Times reported last week that her campaign was directly involved with recruiting Dan J. Sullivan, a retired schoolteacher, to run as a spoiler.

Ballot-stacking is typically not illegal, and both parties have used it in past elections. Still, Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy ordered a criminal investigation into the alleged scheme. Alaska's Supreme Court upheld Dan J. Sullivan's place on the ballot during an earlier legal challenge.

Sullivan on CNN said that the Times report shows Peltola's campaign, along with the Democratic Senate campaign arm, "recognizing they couldn't run on their record, so they had to cheat."

"I think a lot of Alaskans, even Democrats, are thinking she is not fit for office; she's continually lying to the Alaskan people, and I guarantee you she's lying right now," Sullivan said.

Peltola denied any knowledge of the alleged scheme to draft a second Sullivan in an interview with KTUU on Friday.

"I honestly have no idea," Peltola said. "This is offensive to me on a number of different levels; first of all, this is Alaska, we're not in the business of tricking each other up here."

"I've won statewide before, on my own merits, without any tricks involved; I know I can win again," she said.

The stakes of the Alaska race are high. Democrats need to flip four seats while holding all currently Democratic seats to win a Senate majority. Alaska has emerged as a top opportunity for one of those flips.

The midterms will determine which party controls Congress for the remainder of Trump's term in the White House.

#2903. Commanders outscore Eagles 27-0 in the final 3 quarters, keeping NFC East hopes alive - Daily Slop

Editor's note: Each day, Hogs Haven compiles a collection of articles, podcasts & tweets from around the web to keep you in touch with the Commanders, the NFC East, the NFL and sports in general, with a sprinkling of other stuff. Enjoy!

The Commanders (2-4-1) emphasized the importance of being competitive for all four quarters coming out of their bye week. They still have some work to do there; head coach Dan Quinn said he thought they played well in three. They were still plagued by pre-snap penalties — they committed four fouls in their first nine plays — and had a fumble and a missed field goal on their opening two drives.

Once the Commanders started to pick up steam, however, it was difficult for the Eagles to slow them down. They outgained the Eagles 318-201 for the rest of the night, recording 16 first downs and averaging nearly six yards per play.

"After that TD drive [from the Eagles], I thought, 'Okay, that's it. There we are,'" head coach Dan Quinn said after the game.

It still was not pretty; the Commanders had to send Drew Stevens out to kick four field goals — an issue Quinn said must be cleaned up — but their defense bought them time by putting consistent pressure on Eagles quarterback Tanner McKee and the rest of Philadelphia's offense. The Commanders generated pressure on 46.5% of dropbacks — a season high, according to NFL+. It led to five sacks from the unit, one of which resulted in a fumble and recovery deep in Eagles territory.

The Commanders needed this win if just to settle a nervous building, knowing they couldn't afford to lose to a wounded Eagles team at home. But if the Commanders beat the Rams at home next week, then they can talk about turnarounds. Washington committed nine penalties, one of which wiped out an interception deep in Philadelphia territory and continued a season-long theme. Quarterback Jayden Daniels wasn't that sharp, but he made big plays and averaged 13.5 yards per completion against a defense that was healthier than it has been. Daniels got his first win since Oct. 5, 2025, and Washington's defense hounded Tanner McKee all night and held Philly to 3.4 yards per play until late completions boosted that average to 3.9. Maybe this win doesn't represent a turnaround, but the Commanders did what was necessary.

Trend to watch: With Terry McLaurin done for the season, Washington needs other receivers to help more. Sunday night, Treylon Burks, who is taking over for McLaurin at the X spot, caught three passes for 81 yards, including a 53-yarder. Rookie slot receiver Antonio Williams caught a career-best five passes for 54 yards, including a 30-yard grab. Both receivers set up field goals with their long catches. — John Keim

The penalties didn't disappear after the bye, but Washington's pass rush took over, and Rachaad White finished the job on the ground.

With McLaurin out and three defensive tackles unavailable, Washington needed contributions from the players taking on more work. Antonio Williams got involved early, Charles Omenihu made his return count, and Stevens answered his opening miss with four straight makes. Those contributions helped the Commanders overcome another night of costly mistakes and keep their season alive.

Washington's defensive front finished this game. Dorance Armstrong was still making plays against the run, and Odafe Oweh beat Jordan Mailata off the edge for a late sack. A season-high five sacks is the production Washington has needed from this group; Washington did it without Daron Payne, Javon Kinlaw, and Jer'Zhan Newton, while holding Saquon Barkley to 30 yards on 13 carries.

K'Lavon Chaisson's late sack helped put Philadelphia in a desperate situation, and Rasul Douglas took advantage when McKee floated a ball up for grabs. Chaisson's penalties were costly, but he also helped Washington close this game. After having an earlier interception erased by a flag, the defense made this takeaway count.

Follow the action and share every cheer, question and instant reaction with fellow fans on the SB Nation app.

Download the SB Nation app on iOS or Android.

For the Eagles (2-6), it was their sixth loss in a row. They were without starting quarterback Jalen Hurts, No. 1 wide receiver DeVonta Smith and a bevy of other core players because of various injuries. While Philadelphia had a better record before kickoff — and Washington has a lengthy injury report of its own — the Commanders were the superior team on paper.

Daniels started to get groove back in the second quarter. He completed 7 of 10 passes for 70 yards and one touchdown in the period.

Despite being shorthanded, the defensive line thrived. It generated consistent pressure on Philadelphia quarterback Tanner McKee and sacked him five times. While it must be noted that Barkley was playing through injury, Washington still deserves credit for limiting the Eagles to 3.1 yards per carry.

With the D-line rolling, the secondary locked down Philadelphia's passing attack. McKee only passed for 212 yards and threw an interception.

It was easily the best performance of the season for defensive coordinator Daronte Jones's group. The defense held the Eagles scoreless for eight consecutive possessions, forced two turnovers and was instrumental in the Commanders' comeback.

The Commanders didn't seek outside help after losing Terry McLaurin for the rest of the season because of a patellar tendon injury. They relied on their in-house talent and, for the most part, their prudence paid off. Early mistakes by Washington's pass catchers — a dropped pass, a fumble — turned into a standout performance from the Commanders' skill players.

Tight end Chig Okonkwo, who earlier fumbled after a short catch deep in Philadelphia's territory, had perhaps the catch of the year on a third-and-6 in the third quarter. Receiver Treylon Burks led the way with 81 receiving yards on three catches, veteran Stefon Diggs caught the team's lone passing touchdown and rookie wideout Antonio Williams had five catches for 54 yards. All had grabs that could've been incompletions

Quinn got the complementary football he constantly talks about — good plays by the defense feeding into scoring drives by the offense. Luke McCaffrey had his best night of the season returning kicks. Drew Stevens nailed a huge 56-yard field goal late in the first quarter to get the Commanders on the board. If he'd missed, the Eagles would have taken over near midfield, ready to add to their lead.

And Daniels came back from a big hit by Eagles linebacker Nolan Smith late in the first half that knocked the wind out of him. In the second half, he hit tight end Chig Okonkwo for 21 yards on a great catch by Okonkwo, found Treylon Burks on a double-move for a 53-yard strike that set up another Stevens field goal, and got Antonio Williams on a 30-yard vertical route in the fourth, leading to another Stevens 3-pointer. Rachaad White sealed the game on Washington's final drive, creasing a zone run for 33 yards to the Eagles' 4-yard-line, then closing the show with a 1-yard touchdown run with 1:52 left.

The win, Washington's first since Week 3, improved its still-underwater record to 2-4-1, allowed the Commanders to crawl out of the NFC East basement, and moved them within two games of the first-place and idle New York Giants.

"It was a must-win," tight end Chig Okonkwo said. "Every game is a must-win for us; that's how we approach it. And we knew this week is even more important because of the state of the division."

Daniels completed 16 of 29 passes for 217 yards, including an eight-yard touchdown pass to Stefon Diggs in the second quarter and rushed for 23 yards. He looked injured after absorbing a big hit near the end of the first half, but postgame said he had the wind knocked out of him.

Not in the box score: Daniels held a sideline meeting with his offensive teammates after a sloppy first quarter: Four possessions, four penalties — all pre-snap — a turnover and a punt while splitting two long field-goal attempts. The Commanders season in miniature.

Daniels — so peeved by the Commanders' three false starts in the first 14 times they lined up to run a play — called the offense together on the sideline to, um, address the matter. Daniels is famously level-headed. Here, he was plainly furious. This is a leader leading his charges. The message?

"No more BS," tight end Chig Okonkwo said.

"Basically, like, f—ing lock in, bro," left tackle Brandon Coleman said. "Like, we know who we are. We know what we can be. Just do your stuff the way it's supposed to be done."

"If we're going to lose," rookie wideout Antonio Williams said, "have somebody actually beat us."

The Philadelphia Eagles are 2-6 after losing to the Washington Commanders in Landover, Maryland on Sunday night. Final score: 27 to 10.

Well, the Eagles got out to a 1o to 0 lead in this one. That was nice. But then they played like the bad and dumb team that they are as the Commanders took control of the game to score 27 unanswered points.

The Eagles have now lost SIX games in a row. It's their longest losing streak since 2012, which was Andy Reid's final season in Philly.

The Eagles are now 5-11 in their last 16 games.

This team stinks. They're a real tough watch.

Are they even going to win another game this season?

Next up for the Eagles: a Week 9 home game against the NFC East-leading New York Giants, who enjoyed a bye in Week 8.

This loss is on the head coach. Dallas led 24-10 in the fourth quarter and somehow lost the game. The most frustrating part was how passive the Cowboys became once they had something to protect.

Dallas put together a 15-play drive that consumed nearly seven minutes, but once it reached Arizona territory the aggression disappeared. The Cowboys eventually settled for a field goal to make it 27-17.

Dallas recovered the onside kick later in the game at the Arizona 20 with just over two minutes remaining and had another chance to finish the game. What followed was painfully conservative.

Williams ran for no gain. Prescott was sacked. On third-and-16, Dallas handed the football to Williams for three yards and happily settled for another Brandon Aubrey field goal. That made it 30-24, but it left Arizona 1:09 to drive for a touchdown.

Arizona then went 80 yards, scored with ten seconds remaining and would have actually won the game in regulation had Chad Ryland not missed the extra point. Dallas then got another chance in overtime and still couldn't finish it. Arizona eventually kicked the winning field goal.

Schottenheimer spent the closing stages coaching trying not to lose rather than coaching to win. There is a difference between intelligently managing a lead and becoming afraid to attack, this was the latter.

Dallas had multiple opportunities to finish an inferior opponent and repeatedly chose the safest possible option. It was scared football, and eventually Arizona made them pay

They allowed RB Tyler Allgeier to rush for 147 yards and score two touchdowns, also allowing QB Garner Minshew to throw for 188 yards and three TDs in the third and fourth quarter. Whatever was working well for them before halftime completely changed in the second half. They allowed a big play after big play in both the running and passing game and ended up costing them the "W".

The Cowboys had a perfect opportunity to snap a four-game losing streak against the Cardinals and beat them for the first time at AT&T Stadium. Instead, they let a chance to get back-to-back wins go by the wayside, and while they will remain in contention in the NFC East, what have they done to prove they can put together any kind of winning streak? The offense was ineffective on the ground, and quarterback Dak Prescott could not consistently move the ball through the air. This was all with the Cardinals losing three key contributors to injury and benching their starting quarterback.

What to make of the QB performance: For the second time this season, Prescott threw for fewer than 200 yards (195). His touchdown pass required a one-handed catch from WR Ryan Flournoy. He was able to use his legs, picking up his first rushing touchdown of the season, but for a team with so many playmakers, the Cowboys have been kept in check in three of their past four games. — Todd Archer

In the first half against the Chargers, the Rams could not build on their strong offensive showing in Week 7, despite facing a winless team. Los Angeles had 95 scrimmage yards in the first half — its fewest in a half since Week 17 of the 2024 season, according to ESPN Research — and 66 of those came on the team's final drive. The defense has kept the Rams in games for much of the season, including on Sunday with two interceptions. The group should get a boost once edge rusher Myles Garrett returns from injury.

Trend to watch: In an effort to fix their kickoff woes, the Rams had two kickers active on game day. Brandon McManus made a 30-yard field goal and was 3-for-3 on extra points. Joshua Karty, who handled kickoffs, had two of his attempts fall shy of the landing zone. — Sarah Barshop

Next game: at Commanders (Sunday, 1 p.m. ET)

Ravens quarterback Lamar Jackson will undergo surgery after suffering a right thumb injury on his first offensive snap of Sunday's 36-10 loss to the Buffalo Bills, Baltimore head coach Jesse Minter announced after the game.

Minter said that Jackson will be placed on injured reserve, which will sideline the two-time MVP at least four games. Minter said "we'll see" when asked if Jackson's injury could be season-ending, per ESPN.com.

Jackson, who missed the Ravens' previous three games due to an ankle injury, suffered his latest injury when his throwing hand collided with a Bills defender on a pass attempt to Zay Flowers in the first quarter.

The Bucs did not have a completed pass in the game, which was a first in franchise history and it made them the first team since the Bills in 1974 to go an entire game without completing a pass. The game was played in wet and sloppy conditions that joined Bucs quarterback Baker Mayfield's injured right thumb as reasons why the Bucs only tried three passes all game.

"I'll do whatever it takes to win," Mayfield said, via Rob Maaddi of the Associated Press. "That's all that matters to me."

#2899. Fake nuclear warnings in Ukraine as Moscow, Kyiv report separate cyberattacks on emergency apps

Ukraine's air force said Sunday its Air Alert app had been compromised after it reportedly sent false warnings of nuclear and chemical threats in Kyiv and other areas.

The app briefly displayed alerts for nuclear and chemical incidents as well as warnings of street fighting, according to Reuters. The air force said unauthorized persons had interfered with the app's operation and that it had temporarily disabled push notifications.

"There is no radiation threat! Stay calm, follow our messages on our channel," the air force said on Telegram, according to a translation.

Two hours later, Russia's Ministry for Emergency Situations said its mobile app had been subject to "a massive cyberattack, allowing attackers to send fake information to the public."

It was not immediately clear if the incidents were linked. CNBC has reached out to Russian and Ukrainian authorities for comment.

The alleged cyberattacks come as Russia and Ukraine continue to trade strikes on each other's territories and infrastructure.

Overnight, a local ceasefire in the devastated Russian-occupied city of Oleshky and surrounding areas in the Kherson region, near the Crimean Peninsula, expired. The temporary pause in fighting allowed humanitarian aid to enter the city and the evacuation of hundreds of people who had been trapped largely without access to food, water, electricity and medicine.

It was the longest local ceasefire since the full-scale invasion began in early 2022, and both parties appeared to have honored the pause until it ended at midnight Moscow time.

On Monday morning, local media reported that Russian forces had struck a hospital in central Kherson, while Ukraine's General Staff of Armed Forces said Moscow had used the ceasefire to reinforce its military positions in the city.

"During the ceasefire, the rotation of Russian units, the fortification of positions, the expansion of anti-drone measures along roads and increased logistics activity, including the delivery of ammunition, were recorded," the military body said early Monday.

On Sunday, President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said October had been the deadliest month for Russia since the invasion began in 2022.

Over the past month, Russia lost 54,400 troops killed or badly wounded, "a record for any month of the full-scale war," Zelenskyy said, adding that Ukraine's Defense Forces liberated 96.2 square kilometers of Ukrainian land in October – 10.4 square kilometers more than Russia captured during that time.

#2893. Nets get revenge on short-handed Pacers for first win of season

INDIANAPOLIS — For much of Sunday night, the Nets couldn't buy a basket.

The Nets ground their way past the short-handed Pacers 98-90 before a sellout crowd of 17,274 at Gainbridge Fieldhouse. It wasn't perfect or even easy, but it was enough.

"It wasn't a pretty win, but it's a win regardless. We got that zero out of that column, so we're really happy about that," Michael Porter Jr. said. "If you're going to be a good team, eventually you've got to learn how to win games like this.

Brooklyn Nets forward Julius Randle (30) shoots over Indiana Pacers guard Aaron Nesmith (23) in Indianapolis, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026. AP Photo/AJ Mast

"Neither team shot the ball well. Neither team cracked 100 points. That's the type of games we want to win. It was good resilience. Sometimes, we let bad offense dictate our defense, and [Sunday,] we just kept going, grinding it out."

The Nets (1-5) entered Sunday grinding in first gear, their offense struggling and sputtering.

It still wasn't crisp. But against an Indiana team without Tyrese Haliburton, Ivica Zubac, Obi Toppin and T.J. McConnell, their defense, rebounding and improved attack got the job done.

A week earlier, they'd suffered a 136-109 loss to these Pacers, memorable for Haliburton tossing the ball off the court to himself for a dunk. The Nets bounced back in the rematch for their first victory.

"It feels good. I can sleep a little bit better," Julius Randle said. "But we've got a lot to improve on. I'm happy for the group. We've been working real hard. We're sticking together in tough moments. … But it's just a start — we know we can be a lot better. We need to continue to grow and build off what we did."

Brooklyn Nets forward Noah Clowney (21) dunks against the Indiana Pacers on Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, at Gainbridge Fieldhouse in Indianapolis. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

That beats coming unglued in tough moments, which they'd done all too often.

Porter had 23 points and nine rebounds. Randle added 20 points, six assists and five boards. More importantly, he finished a plus-17, and wasn't a ball stopper. The Nets cut better and made themselves available to Randle, who released the ball quicker to find his teammates.

"We just had a good talk [Saturday] as far as our offense, how we can just help each other out, things that we can get into," Randle said.

"We were on the same page. I got into positions where maybe it was isolation and guys knew what I was looking for, and get into screen-and-rolls. It's more diverse for me how I can attack. And it's all of us on the page. Coaches want us to run, get to the corners. We got more organized with our sets, what we were trying to do. We got quality shots. We didn't make them all, but if we keep playing like that, I promise you we'll have a good chance."

The Nets led 18-12 after a Porter free throw with 1:43 left in an ugly first quarter.

They were still clinging to a 43-40 edge at the break but came out of the locker room with a 9-0 run. They forced five straight misses and a turnover.

Porter started the run on a four-point play off a Randle assist. Nolan Traore (16 points, six assists) followed with his own 3, then added a midrange to make it 52-40 with 8:42 left in the third quarter.

Michael Porter Jr. of the Brooklyn Nets handles the ball against the Indiana Pacers on Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, at Gainbridge Fieldhouse in Indianapolis. NBAE via Getty Images

The Nets saw their lead shaved down to three on an Andrew Nembhard 3-pointer that made it 75-72 with 9:29 to play. They never let the Pacers get any closer.

Day'Ron Sharpe finished with 15 points, 13 boards, four assists and hit his only attempted 3.

The Nets shot just 41.1 percent and 13-for-39 from deep. But the ball didn't stick, the shot quality was better, and they didn't let misses seep into porous defense.

"The ball has energy, and everybody touched the ball, and then there was movement of the ball and movement of bodies," head coach Jordi Fernández said. "These games that you're buying into doing the right things and the shots don't fall, but you're playing defense and stay together, those are the most important ones to me."
