#2892. Democratic Rep. Stevens endorses Republican Rogers over rival El-Sayed in Michigan Senate election

Michigan Democratic Rep. Haley Stevens, in a stunning move, endorsed Republican Senate nominee Mike Rogers over Democratic nominee Abdul El-Sayed on Sunday.

Stevens lost the Democratic primary to El-Sayed earlier this year, but since then had backed his candidacy.

Her endorsement of Rogers came after days of rising tension with El-Sayed and his campaign over an inflammatory ad by a political action committee, Rebellion PAC, which depicted a couple debating whether they could afford groceries due to U.S. support for Israel.

El-Sayed's campaign has said it was not involved or affiliated with the ad.

The stunning endorsement was announced two days before Election Day, when voters will decide which party will control Congress for President Donald Trump's final two years in the White House. The outcome of the Michigan Senate race could determine which party holds a majority in the Senate, where Republicans currently hold a slim majority.

"Hey Michigan, as you may have seen, our Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate, Abdul, said I'm no longer welcome in his campaign, and I should stay away, and he's right," Stevens said in a video statement on X.

"And that's why I am endorsing Mike Rogers for the United States Senate today," she said.

"I don't belong in Abdul's campaign. I do not stand with Hasan Piker, who gets trafficked on our college campuses," said Stevens.

Piker is a controversial political commentator who has campaigned with El-Sayed.

El-Sayed, standing with Rep. Jim Clyburn, D-S.C., and Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, D-Md., outside a church on Sunday, dismissed Stevens endorsement of his opponent.

"I actually don't think about Haley Stevens," El-Sayed said. "There are families that are preparing to send their kids to schools tomorrow that look like they did 50 years ago. They're worried about what they're going to feed their kids for lunch. They're worried that if one of their kids gets sick, they're not going to be able to pay the bills. Those are the things that matter."

Rogers and El-Sayed are vying for a seat set to become vacant after Democratic Sen. Gary Peters decided not to seek re-election.

In recent weeks, a deepening rift has worsened between El-Sayed and Stevens. At the center of it is U.S. support for Israel, which was a major issue in the primary contest.

Stevens is a staunch supporter of Israel; El-Sayed has called the country's conduct in Gaza after the Oct. 7, 2023, terror attacks by the radical group Hamas a genocide.

Stevens' fiancé, Jason Turkish, endorsed Rogers before Stevens' flip-flop, citing what he said was rising antisemitism.

"I'm sorry people are upset," Stevens told reporters at a press conference in Detroit, standing with Rogers, whom she had attended church with Sunday morning.

Staffers in Stevens congressional office quit following her endorsement.

"As of this morning, many members of our team have resigned, including myself," Justin German, Stevens' chief of staff for eight years, said in a statement obtained by MS NOW.

"We are shocked and heartbroken by Congresswoman Stevens' decision to support Mike Rogers," German said, adding that he was speaking on behalf of the team "in wholeheartedly supporting Abdul El-Sayed for Senate," German said.

Stevens had the endorsement of much of the Democratic establishment during the bruising primary, making her endorsement of Rogers even more stunning.

She was the preferred candidate of Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer of New York, who on Sunday reiterated his support for El-Sayed and accused Republicans of Islamophobia.

"Senate Democrats are united in our support for Abdul El-Sayed, because we know he will fight for the economic interests of working families in Michigan, unlike Mike Rogers, who is a tool of Donald Trump and billionaire donors," Schumer said. "We Democrats [are] equally united in our total revulsion at the racist, Islamophobic, and over-the-top false attacks on Abdul by Republicans."

In a later post to X, Schumer called her endorsement of Rogers "a despicable betrayal from Rep. Stevens."

Peters and Michigan Democratic Sen. Elissa Slotkin both disavowed Stevens for her endorsement.

"[A]nyone who makes an election this important about themselves, and not Michiganders and our democracy, should think hard about whether they are serving the greater good," Slotkin said in a post to X.

Curtis Hertel, the chair of the Michigan Democratic Party, appeared to suggest that Stevens had been kicked out of the party.

"Congresswoman Stevens is not a current member of the Michigan Democratic Party, nor will she be in the future," he said in an X post.

In a Truth Social post, Trump praised Stevens' endorsement.

"Congresswoman Haley Stevens, a Highly Respected Democrat, who narrowly lost in the Primary to Abdul Mohamed El-Sayed, and who has been breathlessly wined and dined by the Dumocrats to help in their Michigan Campaign for the United States Senate against Mike Rogers, has just ENDORSED Mike Rogers for Senate!" the president said.

"Her following is huge, and the level of respect from the People of Michigan, especially our African American and Jewish populations, is virtually unparalleled," he said.

#2891. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2889. 5 things we learned from the Chiefs’ heartbreaking collapse in Denver

Last week's loss sucked. The Kansas City Chiefs were dominated and outplayed in every phase of the game.

Not just because the Chiefs should have beaten the Denver Broncos, but because of how they lost and what was at stake in the AFC West.

It felt like the defense played well enough to win. It also felt like the offense moved the ball well enough to win. It wasn't the 90% of the plays that cost the Chiefs; it was the minutiae. The little things that all add up to a team coming up one play short when they needed it the most.

Here are five things we learned from the Chiefs losing to Denver 28-24 in Week 8.

The Chiefs' pass rush isn't bad. It's non-existent.

\We're almost halfway through the season, and the Chiefs have recorded a total of eight sacks. It's tough to come to grips with the fact that Chris Jones is no longer the game wrecker he once was.

Peter Woods left early with a neck stinger, but he hasn't been a difference-maker in his short time in the NFL.

And that's about it for interior pass rushers who still have a shot at getting to the quarterback.

The edge rushers are faring a little bit better, getting the occasional pressure, but close only counts in horseshoes and hand grenades.

It's embarrassing to still refer to the Chiefs' pass rush as "Sack Nation." Especially when one of the few things they do well is knock down passes.

So I'm proposing we retire the moniker Sack Nation and come up with a more fitting one.

The choice to trot Harrison Butker out to attempt a 58-yard field goal in the rain when he has failed to convert anything over 50 yards, when you're up three with two and a half minutes left to play, is a wild decision to make.

I get trying to show faith in your guy who has shown up for you in the past. But part of being a head coach is having the huevos rancheros to know when a guy is cooked or in a slump, and make the tough/sometimes unpopular choice to adjust accordingly.

The same goes for defensive coordinator Steve Spagnuolo's refusal to adjust his scheme and game plan to attempt to generate more pass rush. Or the unforgivable crime of slipping into prevent defense when Kansas City was up by two scores late in the fourth quarter.

I won't get into special teams coordinator Dave Toub here, because those huevos went bad two seasons ago; now they're just stinking up the chicken coop.

We'll see if the Chiefs make the move to call up Justin Tucker this week.

Speaking of huevos. I hope the organization finds the internal fortitude to skip plan B (Tucker) and find a less slimy solution to their kicking woes than a sleazeball who is no better than Deshaun Watson.

Perhaps a guy like free agent Tanner Brown, who was the UFL's Special Teams Player of the Year in 2026, making three field goals from 60+ yards this past season.

There were a few bright spots in this game. But for my money, the brightest was rookie running back Emmett Johnson, who at times seemed to be single-handedly trying to will this team to victory.

Johnson finished the day with 128 yards receiving and a touchdown. He also had a clutch kick return late in the fourth quarter that set up the Chiefs with a short field and a chance to win.

Going back to making questionable decisions — you have to wonder, on their final drive, if Andy Reid and offensive coordinator Eric Bieniemy should have ridden the hot hand a little bit more and fed Johnson. Maybe it wouldn't have mattered. Maybe they would have gotten in the endzone; we'll never know.

Regardless, Johnson needs to be an explicit part of this team's game plan moving forward.

Offensive coordinator Eric Bieniemy was brought in to enforce discipline and improve the Chiefs' situational football. So, seeing a game-winning touchdown wiped off the board by a false start on what should have been the final play is disheartening.

These are the things Bieniemy was brought back to clean up.

I like Brett Veach as a general manager, but let's be real for a second. If he doesn't make at least one, maybe even two, significant moves between now and the trade deadline, he is letting this team down.

I'm not usually one for going all in for a championship run. But this is probably tight end Travis Kelce's last season. This might be the last time to get Chris Jones another ring if his decline continues.

The Chiefs need help at wide receiver. Rashee Rice is really good at doing one thing, but it's still just one thing.

Why waste this season by waiting for rookie Cyrus Allen to develop? Allen's ceiling is that he evolves into a player like Josh Downs. You have the chance to go get Josh Downs, so go get him.

The same goes for a pass rusher. Some really bad teams have really good pass rushers.

After finishing with six wins last season and losing back-to-back games, it's clear that this team needs a player or two to get them over the hump.

With where things stand, Veach's actions are the only ones that may put them over the top.

#2886. Connecticut Sen. Chris Murphy hospitalized after stroke, office says

Sen. Chris Murphy, a Connecticut Democrat, was hospitalized Saturday after suffering a stroke at his son's hockey game, his office announced on Sunday.

Murphy is "awake and alert," according to a statement from his chief of staff, Allison Herwitt.

"Yesterday afternoon, Senator Murphy started experiencing numbness in his arm while at his son's hockey game. He was taken to a local hospital, where it was discovered he had experienced a stroke. He was transferred to Johns Hopkins for further monitoring and is responding well to treatment," Herwitt said.

Johns Hopkins Hospital's main campus is in Baltimore, though it has other locations in and around Washington.

"The doctors are encouraged by his progress," Herwitt said. "We are optimistic he will make a full recovery."

Murphy, who came to the Senate in 2013, is not up for reelection on Tuesday, but he's been crisscrossing the country campaigning for Democratic candidates, with recent stops in Michigan, Minnesota and Florida. He was due in New Hampshire on Sunday to campaign with Democratic Senate candidate Rep. Chris Pappas.

He's seen as a potential 2028 presidential candidate and is a prolific Democratic fundraiser.

Murphy, 53, spent six years in the House before running for the Senate. He rose to national prominence as an advocate for stricter gun control laws in the wake of the Sandy Hook shooting, which occurred in his home state. He's been a vocal critic of President Donald Trump and has opposed the war in Iran and the White House's deportation policies.

He's one of multiple sitting senators to suffer a stroke in recent years.

Democratic Sens. Ben Ray Luján of New Mexico and Chris Van Hollen of Maryland each had a stroke in 2022. And Sen. John Fetterman, D-Penn., who was then running for office, suffered a stroke just days before his state's Senate Democratic primary.

"Thinking of my great friend & colleague, Chris Murphy. I know how fiercely he fights & I'm certain I'll be welcoming him back to the Senate soon," Sen. Richard Blumenthal, a fellow Connecticut Democrat, posted to X on Sunday.

#2883. Chicago Bears @ Seattle Seahawks: Week 8 Players to Watch

After captivating the league's attention (if they weren't already on notice), the Chicago Bears once again have an elite Primetime matchup with another Super Bowl contender – if not outright favorite – in the current defending champion Seattle Seahawks. Chicago will not only have to tighten up some mental mistakes that have led to 21 penalties over the last two weeks, but also deal with arguably the most talented roster in the league at the most difficult stadium in the league.

Ben Johnson and Co. have their work cut out for them in what many are saying may be a preview of the NFC Championship game. Let's get to which players from Seattle will have my attention on both sides of the ball.

Anytime you add an All-Pro talent mid-week, he is going to get quite a bit of attention. Fortunately for Chicago, they, too, added an All-Pro talent in the form of Jeffery Simmons – the former Tennessee Titan who played against Nelson's Colts twice a year. All in all, these two have played each other 13 times over the course of their careers but never surrounded by the talent these two have around them this time. Nelson being added to the Super Bowl champs is the definition of the rich getting richer. Considered by many as the best Guard in the league, Nelson is and has been a dominating force in the middle and figures to provide a jolt to the struggling Seahawks run game while assisting in keeping Sam Darnold clean in the pocket. Considering the offensive line is the one position group in the game of football that relies more on cohesiveness than any other, I am expecting a few hiccups in his first game action, especially within his first 7 days as a Seahawk, but Nelson still has the ability coupled with opportunity to make quite an impact Monday night.

If Dennis Allen and his shiny new toy can cause some confusion up front and get after Sam Darnold, Chicago's secondary will have a chance to create some critical turnovers and give Chicago a chance. If Nelson fortifies the Seahawks OL and helps keep Darnold clean whilst simultaneously opening holes in the run game – it will be a humbling experience for Chicago this week.

When it comes to the former 2nd-round pick out of South Carolina, his impact simply cannot be measured in the box score. And yet, out of all the bona fide ballers on the Seahawks' vaunted defense, #3 is the one player who keeps opposing offenses up at night more than anyone else. Think of Kyler Gordon but 3 inches taller, 20lb heavier, and runs a 4.3 forty instead of a 4.5. And like Spidey, Emmanwori is deployed all over the defense, presenting unique challenges for any offense. According to Sumer Sports' Shawn Syed (@SyedSchemes), Emmanwori has been deployed in the slot 36% of his snaps, at Linebacker 34%, and 27% at Edge.

How Chicago manages this demon will be crucial. And like I said, they have multiple difference makers on that side of the ball that are capable in wrecking games. From Devon Witherspoon (arguably my FAVORITE Corner in the league) to Leonard Williams and Byron Murphy Jr. (best DT duo in the league) to pass rushers Derrick Hall and Uchenna Nwosu, these Seahawks likely have more than 11 defenders who would start on most teams. That's not even taking into account dependable veteran difference makers like Safety Julian Love, LB Earnest Jones IV, and DT Jarran Reed. And yet – Emmanwori is more worrisome than them all. If Caleb Williams can avoid Emmanwori and the Bears offense can get a body on him in the run game – Chicago has enough talent on offense to put up points against anyone – Seattle included. But if Emmanwori can confuse the offense, make Bears TE Sam Roush look like the rookie he is, and nullify the impact of Colston Loveland, then Chicago is in trouble.

#2880. Apollo launches $11 billion private credit platform, backed by Abu Dhabi

Apollo Global Management has launched a European private credit platform with more than 10 billion euros ($11.2 billion) of lending capacity, backed by several blue-chip investors, including the Abu Dhabi Investment Council (ADIC).

The European Credit Company will invest in performing credit across the large and mid-market sectors, including corporate and asset-backed lending, as well as lending to real-asset owners in infrastructure and real estate.

The venture is led by Giacomo Petrobelli, serving as chief executive officer, and Christian Meissner, its chairman.

Petrobelli said Apollo's ECC aims to capitalize on a "generational opportunity" to finance Europe's long-term growth agenda using global pools of capital.

The venture's backing from ADIC comes a week after Apollo agreed to buy a nearly 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Mubadala Investment Co., Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund.

Other strategic partners in the new platform include European insurance group Athora, Japanese lender Norinchukin Bank, Standard Chartered and Aranda Principal Strategies, a U.S. credit investor, Apollo said in an announcement.

"We aim to build ECC into a premier pan-European credit platform, focused exclusively on the region, with deep local relationships, differentiated sourcing and flexible solutions that span all credit types," said Petrobelli.

"The Global Industrial Renaissance is creating tremendous financing needs around the world that are acutely felt in Europe, whether it's energy transition and transmission, defense, or digital and traditional infrastructure," added Apollo president Jim Zelter.

#2875. The Lions called a tricky CFL play to set up a touchdown

The Detroit Lions' offense has the unenviable task of trying to score points on the mighty Minnesota Vikings defense in their Week 8 matchup. Minnesota's defense might not be historic, but it is very, very good with Brian Flores calling the shots in a blitz-heavy scheme. Minnesota is the only undefeated team in the league entering the week at 6-0, but the offense is so uninspiring that there are currently 11 teams ahead of them in FanDuel's NFL championship odds.

The Lions' offense has been really good even after losing former OC Ben Johnson a couple seasons ago to the Chicago Bears. Detroit is still coming up with some really interesting passing concepts to complement Jamir Gibbs in the running game, and they used one of them to convert a long 3rd-and-8 in the third quarter against Minnesota.

Detroit pulled a play straight out of the CFL by lining up star receiver Amon-Ra St. Brown 15-yards behind the line of scrimmage, then letting him get a running start to catch a screen pass and convert the first down. The Lions would score a touchdown on the next play, and go on to beat the Vikings, 19-15, to give Minnesota its first loss of the year. Watch the critical third down conversion here:

You see this type of play often in the CFL, where it's legal for eligible receivers off the line to be in continuous motion in any direction at the snap of the ball. The NFL restricts motion to a single player moving horizontally or backward, but this play is legal because St. Brown doesn't actually move until the snap.

The Lions put Goff in a shotgun so he could build up speed and hit the flat as soon as he was ready to throw. As long St. Brown reports as eligible and the Lions have the required amount of players on the line of scrimmage, the play is legal. Detroit had a TE in the los on the left side of the formation to fulfill the line requirement

Jahmyr Gibbs busted a 30-yard touchdown run on the next play to give Detroit the lead. The Lions improved to 5-2 with the win, but the focus will be on the status of injured linebacker Jack Campbell who was carted off after lying motionless on the field.

This was a cool play for the Lions in a great win. Let's just hope Campbell is okay.

I'm screaming at Vance one second and then I'm hugging Vance and shaking Andy's hand. There's nothing like it.

#2871. Canada-India $5.5 billion bank takeover attracts opposition from workers, politicians

Canadian firm Fairfax Financial Holdings' proposed $5.5 billion takeover of IDBI Bank is facing pushback from employees of the Indian state-owned lender and political parties in the country.

On Sunday, the United Forum of IDBI Officers and Employees called for a two-day "nationwide" strike against the sale of the bank to Fairfax. The workers' union also expressed "grave concerns" about the transparency of the sale and valuation process, according to a post on X.

It is demanding that the government "cancel the privatization process" and instead focus on strengthening the bank while protecting public investment and employees' interests.

Last week, Rahul Gandhi, India's leader of the opposition, extended support to the IDBI Bank employee unions opposing the takeover by Fairfax. "Public banks are not assets to be sold off, they are tools to build the nation," Gandhi said in a statement on Thursday.

He added that the sale of over 60% stake in IDBI Bank to Fairfax goes against the commitment of previous governments to hold a 51% stake in the state-owned lender.

Nirmala Sitharaman, India's finance minister, in her response said the public sector bank was handed to the government in "poor shape" and was revived through "professionalism, prudence" and recapitalization of more than 250 billion rupees ($2.6 billion).

"The IDBI Bank is now poised for its next phase of growth," the finance minister said. The government has previously said that the next phase of growth will be driven by a strategic investor.

The Indian government on Oct.14 said the sale of over 60% of the share in IDBI will mark the largest foreign direct investment transaction in India's financial sector. Fairfax made a bid to pay 81 rupees per share for IDBI against the average fair value set at 75.99 rupees apiece.

On Monday, shares of IDBI opened at around 79 rupees per share.

However, Indian media outlet Businessworld on Thursday reported that government officials had suppressed the valuation of IDBI to make Fairfax's offer seem attractive. The Ministry of Finance has defended its procedure, adding that the bidding process was a "double blind" as the bidder did not know the valuation and the valuators did not see the bids.

The Indian government has been divesting stakes this year as it pushes to keep the country's growth engine running amid deepening fiscal pressures. It sold shares in several companies, including Cochin Shipyard, Indian Railways Finance Corp, NHPC and Coal India. In August, it got $3.3 billion for a 6.5% stake in the country's top life insurer, Life Insurance Corporation of India.

#2870. VOTE: CFBHN/Herald-Tribune Boys Athlete of the Week for Oct. 26-31

Each week, the Herald-Tribune staff will select nominees for the Herald-Tribune Boys Athlete of the Week poll sponsored by Central Florida Behavioral Network, Inc. Nominees are derived from scores and stats emailed in by Sarasota and Manatee County coaches during the week to htincoming@gmail.com. Readers can then vote in the online poll to determine the Boys Athlete of the Week.

#2869. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2867. Social Security may have to cut retirement benefits in six years. Here's how much money is at stake for beneficiaries

Social Security retirement benefits may face an automatic, across-the-board cut in just six years.

Without intervention before then, the Social Security retirement trust fund, formally known as the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, which helps pay benefits to eligible workers and qualifying family members, will run out in the fourth quarter of 2032, the program's trustees said in a June report.

At that time, the trustees project retirement benefits will be cut by 22%.

The Congressional Budget Office has a more dire forecast, anticipating the retirement trust fund may run out sooner, in mid-2032, and that retirees may see larger benefit cuts, at 26%, upon insolvency.

Beneficiaries may see an average $500 cut to their monthly benefits across the U.S. based on a 24% benefit cut, according to a June report from the Committee for a Responsible Federal Budget. In 29 states, retirees would see even bigger average reductions, according to the nonpartisan organization focused on educating the public on fiscal policy issues.

Senators elected this November will be in office in 2032, when the retirement trust fund depletion date hits. Their ability to right the ship is crucial for retirees and the economy. But it's not a sure thing given the political climate inside the beltway.

"The attention on 2032 is appropriate in the sense that that is when beneficiaries and future beneficiaries will see and feel the consequences of inaction," said Shai Akabas, vice president of economic policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, a public policy think tank focused on promoting bipartisan cooperation.

"Nothing gets Congress to move like that type of deadline," he said.

Of course, brinksmanship and political deadlock may stymie efforts. Congress could opt to kick the can down the road, rather than enact changes to shore up Social Security — which may require tax increases, benefit cuts or a combination of both.

If lawmakers decide to combine Social Security's retirement and disability trust funds, the depletion date may move to the third quarter of 2034, when benefits may be cut by 17%, according to Social Security's trustees.

Akabas said any delay by lawmakers would have consequences: Social Security's shortfall would continue to increase.

There could also be consequences for the economy, he said.

In 2023, Social Security benefits generated $2.6 trillion in total economic output and supported more than 12 million jobs, according to 2025 research by the National Institute on Retirement Security, a Washington, D.C., based non-profit, non-partisan organization.

A 19% benefit cut could reduce gross domestic product by hundreds of billions of dollars, according to the NIRS research.

Moreover, failing to muster Social Security reform would prompt bond market strain, according to research published in June by George Mason University's Mercatus Center.

Social Security is tied to the bond market because its trust funds are invested in special issue U.S. Treasury bonds.

If Social Security reform is delayed, that may put upward pressure on Treasury yields, with investors wanting higher returns for long-term bonds.

Delayed reform may either prompt higher interest rates and borrowing costs or inflationary adjustments that hurt the value of government liabilities, according to the Mercatus Center research. Those changes may affect household budgets through higher borrowing costs for mortgages, auto loans and other types of consumer credit, as well as student loans, according to the research.

If interest rates and prices rise, that will interfere with economic growth, Akabas said.

"Those effects are also ones that the American people feel, in addition to adjustments to their benefits," Akabas said.

Heading to the midterms, some Democratic congressional candidates have said they would raise or eliminate the Social Security payroll tax cap so high earners pay more into the program. In 2026, the 6.2% tax paid by both workers and their employers applies to earnings up to $184,500.

Yet it remains to be seen whether Washington leaders will have the political will to address the problem soon. Tax increases, in particular, may not be viewed as politically popular heading into the 2028 presidential election.

As such, payroll taxes are "extraordinarily unlikely" to rise in the next couple of years, said Jessica Riedl, a fellow at the Urban-Brookings Tax Policy Center, which provides independent analyses of tax issues. If those tax hikes happen, it would likely be closer to 2032, she said.

"In Washington, they love to wait until the last minute," Riedl said. "There's no interest on either side for these sorts of tax increases in the short term. In the long term, I think everything is on the table."

The National Committee to Preserve Social Security and Medicare, founded by President Franklin Delano Roosevelt's son, former Congressman James Roosevelt, has scored incumbent lawmakers on whether their votes have protected those programs.

The advocacy organization has also endorsed candidates that support raising payroll taxes and protecting Social Security benefits from cuts. With those endorsements, NCPSSM gives candidates a set of boxing gloves as a symbol of the fights they'll face in Congress, according to Dan Adcock, NCPSSM's director of government relations and policy.

The organization is also working to educate voters that the senators elected this year will be among those to deal with Social Security, he said.

"We've been trying to impress upon voters why this is an issue that should be top tier," Adcock said. "They need to be asking candidates about what their plans for the program would be."

#2866. Rubio says Trump wants to end Iran war 'peacefully' even as both sides prepare for strikes

U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said President Donald Trump would like to resolve the Iranian war "diplomatically," and prefers not to use military force.

Speaking over the weekend, Rubio told reporters that "He [Trump] hasn't made a decision...what the president expresses to you is incredible confidence that if we needed to do something militarily, we would have the ability to do it, and we really we have everything we need to be successful. But we would prefer to do it peacefully."

The remarks come as reports suggest both sides are gearing up for additional military strikes after the midterm elections.

On Saturday, Iranian Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

He also ordered that its military database be updated so that "all kinds of physical and non-physical assets of the U.S.-Zionist enemy are brought within the reach of the Armed Forces," referring to U.S. and Israeli forces.

Washington has not explicitly stated it plans to resume attacks on Iran, but reports from The Wall Street Journal and The New York Times indicate the U.S. is preparing for possible renewed military actions after the Nov. 3 midterm elections.

On Sunday, Trump told the Right Side Broadcasting Network. "We've had such a victory, and we'll put it away very soon, one way or the other."

"Either they'll make a deal, or we'll just end it," he added.

On Sunday, Iran's foreign ministry designated the U.K.'s Military Intelligence Services as a "hostile group" and accused the Royal Navy and Air Force of supporting "terrorist organizations."In a Telegram post, the ministry said that the move was a response to what it called London's "malicious" actions, including the designation of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps as a "terrorist organization."

Tehran has separately accused the U.K. of participating in the U.S.-Iran war by allowing American forces access to British military and logistical bases.

The U.K. had allowed U.S. forces to use RAF Fairford and Diego Garcia for what were described as "defensive" strikes on Iran, according to the BBC.

#2863. Oracle files to sell $20 billion in new stock, bonds to finance AI buildout

Oracle filed plans on Monday to issue new stock and more debt to finance its artificial intelligence buildout.

The hyperscaler will sell $10 billion in common stock and $10 billion in senior notes, according to filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission. While the fundraising was already outlined at an investor day last week, shares tumbled more than 4% Monday.

Oracle said it plans to use the proceeds for general corporate purposes, which may include capital expenditures, repayments of debt and future investments.

The hyperscaler's stock initially bounced after last Wednesday's investor day, when Oracle also issued a revenue forecast of at least $225 billion for fiscal 2030. Stock and bonds in Oracle, co-founded by former CEO Larry Ellison, have been under pressure amid growing investor concerns about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Oracle shares are down 33% in 2026 and 50% over the past 12 months. The yield on its investment-grade 4.95% bond due in 2031 is currently 7.157%.

The sentiment shift is also seen in Oracle's five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default. The spread sits at 296, up 270% in one year. That's more in line with a high-end junk bond rating, according to S&P Global Market Intelligence's Gavan Nolan.

While still an investment-grade credit, Oracle was downgraded by S&P to a BBB- in July, one rating level above high yield. It has $125.34 billion in debt, as of August 31, with the bulk in senior notes.

#2861. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2860. Defense, healthcare, crypto and AI: How the midterm election could affect stocks

Lockheed Martin gets about 70% of its sales from the U.S. government. Medicaid accounts for three-quarters of Molina Healthcare's premium revenue. Coinbase is waiting on Congress to finish writing the rules for cryptocurrency.

For some of the biggest U.S. companies, Washington is part of the business model. Now, the midterm election could change who holds the leverage.

A Democratic victory in either or both chambers of Congress would grant the party new leverage to investigate companies, challenge federal spending and demand legislative concessions. But their reach would have limits: President Donald Trump would retain his veto and broad authority over tariffs, immigration and federal regulation.

Historically, stocks have performed well under divided government. Under Republican presidents, the S&P 500 has gained an average of 23% in the year after midterms when Congress was split, compared with 16% when Democrats controlled both chambers, according to a Morgan Stanley analysis published Thursday. Analysts often attribute the pattern in part to congressional gridlock limiting sweeping policy changes.

Still, some analysts caution against assuming that pattern will repeat.

"We don't think that blueprint holds up this time around, and we still see plenty of reasons to be constructive on the U.S. equity market," said Ariana Salvatore, head of U.S. public policy research at Morgan Stanley.

For investors, the midterm election could reshape which businesses preserve their advantages in Washington, particularly in industries such as defense, healthcare, banking and artificial intelligence.

"There are some policy changes that might be market moving, at least for certain segments," said Jeff Buchbinder, chief equity strategist at LPL Financial.

Control of either the House or Senate would give Democrats committee gavels and subpoena power, allowing them to demand documents, compel testimony and haul executives before public hearings.

On Oct. 21, Sen. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., the top Democrat on the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, sought records tied to Trump's White House ballroom from donors including Cisco, Salesforce, ServiceNow and Kraken. Apple and Nvidia each committed $25 million to the project, according to records obtained by The Washington Post.

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., has separately sought records involving tariff exclusions, including communications involving Apple's former CEO, Tim Cook, as Democrats investigate whether Trump used trade policy to reward political allies and donors.

While donations do not prove wrongdoing or favorable treatment, congressional probes still carry risks: subpoenas consume executive bandwidth, reveal internal communications and generate unpredictable news coverage. Few CEOs relish the idea of sitting at a witness table before lawmakers and television cameras.

"It creates volatility because you get a negative headline," said Monica Guerra, Morgan Stanley's head of policy and geopolitical strategy, who expects Democrats to cast a wide net for their probes.

Democrats have signaled interest in Trump family businesses, government contractors, firms tied to Donald Trump Jr.'s 1789 Capital, and companies associated with Elon Musk and the so-called Department of Government Efficiency.

Then there is the most basic power Congress has: deciding where the money goes.

Trump's 2025 tax cuts and spending law, known as One Big Beautiful Bill, is projected to reduce federal Medicaid spending by $911 billion through 2034, according to a KFF analysis of Congressional Budget Office estimates. KFF is a nonpartisan nonprofit that provides health policy information. Meanwhile, new work requirements for Medicaid enrollees are scheduled to take effect Jan. 1, or earlier in some states, while tighter state budgets could mean fewer people are covered and greater pressure on provider payments.

Molina Healthcare generated $32.2 billion, or 75% of its 2025 premium revenue, from Medicaid, according to the company's annual Securities and Exchange Commission filing. In the same filing, Molina warned that losing government contracts or failing to renew them on favorable terms could materially reduce premium revenue.

A Democratic-controlled chamber could not repeal the 2025 law on its own but could use must-pass budget negotiations to seek funding changes or delays to some cuts.

"They're probably going to push back on those Medicaid cuts," Buchbinder told CNBC, noting that preserving coverage could help stabilize enrollment for Medicaid insurers and patient volumes for hospitals.

Hospitals face their own squeeze: fewer insured patients can mean more uncompensated care, while state budget pressure can reduce reimbursement rates, according to KFF. Congress created a $50 billion rural health fund over five years to cushion some of the blow, but KFF and medical providers have said the money would not fully offset the broader Medicaid reductions.

Democratic gains could still cut both ways for healthcare. The same lawmakers seeking to protect Medicaid funding could also intensify scrutiny of insurers, hospitals and drugmakers over prices, coverage and profits.

Defense contractors face a fight over which weapons and programs Congress keeps funding. The White House has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027, with a focus on replenishing weapons, bolstering readiness and covering other needs, some tied to the Iran war.

The White House has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027, while separately seeking an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, driven primarily by military operations related to Iran.

A Democratic Congress could challenge some Iran- and Israel-related spending while pushing for more aid to Ukraine. However, bipartisan consensus on China deterrence, nuclear modernization and missile defense could protect other major programs, according to multiple analysts.

For contractors, the difference could mean billions of dollars in orders.

Lockheed Martin, for example, recently reached a framework agreement with the Pentagon covering 850 F-35 fighter jets over a decade. But the agreement is not yet a finalized contract: Congress must authorize the multiyear purchase and provide the funding.

"You want to be a stock picker and not just ride the whole sector," Guerra told CNBC, pointing to areas such as shipbuilding, autonomous systems and missile defense as likely winners.

For crypto companies, the midterms could determine whether Trump's regulatory shift becomes durable law or remains vulnerable to the next administration. The industry's push for permanence stalled in September, when the Senate failed to advance the Trump-backed Clarity Act, a sweeping market structure bill.

Shares of Coinbase and stablecoin issuer Circle fell as much as 11% when the measure failed.

Getting a bill through the next Congress could come at a price. Democratic gains would give the party more leverage to demand stricter consumer safeguards, limits on yield-bearing stablecoins and tighter scrutiny of Trump's crypto businesses.

"In some ways, the industry doesn't really care who got elected," said Sam Stovall, chief investment strategist at CFRA Research. "They just want to know what the rules are going to be for the coming year."

Federal regulators estimate their proposed capital overhaul would cut requirements for the largest banks by roughly 4.8%, potentially freeing billions for lending and shareholder payouts. The Federal Reserve writes the rules, but Senate control shapes who fills future vacancies, and congressional committees can pressure regulators through hearings and investigations.

"It's at the regulatory level where stuff can happen that matters," Buchbinder said.

AI may be the messiest political trade of all.

Trump has made rapid data center construction an economic and national security priority. Morgan Stanley expects hyperscalers including Microsoft, Amazon, Alphabet and Meta to spend more than $1 trillion on capital projects in 2027, even as the buildout faces growing backlash over electricity bills, water use and infrastructure costs.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey from October found that 63% of registered voters oppose having a data center within 10 miles of their home, compared with just 13% who support one. The level of opposition is higher from Democrats than Republicans.

That friction has already reached Congress. In September, the House voted 417-3 to require state regulators to consider making large data centers cover the additional electricity infrastructure costs needed to serve them. The measure stalled in the Senate, 57-43 on a procedural vote, after Democrats argued it was too weak and pushed for mandatory protections instead of trusting the industry to police itself.

The midterms therefore could decide who gets the power. Democratic gains could put more grid-upgrade costs on tech developers, raising project costs just as access to power is constraining new construction.

But Congress is only part of the equation. State regulators, utilities and local governments will still decide where projects connect to the grid and which facilities get built.

"We think the election can have meaningful consequences, but on a highly localized basis," Salvatore said.

#2856. Crude oil dives to session low on Iran peace talk hopes ahead of midterms

Crude oil prices fell sharply Monday after U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said President Donald Trump prefers not to use military force against Iran and would like to resolve the conflict diplomatically.

Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, fell 6.4% to $106.20 a barrel as of 9:50 a.m. ET. U.S. West Texas Intermediate declined 6.5% to $97.35 per barrel.

Trump has previously said that he will not attack Iran before the U.S. midterms. But the oil market had priced in the risk of a major military escalation after the elections.

The president "hasn't made a decision," Rubio told reporters over the weekend.

"What the president expresses to you is incredible confidence that if we needed to do something militarily, we would have the ability to do it, and we really we have everything we need to be successful," the secretary of state said. "But we would prefer to do it peacefully."

Iran's Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said an agreement with the U.S. is possible if Washington "demonstrates goodwill" and is "truly seeking a peaceful solution," according to the state-owned Islamic Republic News Agency.

Araghchi said mediators are discussing the proposal Iran made during the United Nations General Assembly in September. Tehran expects the U.S. to lift sanctions, reduce its military presence in the region to previous levels and lift the blockade, the foreign minister said.

Iranian Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei on Saturday called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

Concerns over the Middle East and Ukraine conflicts will likely linger in the near term.

"Attacks on ships and refineries in the Middle East and on civilians in Ukraine continue, doing little to inspire confidence in the prospects for lasting ceasefires in the foreseeable future," said Tamas Varga, analyst at PVM Oil Associates.

"Nonetheless, hope springs eternal," Varga added.

—CNBC's Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#2855. Anthropic reports $17 billion in Q3 revenue, purchase commitments of $895.4 billion in revised S-1

Anthropic reported updated financials as part of an amended Form S-1 filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday, giving investors a more comprehensive look into the state of its business.

The artificial intelligence company said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025.

Anthropic unveiled its highly anticipated prospectus last week, bringing the company one step closer to a potentially historic IPO. The initial filing only included the company's financials through the first half of the year.

During its third quarter, Anthropic generated roughly $17 billion in revenue. The company reported a net loss of $23.29 billion for the period.

The five-year-old company is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it primarily sells its products and services to enterprise customers. Anthropic is a capital intensive business, largely because of the complex infrastructure and hardware required to train and run large models.

In the amended S-1, Anthropic said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Anthropic said its compute and infrastructure expenses increased by $14.4 billion for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up 329% compared to the same period last year.

"As our compute and infrastructure capacity grows, the associated compute and infrastructure expenses have increased," Anthropic said in the filing.

The company's CEO Dario Amodei co-founded Anthropic alongside a handful of other researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the SEC in June, but its executives have signaled that it's aiming for a 2027 debut.

Anthropic has worked to position itself as more safety-conscious than its competitors, and its S-1 includes more than 90 pages of risk factors. The company highlights risks including potential political headwinds, its complex infrastructure needs as well as the difficulties that can come from balancing safety and commercial objectives.

#2854. Marvell to buy Lumentum in all-stock deal, a massive bet on AI connectivity

Marvell Technology on Monday said it will buy optical components maker Lumentum to address growing connectivity demands from the artificial intelligence infrastructure buildout.

"AI infrastructure is undergoing a fundamental architectural shift toward optical connectivity, creating an opportunity that comes along once in a generation," said Marvell Chairman and CEO Matt Murphy in a release. "The companies that move first and help define this transition will set the standard for decades to come."

Since the tie-up is a fixed ratio deal with no cash, the value will depend on the price of Marvell shares. The company said the transaction has an implied enterprise value of $115 billion for Lumentum, in a release.

Through the acquisition, Marvell said it can combine electrical and optical technologies to meet accelerating and shifting AI connectivity demands across regions and systems. Companies are increasingly relying on optical connections to piece together chips used to run and train large language models.

Lumentum shareholders will receive 4.5 Marvell shares for each Lumentum share under the agreement. The deal is expected to close during the first half of 2028.

Marvell's takeover marks one of the most significant bets on AI in 2026 and comes despite recent concerns over the long-term sustainability of infrastructure spending.

This isn't the company's first bet on AI growth. This year, the company closed a multibillion-dollar deal for Celestial AI, a startup focused on optical interconnectivity technology.

Marvell has seen its market value more than triple over the last three years, buoyed by unrelenting AI enthusiasm. Lumentum's stock has more than doubled in 2026 alone.

Marvell shares slumped 3%, while Lumentum's stock declined about 4% before the bell.

#2846. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2843. The Spurs should be patient with Luke Kornet, for now

The Spurs have rallied after losing their first two games of the season, rattling off four wins in a row. They are starting to round into form while still having room for growth, looking like the contender their talent suggests they can be, through ups and downs.

Among the areas that still need improvement is the play of the second unit. And among the reserves, Luke Kornet has been getting a lot of scrutiny, as he hasn't bounced back to the form he showed last year and now has an exciting rookie behind him who has looked productive in limited minutes.

In the Spurs' latest win against the shorthanded Timberwolves, Kornet looked good, but his solid performance won't completely get him off the hook from those who might want to see Tarris Reed Jr. get a chance to fight for the backup center spot as soon as possible.

While the rookie should get his opportunity if Kornet continues to struggle, for now, being patient with the veteran seems like the right move.

It's always tricky to put too much stock in advanced numbers with so few minutes to go by, but they are not kind to Kornet. The Spurs score just 100.5 points per 100 possessions when he's on the floor, a ghastly number. His limitations on that end are well-known, and it doesn't feel like he has developed any other tools, such as a floater, to change that. His strengths, however, are also clear and are not insignificant.

Kornet is a good screener. In just 14.6 minutes a game, he's averaging 2.7 screen assists, the 18th most in the league, and those screen assists are resulting in six points per game, the 25th-best mark in the league. Now, screen assists tend to be higher for limited role-playing centers who don't get the ball back after initially freeing their ball-handler. There's a reason why Victor Wembanyama averages fewer despite playing over twice as many minutes as Luke: Wemby screens, then the guards find him, and Wemby scores. The Alien leads the league in plays finished as the pick-and-roll dive man, which makes sense. Luke can't do that. Context is important.

Still, the screening is good, as anyone who has watched Kornet play can attest to, and while he doesn't finish after a pass, he has another skill that he can use after setting them: grabbing offensive rebounds. His offensive rebound percentage has dipped slightly from last year, but it's still very good.

So if Kornet is doing his job on offense, why are the Spurs struggling to score when he's on the floor? First, he's currently posting an uncharacteristically high turnover percentage that is bound to come down significantly. He's been fumbling passes and losing offensive boards, and while he'll always do that to an extent, there's simply no way he continues to do it at the pace his done it so far. If he somehow does, he won't only be out of the rotation but out of the league soon. Second, the Spurs are shooting very few threes per possession when he's on the floor (in part explained by the fact that Kornet doesn't take them) while also connecting on just 33.2 percent of them. When Kornet is off the floor, that number climbs to 35.9 on more attempts.

There's a decent chance the Spurs simply start shooting better when Kornet is on the floor, and there's no way his turnover percentage remains as high, considering his usage. Mitch Johnson should try giving Reed Jr. some minutes whenever possible, but prematurely giving up on Kornet because of the offense's struggles would be unwise.

If the offense was the only issue, there would be little reason to demote Kornet. The problem is, the Spurs have also been horrendous on defense, both by the numbers and the eye test, when Wembanyama is not on the floor. A big decline in efficiency is obviously expected when the best defender of his generation rests, but so far, it's been precipitous. No one expects Kornet to match Wemby, but a 22-point swing in points allowed per 100 possessions is simply too big and not even close to the more reasonable seven-point swing from last season.

Even though the numbers should normalize with more minutes, Kornet just hasn't looked good out there. Still, he's rebounding at a decent level, but the Spurs are allowing a lot of second-chance points when he's on the floor anyway, and while his allowing a very low percentage at the rim when he's the closest defender, other teams' points in the paint have been high.

What those numbers suggest, then, is that maybe the issue is not necessarily Kornet but the teammates he shares the floor with. Mitch Johnson explicitly said that the struggles of units featuring Kornet have to be seen as collective instead of individual, because there are four other players out there with him, and he's right.

Kornet has shared the floor very often with Keldon Johnson, a poor defender, and those minutes have been horrendous. In lineups that feature more of the starters (and once again, the numbers need to be taken with a grain of salt), the defensive rating is much lower. Most other Spurs are also used to playing a very aggressive style of perimeter defense when Wembanyama is around, and Kornet simply can't cover as much space as Wemby does or erase or deter as many shots at the rim once the ball handler gets past the first line of defense. The new lineups developing familiarity should help.

As with the offense, it seems like Kornet, while partially responsible, is not individually to blame for the bad results. He has not been as good as he was last season so far, but a combination of issues involving the lineups he's in and some seemingly aberrant numbers that should, on paper, get back to normal with a bigger sample size are likely the biggest reasons for the poor performance of the bench units.

Whether Kornet can go back to being the version of himself from last season, which was a perfectly good backup in most matchups, is irrelevant if there's a better option waiting in the wings. If Reed Jr. is simply a superior player, he should be getting Kornet's minutes.

There are two issues with any claim that he is, however. The first is that he's untested, which can only be solved by playing him, but the second has to do with the limitations that Reed has displayed so far.

The rookie has shown some touch around the rim and, perhaps more importantly, more ambition in terms of finishing plays when he gets the ball in the paint than the more passive Kornet, but they both share strengths and weaknesses. Kornet is likely the better screener while Reed Jr. is almost certainly a better offensive rebounder, but neither spaces the floor either horizontally, as both have limited range, or vertically, as they are both big but not explosive. Passing has never been Reed Jr.'s biggest strength, so he won't likely offer another dimension to the offense in that area, at least as a rookie.

Defensively, both Reed Jr. and Kornet are only suited for drop coverage, since their foot speed is limited, and both are at their best close to the rim. While the rookie might offer more energy, that could be a plus when it becomes contagious, but it can also be a negative if it results in chaos. Kornet is likely a better steadying force in that regard due to his experience, while once again Reed Jr. has an edge in rebounding. Both have similarly good cases for the non-Wemby minutes.

If Kornet continues to struggle, Mitch Johnson should absolutely give the rookie some more playing time, but Reed Jr. shouldn't be considered an automatic upgrade who will solve every issue. Especially if the coaching staff's only change is swapping the two in the rotation instead of addressing the other problems with the non-Wembanyama units.

Kornet is far from untouchable, and he might lose his spot eventually. But there's no rush to make a change now, as the season is long and the Spurs are clearly still a work in progress, trying to figure out more important things than their backup center situation.

#2840. Dak Prescott: "Cowardice" to put blame for Sunday's loss on Brian Schottenheimer

The Cowboys blew a 14-point lead and lost at home for the first time in franchise history on Sunday, so quarterback Dak Prescott may have been understating things when he called it "one of the toughest" losses of his career.

Arizona scored 20 points in the final 11 minutes of regulation and then missed an extra point that would have given them a win. That reprieve did not spark the Cowboys as they went three-and-out on their only possession before the Cardinals' game-winning field goal in overtime.

The magnitude of the collapse during a season that has featured other underwhelming performances has led to questions about head coach Brian Schottenheimer's job security. Prescott was adamant after the loss that all can't be put on Schottenheimer's shoulders.

"It's all of us," Prescott said, via the team's website. "Don't put the blame on one person. Simple as that. I think that's weak. I think that's cowardice. This game is about all 11 that touch the field and every person that has a part in it coaches and support staff. It's never just one person's fault. Even if I go out there and throw a bunch of interceptions, it's never just truly one person's fault. And so, don't take the easy and cheap way out. This is about us and it's about us backing him up and backing one another up and we'll have to show it."

Cowboys owner Jerry Jones said he's not thinking about an in-season coaching change, but he also called the loss an "indictment of the whole show" and Schottenheimer's job puts him at the top of the pyramid. That could wind up working out well for him if the 3-5 team can navigate its way past the Giants to win the NFC East, but there's been little about their performance to this point in the season to suggest they're capable of that kind of consistent success.

#2836. Lions vs. Dolphins odds: Opening lines for Week 9 matchup

The Detroit Lions (5-2) will travel to Florida to take on the Miami Dolphins (1-6) in Week 9, in a battle between teams at two very different stages of their roster development. While the Lions are shaking off some early-season mistakes, they've strung together three wins, including back-to-back wins in their division, while the Dolphins are in the early stages of a rebuild under their latest regime.

The folks over at FanDuel Sportsbook have set the opening betting line at Lions -7 points.

The Dolphins made a general manager and head coaching change this offseason, firing GM Chris Grier and coach Mike McDaniel after four unsuccessful seasons and hiring Jon-Eric Sullivan as their new GM and former Green Bay Packers defensive coordinator Jeff Hafley as their new coach. Like most new regimes, the new staff got to work early, cutting veteran contracts, including quarterback Tua Tagovailoa, wide receiver Tyreek Hill, and edge rusher Bradley Chubb. They also traded away former first-round pick Jaylen Waddle for a trio of picks to use in the 2026 NFL Draft to help them with the rebuild.

Step one in establishing a new foundation was signing Malik Willis as their new signal caller—who most recently played in Green Bay with Hafley—and drafting six (!) rookie starters, including first-round picks guard Kadyn Proctor and corner Chris Johnson, as well as second-round pick linebacker Jacob Rodriguez, third-round receivers Chris Bell and Caleb Douglas, and fifth-round safety Michael Taaffe. They also have another eight rookies on their 53-man roster, meaning over 25% of the Dolphins roster (14 players) is made up of first-year players.

As you would expect with a young roster at the beginning of a rebuild, the Dolphins have struggled out of the gate. In Week 1, the Raiders beat them by two touchdowns; the 49ers won by three touchdowns the week after; and the Chiefs beat them by two touchdowns in Week 3. In Week 4, they showed signs of improvement, losing to the Vikings by only five points; the Bengals won by just seven in Week 5, and in Week 6, they got their first win of the season, beating the Jets by three points.

On Sunday, the Dolphins and Patriots squared off in an AFC East showdown, and while the Dolphins built a 13-3 lead at the half, the Patriots scored 21 unanswered points to go up 24-13 late in the fourth quarter. Willis connected on an explosive touchdown pass at the 2-minute warning to bring them close, but a failed 2-point conversion and a failed onside kick set the final score at 24-19.

The Lions and Dolphins are scheduled to play on Sunday, November 8 at 1 p.m. ET at Hard Rock Stadium in Miami Gardens, Florida.
